<a id="lesson-0.2-why-ownership-wins"></a>
## 0.2  Why Ownership Wins: Cash Flow + Tax Savings + Equity

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Module 0 - Ownership vs. Arbitrage |
| Code | 0.2 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.1 (before) and 0.3 (next); Sam's PA.4 underwriting and PA.5 cost segregation; SR.3 cost segregation estimator; Preston's PA.10 and OP.12 for tax and exit |

### 0.2.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can split an ownership return into four streams: cash flow, principal paydown, change in value, and tax timing. You can say which of the four you can actually spend this year. You can compare owning a house with leasing a similar one on the same assumptions. And you can run a simple test that tells you whether "ownership wins" is true for a specific deal, or only true on a slide.

### 0.2.1 Treat "wins" as a claim you have to test

The title of this lesson makes a claim: ownership wins. Sometimes it does. Sometimes it loses badly. The only honest way to use that title is as a question you answer for one property, one loan, and one household.

The case for owning usually lists three benefits: cash flow, tax savings, and equity. I'm going to split equity into two parts, because they behave differently. That gives four possible return streams:

1. Operating cash flow: what's left after operating costs and the loan payment.
2. Principal paydown: the part of each loan payment that reduces what you owe.
3. Change in value: what the house is worth later compared with what you paid.
4. Tax timing: deductions, mainly depreciation, that may lower your tax bill now. Part of that benefit may be taxed back when you sell, which is why I call it timing rather than savings.

Each stream has a cost or a risk attached. And only one of them lands in your bank account on a normal month. The mistake I want you to avoid is adding all four together and calling the total "income". It is not income. Three of the four are either locked inside the house or depend on facts that won't be known for years.

### 0.2.2 Stream one: operating cash flow

This is the only stream you can spend this year. It is the money left after the house pays for itself.

Our example all the way through is Cedar Ridge. It's a hypothetical three-bedroom lake house that Maya and Chris, a two-earner household, are buying for $385,000. They're financing it with a DSCR loan quote. DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio. In plain words, it's how many dollars of property income the lender counts for every dollar of loan payment. Lesson PA.6 (DSCR Loans 101) covers it. All figures are assumptions from the course example file.

- Gross booking revenue: 212 booked nights at $300, plus 61 cleaning fees of $175, for $74,275. That's an average nightly rate (ADR) of $300. With 212 of 365 nights booked, occupancy is about 58%.
- Operating costs: platform fees, cleaner, utilities, supplies, software, an on-call handyman, property tax, insurance, and two reserves. The total is $45,066.
- Net operating income (NOI): $74,275 minus $45,066, which is $29,209.
- Annual principal and interest on the DSCR quote (the debt service): $23,637.
- Cash flow: $29,209 minus $23,637, which is $5,572 a year, or about $464 a month.

That $5,572 is real money, if the base case happens. Notice what it already pays for: a 5% repairs reserve and a 5% capital reserve. That's money you set aside for repairs and big replacements, such as a roof. It stays in your account, but it isn't free to spend. If you drop those reserves to make the number look bigger, you are borrowing from the next roof.

Now the cost side. Against $142,688 of cash in, $5,572 is a 3.9% cash-on-cash return. Cash-on-cash means one year's cash flow divided by the cash you put in. The $142,688 is a $96,250 down payment, $14,438 of closing costs including points, and $32,000 of furnishing.

Then the weak case. The nightly rate is $265 instead of $300, and occupancy is 50% instead of 58%. The same house then loses $5,475 a year after debt service. This loss and the $5,572 base case are both pre-diligence figures, and PA.9 revisits them. So stream one is small in a good year and negative in a bad one.

### 0.2.3 Stream two: principal paydown

Each month, part of the loan payment reduces the loan balance. That part isn't money gone. It becomes equity: the share of the house you own outright.

On the Cedar Ridge DSCR quote, the balance falls by $2,795 in year one. That number grows a little each year because more of each payment goes to principal as the balance shrinks.

Here's the catch. You paid that $2,795 out of your cash flow already. It's inside the $23,637 of debt service. You can't spend it. You get it back only when you sell or refinance.

Refinancing can turn some of it into cash. In the course example, a refinance at the end of year two frees $15,725 after costs. The new loan is larger, so base-case cash flow falls from $5,572 to $4,752 a year.

The equity it builds also depends on the house holding its value. If the value falls, you can pay down the loan and still have less equity than you started with.

### 0.2.4 Stream three: change in value

This is the stream people talk about most and control least.

For teaching, the course example assumes Cedar Ridge's value grows 3% a year (assumption). In year one that would be 3% of $385,000, or $11,550. It is an assumption, not a forecast. Values can fall. Lake markets can see new supply, rule changes, or a slow year.

Three rules for this stream:

- Never add modeled appreciation to spendable monthly income. $11,550 of assumed value change is not about $960 a month you can use.
- You only collect it by selling or borrowing against it, and both cost money.
- Selling costs are large. The course example uses 7% of the sale price. At 3% a year, the house is worth an assumed $446,321 after five years. (That's $385,000 grown 3% a year for five years.) Selling costs of 7% on that are $31,242.

Here is a test worth running on any deal. What happens if the value is flat for five years? For Cedar Ridge, that means selling at $385,000. Subtract 7% selling costs of $26,950 and the year-five loan balance of $272,519. Net proceeds before tax are $85,531. You put in $142,688. That's a $57,157 gap. Only cash flow and tax timing can close it. Section 0.2.8 runs this.

### 0.2.5 Stream four: tax timing

Depreciation is a paper deduction for wear on the building. You don't write a check for it, but it can reduce taxable income. Whether it reduces your tax bill depends on several facts. Each one is a question for your CPA, not a number to assume.

**Basis and land.** You depreciate the building, not the land. IRS Publication 527 states that you can never depreciate land ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). So the first input is how much of the price belongs to the land. The course example assumes 20% (assumption). Basis is, roughly, what the property cost you for tax purposes. Some closing costs can be added to it, which is what "capitalized" means. Your CPA decides which ones. The example's basis is $391,000: the $385,000 price plus $6,000 of the $14,438 of closing costs (assumption). Take out 20% for land ($78,200), and $312,800 is left for the building.

**Placed in service.** Depreciation starts when the property is ready and available for rent, not when the first guest arrives. Publication 527 gives an example: a house ready for rent in July and first rented in September starts depreciating in July ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). For Cedar Ridge, a late-year launch gives a small first-year deduction on the building.

**Recovery period and classification.** The example file shows the building on two schedules. The 27.5-year schedule, labeled residential rental, gives $11,375 per full year. The 39-year schedule, labeled nonresidential or transient use, gives $8,021. Those labels come from the course example file. Short average stays can push a property onto the 39-year schedule. Your CPA decides which applies to your facts. The example uses a full year in year one to keep the math simple. A real first year is shorter and depends on the month the house is placed in service.

**Accelerated depreciation.** A cost segregation study can move some of the building into 5, 7, and 15-year property. In plain words, it's a report from a specialist that sorts parts of the building into shorter-life categories. Those parts can then be written off faster than the building itself. Furnishings are also short-life property. IRS guidance describes a permanent 100% first-year (bonus) depreciation deduction for eligible property acquired after January 19, 2025 ([IRS newsroom, Notice 2026-11 guidance](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill)). It applies to eligible property, not to land and not to the whole building. Sam's PA.5 (Cost Segregation & Year-One Depreciation, Explained) walks through the study itself.

In the course example, a 22% reclassification (assumption) puts $68,816 of the building into short-life property. The real share comes from the study. Add the $32,000 of furnishings, and the year-one bonus amount is $100,816, if eligible. Add a full year of 39-year depreciation on the remaining building ($6,256) and year-one deductions are about $107,072. At an assumed 32% marginal rate, that could lower tax by about $34,263. Your marginal rate is the rate on your top slice of income. The 32% is an assumption for the example.

Read the word "could" carefully. That result depends on the facts below: the passive rules, your participation hours, the $25,000 allowance, and personal use.

**Passive activity rules.** Losses from passive activities generally offset only passive activity income. IRS Publication 925 explains the rules ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Two parts of it matter most for short-term rentals:

- If the average stay is 7 days or less, Publication 925 doesn't count the activity as a "rental activity" under these rules. You figure the average by dividing total days in all rental periods by the number of rentals. Cedar Ridge's average stay is 3.5 nights: 212 nights over 61 stays.
- That alone doesn't make the loss usable. The loss is non-passive, and can offset other income such as W-2 wages (your paycheck), only if you also materially participate.

Publication 925 lists the material participation tests. Three of them matter most here. You participated more than 500 hours. Or your participation was substantially all the participation by anyone. Or you participated more than 100 hours and at least as much as any other individual, owner or not ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). That last point matters for a short-term rental. A cleaner who turns the house over 61 times a year may log more hours than you do. If you hire a full-service co-host, their hours may exceed yours by a wide margin. Keep a real log of your hours. Your CPA will ask.

If you don't meet a test, the loss stays passive and may be suspended. The course example file notes that a suspended loss isn't lost: it's carried forward to later years. Your CPA confirms how that works for you.

**The $25,000 allowance does not rescue a high-income household.** Publication 925 also describes a special allowance of up to $25,000 for rental real estate with active participation. It is reduced by 50% of modified adjusted gross income above $100,000 and is gone at $150,000 ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Maya and Chris have $185,000 of combined W-2 income. If their modified AGI is near that figure, the allowance gives them nothing. At a short average stay, it may not apply at all.

**Personal use.** Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal days a year. Publication 527 has a day test. A dwelling is used as a home if personal use exceeds the greater of 14 days or 10% of fair-rental days. It also requires dividing expenses between rental and personal use by days ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). At 212 rented nights, 10% is 21.2 days, so the threshold is 21.2 days. Ten days is below it. More personal use would change the picture, so ask your CPA how it would affect the loss.

**It's timing, not a gift.** Depreciation lowers your basis. When you sell, some of the deduction can come back as taxable gain (often called depreciation recapture). That's why this stream is called "tax timing". Lesson OP.12 (Exit Strategy: Hold, Refinance, Sell, or 1031 Exchange) treats the tax on sale as a CPA input.

### 0.2.6 Owning compared with leasing a similar house

Part One of this course is about leasing a place and re-renting it to guests (arbitrage). So a fair question is: why not lease a house like Cedar Ridge instead of buying it?

To compare fairly, keep the operating assumptions identical. Same revenue, same cleaner, same utilities. Change only what differs between owning and leasing. The lease figures below are a hypothetical arbitrage-style lease on a comparable house. **All figures in this table are assumptions.** Rent is $2,900 a month on a 12-month lease with written short-term rental (STR) permission. The tenant carries minor repairs of $1,200 a year and a renter's STR liability policy of $1,800 a year. The landlord pays property tax, building insurance, and capital repairs.

| Line (annual) | Own Cedar Ridge (DSCR quote) | Lease a comparable house (assumption) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Gross booking revenue (base) | $74,275 | $74,275 |
| Platform fee, cleaner, utilities, supplies, software, handyman | -$30,588 | -$30,588 |
| Repairs + capital reserve (own) / minor repairs (lease) | -$7,428 | -$1,200 |
| Property tax + insurance (own) / renter's STR policy (lease) | -$7,050 | -$1,800 |
| Loan payment (own) / rent (lease) | -$23,637 | -$34,800 |
| **Base-case cash flow** | **$5,572** | **$5,887** |
| Weak-case cash flow ($265 a night, 50% occupancy) | -$5,475 | -$6,856 |
| Cash in (see below) | $142,688 | $44,500 |
| Cash-on-cash, base | 3.9% | 13.2% |
| Principal paydown, year 1 | $2,795 | none |

How the lease cash in adds up: $32,000 of furnishing, a $2,900 deposit, and $2,900 of first month's rent. Add a two-month operating reserve of $6,700 (rent plus $450 of utilities, times two). Total: $44,500.

- Base-case cash flow is about the same. Leasing is $315 a year higher.
- Owning needs $98,188 more cash ($142,688 minus $44,500). That cash is the cost of streams two, three, and four.
- In the weak case, the lease loses more, because rent is fixed and higher than the owner's loan payment. The owner, though, also carries capital repairs that the reserve may not fully cover in a bad year.
- The owner builds equity and carries the value risk, up or down. The tenant has neither.
- The owner may depreciate the building, improvements, and furnishings. The tenant may depreciate only furnishings and their own improvements.
- The tenant's exposure ends with the lease, or on its early-exit terms. The owner's continues until a sale (7% costs assumed) or a refinance.

For some households, $44,500 in a lease beats $142,688 in a house. For others, the long hold and the equity matter more than the cash-on-cash rate.

One practical caveat. The lease column is a teaching comparison. In some drive-to lake markets, owners of houses like this may not be willing to lease them for short-term rental. Sam's PA.2 (Choosing a Market for Ownership) and the Part One research lessons show how to check.

### 0.2.7 Worked example: stacking the four streams without cheating

Here is the right way to present Cedar Ridge's year one on the DSCR quote. Keep the streams in separate rows, and label each by whether you can spend it.

| Stream | Year 1 amount | Can you spend it this year? | What it depends on |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Operating cash flow | $5,572 | Yes | Base case revenue; weak case is -$5,475 |
| Principal paydown | $2,795 | No, locked until sale or refinance | Paying the loan every month |
| Change in value (3%, assumption) | $11,550 | No, and it may be negative | Market; only realized by selling or borrowing |
| Tax timing (illustrative, if usable) | $0 to about $34,263 of tax saved | Only if the loss is usable against other income this year | Basis, land split, placed-in-service date, classification, passive rules, material participation, personal use, household tax rate; partly taxed back at sale (recapture) |

Now look at return on cash in, one layer at a time:

- Cash flow alone: $5,572 / $142,688 = 3.9%.
- Cash flow plus principal paydown: $8,367 / $142,688 = 5.9%.
- Adding assumed appreciation: $19,917 / $142,688 = 14.0%.

Those three percentages describe three very different things. Only the first one pays a bill. The third one includes an assumption you can't check until you sell. When someone shows you a single "total return" number, ask for this table instead.

I left tax timing out of those percentages on purpose. It depends on your CPA's answers, and it can be zero in year one if the loss is suspended (held for later years, not lost).

### 0.2.8 One case where ownership works, one where it doesn't

Now a five-year view, using the course example's sale model. Assumptions: base-case cash flow stays flat for five years (assumption), and the original DSCR loan stays in place. The house sells at the end of year five with 7% selling costs. Every figure is before income tax, and before any tax on the sale or depreciation recapture.

**Case A: ownership works (self-managed, base case, 3% value growth).**

- Five years of cash flow: 5 x $5,572 = $27,860.
- Net sale proceeds before tax: $142,560 (value $446,321, less loan balance $272,519, less selling costs $31,242).
- Total back: $27,860 + $142,560 = $170,420.
- Less cash in: $170,420 - $142,688 = $27,732 ahead, before tax.

Principal paydown isn't a separate line here. It's already inside net sale proceeds. The loan balance falls from the original $288,750 to $272,519 by year five, so $16,231 of paydown is in there.

Plus whatever tax timing your CPA confirms, less whatever tax is due on the sale. It is a modest result for five years and $142,688. But it is positive, and Maya and Chris keep control. It can absorb a year or two of weak revenue only if they hold cash reserves beyond the $142,688. Lesson 0.3 sizes those reserves.

**Case B: ownership does not work (full-service co-host at 18%).**

Suppose Maya and Chris decide they don't want to run the house. They hire a full-service local co-host: a local manager who runs the listing and guests for a share of revenue. This one takes 18% of booking revenue. The co-host costs $13,370 a year in the base case.

- Annual cash flow on the DSCR quote: -$7,798.
- Five years: 5 x -$7,798 = -$38,990.
- Total back: -$38,990 + $142,560 = $103,570.
- Less cash in: $103,570 - $142,688 = $39,118 behind, before tax.

In this case they pay about $650 a month to own the house. The only path to breaking even is appreciation above the 3% assumption or tax timing, and neither is spendable while you're writing checks every month. If revenue is also weak, the co-host case loses $15,794 a year: weak NOI of $18,162, less the co-host's $10,319, less $23,637 of debt service.

Two more versions of the same test, Cases C and D:

- Case C. Weak case, self-managed, 3% value growth: 5 x -$5,475 = -$27,375 of cash flow. Add $142,560 of proceeds and subtract $142,688 of cash in. Result: $27,503 behind, before tax.
- Case D. Base case, self-managed, flat value: $27,860 of cash flow plus $85,531 of proceeds, less $142,688. Result: $29,297 behind, before tax.

So Cedar Ridge "wins" only on specific facts. Revenue hits the base case or better. The owners self-manage, with a local cleaner and handyman rather than a full-service co-host. The house holds or gains value. Change any one of those and it falls behind over five years, before tax. Whether a weak-case loss is a deal-breaker depends on how much you could accept losing. That's test 2 in the decision rule below.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### 0.2.9 Exercise: the four-stream test for your deal

Fill this in for any property you're seriously considering. Sam's PA.4 (Reading a Full Underwriting Package) produces the NOI, weak case, and loan payment it needs. No property yet? Fill it in for Cedar Ridge using the numbers in this lesson. For a real deal, use your own lender quote. Do not fill in the tax row until your CPA answers the questions in 0.2.5.

**Part A. Year-one streams**

| Stream | Base case | Weak case | Spendable this year? (Y/N) | Source of the number |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| Operating cash flow (NOI minus principal and interest) | | | | PA.4 model |
| Principal paydown, year 1 | | | | Lender amortization schedule |
| Change in value (state your % assumption) | | | | Your assumption; write it down |
| Tax timing (CPA estimate, or blank) | | | | CPA |

**Part B. Five-year test (before income tax)**

| Line | Base, value +__% | Weak, value +__% | Base, value flat |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Five years of cash flow | | | |
| Sale value, year 5 | | | |
| Less loan balance, year 5 (lender amortization schedule) | | | |
| Less selling costs (__%) | | | |
| Net proceeds before tax | | | |
| Total back (cash flow + proceeds) | | | |
| Less total cash in | | | |
| **Ahead / behind** | | | |

**Part C. Same house, leased (if a lease is realistic in this market)**

| Line | Own | Lease |
|---|---:|---:|
| Cash in | | |
| Base-case cash flow | | |
| Weak-case cash flow | | |
| Cash-on-cash, base | | |
| Worst realistic exit cost (own: selling costs; lease: early-termination terms) | | |

**Decision rule.** Ownership "wins" for this deal only if all three are true:

1. The Part B base case is ahead.
2. The Part B weak case is behind by no more than you could accept losing.
3. In Part C, the extra cash for owning buys something you want: a long hold, personal use, or equity. If any one fails, write down which, and bring it to 0.3.

**Cedar Ridge on this rule.** Test 1 passes: +$27,732 in the base case. Test 2 depends on Maya and Chris. The weak case ends $27,503 behind, and only they can say whether that's acceptable. Test 3 is their call too. Their $98,188 of extra cash over leasing buys equity, value exposure, and possible tax timing.

**Do this week.**

1. Rebuild the four-stream table for Cedar Ridge from this lesson, labeling each row spendable or not.
2. Write your own downside limit: the most you'd accept being behind after five years, before tax.
3. List the CPA questions from 0.2.5 and book a first meeting.
4. Ask one lender for a sample amortization schedule at a quote you might use.

### 0.2.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** your CPA the land allocation, placed-in-service date, recovery period, and bonus depreciation eligibility. Also confirm whether the activity counts as a rental activity for your household, your participation hours, and personal-use days. Ask whether any loss is usable this year or suspended, and what tax is due on a future sale.

**Confirm with:** your lender the loan balance schedule (amortization) and the actual payment, so principal paydown uses real numbers.

**Confirm with:** a local agent or appraiser the value assumptions you use for the five-year test. Treat any growth rate as your assumption, not theirs.

**Confirm with:** a cost segregation provider and your CPA whether a study makes sense for the property, before counting accelerated depreciation. Sam's PA.5 covers the study, and resource SR.3 (Cost segregation & tax-impact estimator) helps you prepare.

### 0.2.11 Next lesson

You now know that ownership can win or lose on the same house depending on revenue, management, and value. Lesson 0.3, Is Ownership Right for You? A Capital & Readiness Checklist, asks the next question. Can Maya and Chris fund the full cash stack, including reserves and their own emergency savings? And can they get through a weak first season?

### 0.2 sources

- [IRS Publication 527, Residential Rental Property](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): land is never depreciable; depreciation begins when property is ready and available for rent (July/September example); dwelling used as a home if personal use exceeds the greater of 14 days or 10% of fair-rental days; expense division between rental and personal use. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov (direct fetch blocked).
- [IRS Publication 925, Passive Activity and At-Risk Rules](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925): 7-day average period of customer use exception and how to compute the average; material participation tests (500 hours; substantially all; more than 100 hours and not less than any other individual); $25,000 special allowance with phase-out between $100,000 and $150,000 of modified AGI. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov (direct fetch blocked).
- [IRS newsroom, Treasury and IRS guidance on the additional first-year depreciation deduction (Notice 2026-11)](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill): permanent 100% additional first-year depreciation for eligible property acquired after January 19, 2025. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov.
- Cedar Ridge figures (sections B1 to B8 of the course example file, `reference/recurring-examples.md`), all assumptions. The schedule labels, the full-year simplification, and "suspended and carried forward" are from B6. New figures are assumptions computed with python from `tools/examples.py`: the 3% year-one value change of $11,550, the flat-value sale and its $57,157 gap, the $16,231 of five-year paydown, five-year totals, and the lease comparison ($2,900 rent, $1,200 repairs, $1,800 renter's policy, $44,500 cash in).

<a id="lesson-0.3-capital-readiness-checklist"></a>
## 0.3  Is Ownership Right for You? A Capital & Readiness Checklist

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Module 0 - Ownership vs. Arbitrage |
| Code | 0.3 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.2 (before) and PA.1 (next); Preston's PA.6 and PA.7 for financing detail; Part One lease path (1.3, 1.5); Preston's PA.10 for tax questions |

### 0.3.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you can add up the full cash an ownership deal requires, not just the down payment. You can compare it with the money you actually have, after keeping your own emergency savings. You can test whether you'd survive a delayed opening or a weak first season. And you finish with a one-page readiness scorecard. It sends you to one of four places. The first is PA.1, the next lesson, where you write your buy box: a written spec of what you'll buy. The second is better financing or liquidity first. The third is the Part One lease path: renting a place from a landlord and hosting it instead of buying, sometimes called arbitrage. The fourth is wait.

### 0.3.1 The down payment is the smallest question

**Meet the example.** I'll use one hypothetical couple all the way through. Maya and Chris are a two-earner household with $185,000 of combined income and credit scores around 740. They have $210,000 in liquid savings outside retirement accounts. They own their home, worth $520,000, with a $260,000 mortgage.

They're buying Cedar Ridge, a hypothetical three-bedroom house in a lake market about two hours from a metro area. The contract price is $385,000, with 25% down. It's a short-term rental (STR) they'll also use about 10 days a year. It isn't their main home.

They have two hypothetical loan quotes. The first is a **DSCR loan**. DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio: the lender qualifies the loan mainly on the property's income compared with its payment. That quote is 7.25% with 1 point. Its monthly payment, with taxes, insurance, and dues (PITIA, explained in 0.3.4), is $2,557. The second is a **conventional loan**, qualified mainly on their household income and debts. It's 6.875% with no points, and its PITIA is $2,484.

A *point* is an upfront fee equal to 1% of the loan, paid to get a lower rate. In the course example, the house books about 212 nights a year across 61 stays. Its average nightly rate (ADR) is $300. Every Cedar Ridge figure is an assumption for teaching.

Most buyers start with one number: the down payment. For Cedar Ridge, 25% of $385,000 is $96,250. Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings outside retirement accounts. On that math alone, they look ready twice over.

That math leaves out most of the bill. An owned short-term rental needs money for closing, furnishing, repairs, and permits. It needs money for the months before the first guest. And it needs reserves, for the lender and for you. It also needs your own household to stay safe if the house has a bad year.

This lesson lays out every layer, then runs Maya and Chris through it. Learning "not yet" here is much cheaper than at a closing table.

### 0.3.2 The full cash stack, layer by layer

Think of the cash requirement as a stack of eight layers. The order doesn't change the total. You need all of them funded, or you are relying on the house to perform from day one.

| Layer | What it covers | Spent or held? |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Down payment | Your share of the price | Spent (becomes equity) |
| 2. Closing costs and points | Lender fees, title, escrow, prepaid items, discount points | Spent |
| 3. Furnishing and setup | Furniture, linens, kitchen, safety equipment, photos | Spent |
| 4. Initial repairs and safety punch list | Inspection findings you'll fix yourself after closing, plus work needed before guests | Spent |
| 5. Permit, registration, inspections | Local fees and specialist inspections | Spent |
| 6. Launch carrying costs | Loan payment, utilities, software, and vendor retainers before the first booking | Spent |
| 7. Property reserves | The lender's required reserves, which after closing become your operating cushion for the house | Held |
| 8. Personal emergency savings | Your household's own safety net, separate from the house | Held |

Layers 1 to 6 are money you will spend. Layers 7 and 8 are money you must still have after spending it.

A common failure is merging layers 7 and 8. A buyer counts the same dollars as the house's reserve and the family's emergency fund. Then the water heater fails in the same month a paycheck is late, and there's only one pile of cash for two problems.

### 0.3.3 Where the money can come from, and the limits on each source

Money for the stack can come from several places. Each has limits, and your lender may treat them differently. For this lesson, we count only liquid savings.

**Liquid savings.** Checking, savings, and taxable brokerage accounts. This is the cleanest source. Lenders usually want to see where it came from and how long it has been there. Ask your lender what documentation they need for large recent deposits.

**Retirement accounts.** Maya and Chris's $210,000 excludes retirement accounts. Some lenders may count part of a retirement balance toward reserves, and withdrawals can carry taxes and penalties. Treat this as a question for your lender and CPA, not as spendable cash.

**Home equity.** Maya and Chris own their primary home. It's worth $520,000 with a $260,000 mortgage balance, so they have $260,000 of equity on paper. A home equity line of credit (HELOC) lets you borrow against it. The CFPB notes that HELOCs usually carry a variable rate, so payments can change, and your home is the collateral. If you can't repay, you could lose your home ([CFPB, What is a HELOC?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/)). A HELOC is borrowed money, not savings. It adds a payment to your household budget. Ask the lender whether HELOC funds can go toward a down payment or count as reserves. Also ask how the new payment affects your qualification. OP.11 covers HELOCs for scaling in more depth.

**Gifts or partners.** Lenders have rules on gift funds and co-borrowers. If a partner puts in money, ownership and loan paperwork must match. PA.10 (entity and tax structure) covers that.

**Seller concessions.** A seller may pay part of your closing costs. That's a negotiation item for PA.8 (offers and negotiation), not something to count on now.

If the stack only works with borrowed money, record that as a finding.

### 0.3.4 Reserves: the lender's minimum is not your plan

Fannie Mae's Selling Guide measures reserves in months of the new loan's payment. That payment is principal, interest, taxes, insurance, and association dues (PITIA). The question is how many months your financial assets could cover ([Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-4.1-01 Minimum Reserve Requirements](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements)).

A few points from that section, as they apply to a buyer like Maya and Chris:

- There is no minimum reserve requirement for a one-unit principal residence. Second homes and investment properties do have requirements. Cedar Ridge isn't Maya and Chris's principal residence, so the no-minimum rule doesn't apply to it. Their lender decides which category it falls in.
- If you already own other financed properties besides your home, lenders can require extra reserves. The add-on leaves out the house you're buying and your principal residence. For Maya and Chris, that leaves nothing, so the add-on is likely zero. Their lender confirms.

The DSCR quote in the course example is not a Fannie Mae loan. Each DSCR lender publishes or quotes its own reserve rule. For teaching, we assume the DSCR lender requires **6 months of PITIA (assumption)**. On the DSCR quote that is 6 x $2,557 = $15,342. We use the same 6 months (assumption) on the conventional quote so the two are comparable: 6 x $2,484 = $14,904. Get the real number from each lender in writing. PA.6 and PA.7 go deeper.

After closing, the lender stops watching. The reserve becomes your operating cushion for the house. The lender's number is a floor to get the loan. It is not a plan for a bad season. Maya and Chris will set their own reserve target in the worked example.

### 0.3.5 Credit, eligibility, and debt capacity

Besides cash, three things shape whether a lender will lend: credit, loan type, and debt capacity. You can check all three before you choose a house.

**Credit.** Maya and Chris have scores around 740. Each lender sets its own minimum and prices its rate by score. Kiavi's DSCR guide, for example, says the lender looks at credit score and down payment, not only the property's coverage ratio ([Kiavi DSCR guide](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans)). That is one lender's approach, not a market standard.

**Eligibility of the loan type.** A DSCR loan qualifies mainly on the property's income compared with its payment. A conventional loan qualifies mainly on your household income and debts. Fannie Mae's Selling Guide has a section on short-term rental income from the property being bought ([Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-3.8-03](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental)). Confirm the current version with your lender. Whether a particular lender will count projected or actual STR income for your loan is their call. PA.7 (DSCR versus conventional) compares the two paths.

**Debt capacity.** A conventional lender compares your monthly debts with your income (the debt-to-income ratio, or DTI). Here is a rough illustration for Maya and Chris. **All figures are assumptions** except their income and the Cedar Ridge PITIA.

| Line | Monthly |
|---|---:|
| Gross income ($185,000 / 12) | $15,417 |
| Primary home payment (assumption) | $2,400 |
| Other debts: cars, cards (assumption) | $600 |
| Cedar Ridge PITIA, conventional quote | $2,484 |
| **Total debts** | **$5,484** |
| **DTI with no STR income counted** | **35.6%** |

The lender sets the maximum ratio and decides how much rental income, if any, to credit. So 35.6% isn't good or bad on its own. Ask each lender for its maximum DTI in writing and write it next to yours. This rough check only tells you whether a conventional path is worth asking about. (For your own household budget, the DSCR payment of $2,557 gives 36.0%.)

A DSCR lender looks at a different number: the property's income divided by its PITIA, called the coverage ratio. In the course example, Cedar Ridge's ratio on the DSCR quote runs from 1.18 to 2.42. It depends on how the lender defines income. The lender's written definition decides which one counts, and PA.6 (DSCR loans) walks through the math. Ask each DSCR lender for its minimum ratio in writing.

### 0.3.6 A delayed opening or a weak first season

Two things go wrong often enough that you should budget for both.

**Delayed opening.** Permits take longer than expected. Furniture arrives late. A contractor misses a week. Every month between closing and the first guest costs the full carrying cost with no revenue. For Cedar Ridge on the DSCR quote, that is $3,257 a month. It's the $2,557 PITIA plus the course example's $450 of utilities, $100 of software, and $150 handyman retainer.

The base stack includes two months of carrying costs (assumption), or $6,514. A three-month delay beyond that adds another 3 x $3,257 = $9,771. Paid from the 6-month property reserve of $15,342, that would leave $5,571.

**Weak first season.** A new listing has no reviews and may book below its long-run potential. The course example's weak case has an ADR of $265 at 50% occupancy (the share of nights booked). On the DSCR quote it loses $5,475 a year, after the loan payment and all operating costs. That is about $456 a month. This is the pre-diligence figure, and PA.9 revisits it.

Now put the two together. A 3-month delay takes $9,771 of the reserve, leaving $5,571. One weak year then takes $5,475. Together they use $15,246 of the $15,342 reserve, leaving $96. Plan for that pairing. It's the kind of pairing that can force a sale at a bad time.

### 0.3.7 Time, involvement, and risk tolerance

Cash isn't the only resource. Owning also takes time and a stomach for variance.

**Time.** In the course example's base case, Maya and Chris self-manage with a local cleaner and an on-call handyman. Assume they spend 1.5 hours per stay on messages, scheduling, and turnover checks (assumption). Add 4 hours a month on bookkeeping, pricing, and vendor calls (assumption). With the example's 61 stays a year, that is 91.5 + 48 = about 140 hours a year, or 2.7 hours a week (assumption). Some weeks will take much more: a lockout at 11 p.m., a broken hot water heater on a Saturday.

That time estimate also matters for taxes. A rental can show a loss on paper for tax purposes. Whether that loss is usable against your other income can depend on *material participation*. That's a set of IRS tests about how involved you are, and lesson 0.2 covers it. Publication 925's tests look at hours, including how your hours compare with those of other individuals, such as a cleaner ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). If the cleaner spends 4 hours on each of 61 turnovers (assumption), that is 244 hours, more than the owners' 140. Your CPA confirms how your hours count.

**Involvement choice.** If you don't want to spend those hours, the alternative is a co-host or manager. In 0.2, a full-service co-host charging 18% of booking revenue turned Cedar Ridge's pre-diligence base-case cash flow from $5,572 to -$7,798 a year. Decide now which kind of owner you'll be, because it changes whether the deal works.

**Personal use.** Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal days a year. IRS Publication 527 treats a dwelling as used as a home if personal use passes a threshold. The threshold is the greater of 14 days or 10% of fair-rental days. It also requires dividing expenses by days ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). Here I count the example's 212 rented nights as fair-rental days, and your CPA confirms the count. On that basis the threshold is 21.2 days, so 10 days is below it. There is also a revenue cost. If those 10 nights would have booked at the $300 base ADR, they're worth up to $3,000 of nightly revenue (assumption). Plan your own stays for slower weeks.

**Risk tolerance.** Lesson 0.2 ran a five-year test. It added five years of cash flow to the sale proceeds, then compared the total with the $142,688 they put in. In the base case they came out $27,732 ahead before tax. In the weak case they came out $27,503 behind. Both of you should look at the second number and say out loud whether you could live with it. If the honest answer is no, that's a readiness result, not a failure.

### 0.3.8 Worked example: Maya and Chris's full cash stack

Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings. Their household's essential spending is $8,500 a month (assumption), and they want to keep 6 months of it as personal emergency savings (assumption): $51,000.

They also set their own property-reserve target (assumption). It has to survive the pairing from 0.3.6: a 3-month delay plus one weak year. That's $9,771 + $5,475 = $15,246. The 6-month lender figure of $15,342 just covers it, with $96 to spare. So they keep $15,342 as both the lender floor and their own target.

Here is the stack on each quote. Figures without a tag come from the course's example house file. New figures are marked (assumption).

| Layer | DSCR quote | Conventional quote |
|---|---:|---:|
| Down payment (25%) | $96,250 | $96,250 |
| Closing costs incl. points | $14,438 | $11,550 |
| **Cash to close** | **$110,688** | **$107,800** |
| Furnishing and setup | $32,000 | $32,000 |
| Initial repairs and safety punch list (assumption) | $4,000 | $4,000 |
| Inspections: general, septic, specialist (assumption) | $1,500 | $1,500 |
| Permit and registration fees (assumption) | $600 | $600 |
| Launch carrying costs, 2 months (assumption) | $6,514 | $6,368 |
| **Total spent (layers 1 to 6)** | **$155,302** | **$152,268** |
| Property reserve, 6 months PITIA (assumption) | $15,342 | $14,904 |
| Personal emergency savings, 6 months (assumption) | $51,000 | $51,000 |
| **Full stack** | **$221,644** | **$218,172** |
| Liquid savings | $210,000 | $210,000 |
| **Surplus / gap** | **-$11,644** | **-$8,172** |

Step by step for the DSCR column. Spent layers: $110,688 + $32,000 + $4,000 + $1,500 + $600 + $6,514 = $155,302. Add the $15,342 property reserve and the $51,000 emergency fund: $221,644. Subtract from $210,000: a gap of $11,644.

Two readings of the same table:

- **The lender's view.** After spending $155,302, Maya and Chris would still hold $54,698. That's about 21 months of the DSCR PITIA, well above a 6-month reserve rule. On paper, a lender may say yes.
- **Their own plan.** They can't fund the house's reserve and their own emergency savings at the same time. They are $11,644 short on the DSCR quote and $8,172 short on the conventional quote.

This is the gap the down payment check hides.

Options to close the gap, in the order I'd look at them:

1. **Time.** If they save $3,500 a month (assumption), the DSCR gap closes in about 3.3 months, the conventional gap in about 2.3 months. Searching, underwriting, and closing take a while anyway, so time is often the cheapest fix.
2. **Financing.** The conventional quote needs $2,888 less at closing (it has no point) and has a lower payment, if they qualify. That shrinks the gap to $8,172; it doesn't close it. PA.7 compares the two loans.
3. **Price.** A lower price reduces the down payment and closing costs. PA.1 turns this into a hard cap.
4. **Furnishing plan.** A phased furnishing plan may cut the up-front budget. OP.6 (furnishing) covers this without cutting safety or listing accuracy.

What they should **not** do: shrink their emergency fund to make the table balance, or count a HELOC as savings.

### 0.3.9 The one-page readiness scorecard

Fill this in before you shop. Four tasks get you there.

1. Total your liquid savings, leaving out retirement accounts and home equity.
2. Write your essential monthly spending and multiply it by the months of emergency savings you'll keep.
3. Fill in the eight-layer stack for one realistic price, and write down the surplus or gap. Where you don't have a number yet, use Maya and Chris's line as a placeholder and mark it (assumption). Scale it to your price where it's a percentage of price, like the down payment and closing costs. Then replace each assumption using the "Who confirms what" list in 0.3.11.
4. Score all 13 lines below, with evidence, and read off your outcome.

The first two tasks take minutes. The stack takes longer until you have lender numbers.

Score each line Green, Yellow, or Red. Green clears the line with room. Yellow clears it only with a written condition and a date. Red doesn't clear it now. Some scores rest on a lender figure you don't have in writing yet. Mark those "lender to confirm" and put a confirm-by date in the Evidence column. The outcome rules follow the table.

**Readiness scorecard** (one page)

Name(s): ____________ Date: ____________ Quote used: DSCR / Conventional / Other ____________

| # | Check | Green | Yellow | Red | Your score | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Full cash stack (0.3.8) from liquid savings | Surplus of $0 or more | Gap you can close with normal saving within 6 months | Larger gap, or funded only with borrowed money | | |
| 2 | Personal emergency savings kept intact after closing | 6+ months of essential spending | 3 to 6 months | Under 3 months | | |
| 3 | Property reserve after closing | At or above the lender's rule and your own target | At lender rule only | Below lender rule | | |
| 4 | Credit | At or above the lender's written minimum, with room | At the minimum | Below the minimum | | |
| 5 | Lender fit | Written pre-qualification or term sheet for at least one loan path | Verbal quote or pre-qualification only, with written quotes scheduled in PA.6 and PA.7 | None, or declined | | |
| 6 | Debt capacity (conventional) or property coverage (DSCR) | Inside the lender's stated limit (ask the lender for its maximum DTI or minimum coverage ratio, in writing) | Near the limit | Outside it | | |
| 7 | Delayed opening: 3 extra months absorbed | From property reserve, emergency fund untouched | Needs part of emergency fund | Cannot be absorbed | | |
| 8 | Weak first season | Weak-case loss covered 2+ years by property reserve | 1 to 2 years | Under 1 year | | |
| 9 | Time available | Weekly hours available exceed the plan's estimate, plus a local backup | Just enough | Not enough, and no budget for a co-host | | |
| 10 | Involvement choice tested | Deal still works with your chosen management model (0.2) | Works only if you self-manage and you're unsure you will | Doesn't work with the model you'll actually use | | |
| 11 | Risk tolerance | Both owners accept the five-year weak-case result in writing | One owner unsure | Either owner can't accept it | | |
| 12 | Personal use plan | Below the Pub 527 threshold, or reviewed by CPA | Plan unclear | Heavy personal use, not reviewed | | |
| 13 | Lease path considered | You compared owning with leasing (lesson 0.2) and chose owning for a stated reason | Not compared | Leasing clearly fits better and you'd still buy | | |

The cut-offs in this scorecard are teaching defaults (assumption), not lender or industry rules. That covers the 6-month saving window, the 3- and 6-month emergency bands, and the 1- and 2-year weak-season bands. It also covers the rescore timing in the outcome rules below. Set your own if your situation calls for it.

**Outcome rules**

Check the rows from top to bottom. The first row that matches your scores decides where you go.

| Result | Where you go next |
|---|---|
| Two or more Reds anywhere | **Wait.** Rescore in six months. |
| One Red, on line 9, 10, 11, or 12 | **Wait.** Fix the time, risk, or personal-use question before money is at stake. If time is the only issue, check whether a co-host still leaves the deal positive. |
| One Red, on line 1, 2, or 13 | **Use the lease path (Part One).** A lease needs far less cash. Alder Street, the course's example leased apartment, needed $20,370 on day one. Start with lesson 1.3. |
| One Red, on any of lines 3 to 8 | **Improve financing or liquidity first.** Get written quotes (PA.6, PA.7), pay down debt, or save. Rescore in 60 to 90 days. |
| No Reds, all Green | **Proceed to PA.1.** |
| No Reds, and every Yellow has a written condition and a date | **Proceed to PA.1 with conditions.** Write each condition into your buy box: no offer until it's met. |
| No Reds, but a Yellow has no condition or date | **Wait.** Write the condition and the date, then rescore. |

### 0.3.10 Maya and Chris, scored

| # | Check | Score | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Full cash stack | Yellow | Gap of $11,644 (DSCR) or $8,172 (conventional). Condition: save $3,500 a month (assumption) and have the stack funded four months after they start PA.1, with that date on the calendar |
| 2 | Emergency savings | Green | They refuse to spend below 6 months, $51,000 |
| 3 | Property reserve | Green | $15,342 meets the assumed 6-month rule and their own delay-plus-weak-year target of $15,246 |
| 4 | Credit | Green (lender to confirm in writing) | Around 740; confirm the lender's minimum by PA.7 |
| 5 | Lender fit | Yellow | Hypothetical, verbal quotes only. Condition: written quotes in PA.6 and PA.7, before any offer |
| 6 | Debt capacity / coverage | Green (lender to confirm in writing) | 35.6% illustrative DTI with no STR income counted. Coverage on the DSCR quote: 1.18 to 2.42 depending on the lender's definition (course example). Confirm by PA.7 |
| 7 | Delayed opening | Green | 3-month delay costs $9,771; reserve covers it with $5,571 left |
| 8 | Weak first season | Green | $15,342 covers about 2.8 years of the $5,475 weak-case loss |
| 9 | Time | Yellow | About 2.7 hours a week (assumption); local backup not yet named. Condition: name one in PA.1 |
| 10 | Involvement | Yellow | The deal works only if they self-manage, and they haven't yet tested whether 2.7 hours a week fits both jobs. The course example loses $7,798 a year with an 18% co-host (lesson 0.2). Condition: self-management goes in the buy box |
| 11 | Risk tolerance | Green | Both reviewed the -$27,503 five-year weak case and accept it |
| 12 | Personal use | Green | 10 days, below the 21.2-day threshold; CPA to confirm |
| 13 | Lease path | Green | Compared in 0.2; they chose owning for a long hold and personal use |

**Outcome:** Proceed to PA.1 with conditions. There are no Reds. The Yellows are on lines 1, 5, 9, and 10, and each one has a written condition and a date. Line 1 has a funding date four months after they start PA.1 (assumption). The saving runs while they work through the next few lessons (PA.2 to PA.4). Line 5 is resolved in PA.6 and PA.7 by getting written quotes before any offer. Line 9 gets a named local backup in PA.1. Line 10 becomes a hard requirement in their buy box: the deal has to work with self-management.

### 0.3.11 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** each lender, in writing, the reserve months and how they're calculated, the minimum credit score, and the down payment. Also confirm acceptable sources of funds (HELOC funds, gifts, retirement assets) and how rental income is counted.

**Confirm with:** your CPA the tax effect of retirement withdrawals, how your hours count for material participation, and how personal-use days are treated.

**Confirm with:** your insurance agent the premium and when it's due, so layer 2 or layer 6 of the stack is accurate.

**Confirm with:** the local permit office the fees and processing time, so the launch carrying-cost assumption is realistic.

### 0.3.12 Next lesson

If your scorecard says proceed, go to PA.1, Discovery: Defining Your Buy Box (Market, Property Type, Price Range, Goals). It turns your goals and numbers into a written search specification. Maya and Chris carry their conditions and their reserve targets straight into it. If your scorecard said lease path, go to Part One, lesson 1.3. If it said wait, set a rescore date on your calendar now.

### 0.3 sources

- [Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-4.1-01 Minimum Reserve Requirements](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements): reserves measured in months of the subject loan's PITIA; DU determines reserves for DU loans and the Eligibility Matrix sets them for manual loans; no minimum for one-unit principal residence; two months for a DU second-home transaction; additional reserves for multiple financed properties at 2% / 4% / 6% of aggregate UPB of other financed properties (excluding subject and principal residence). Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to fanniemae.com (direct fetch blocked). The exact month count for an investment property transaction was not shown in search results.
- [Fannie Mae Selling Guide, B3-3.8-03 Rental Income from the Subject Property: Short-Term Rental](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental): section exists within the September 2026 rental income chapter. Brief-provided; existence confirmed via web search 2026-09-25; details not independently opened.
- [Kiavi, The Complete Guide to DSCR Rental Property Loans](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans): the lender considers credit score and down payment as well as coverage ratio. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to kiavi.com. One lender's terms, not a market standard.
- [CFPB, What is a home equity line of credit (HELOC)?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/): variable rate, home as collateral, draw and repayment periods. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to consumerfinance.gov.
- [IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925): material participation tests compare your hours with other individuals'. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov.
- [IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): greater of 14 days or 10% personal-use threshold; expense division by days. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to irs.gov.
- Cedar Ridge and Alder Street figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md`. New figures are assumptions computed with python: 6-month reserve rule, $8,500 essential spending, $4,000 repairs, $1,500 inspections, $600 permits, 2 launch months, $3,500 monthly saving, $2,400 and $600 debts, hours per stay.
