<a id="lesson-PA.1-discovery-buy-box"></a>
## PA.1  Discovery: Defining Your Buy Box (Market, Property Type, Price Range, Goals)

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1 - Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.1 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Preston's 0.3 (before); Sam's PA.2 market selection, PA.3 deal sourcing, PA.4 underwriting, PA.5 cost segregation (next); Preston's PA.6 (after Sam's lessons); SR.1 regulatory compliance checklist |

### PA.1.0 Learning outcome

By the end of this lesson you have a written, one-page buy box. It ranks your goals, states your constraints, and names your hard requirements. It also describes the property you want in terms someone else can search with. It ends with a pass rule: a sentence that tells you when to walk away from a listing without debate. ("Pass" here means decline, as in "we'll pass on that one.") Sam's PA.2 and PA.3 lessons use this page to choose a market and build your property pipeline.

**Terms this lesson uses.** The Cedar Ridge figures come from the course's running example, and all of them are assumptions.

| Term | What it means here |
|---|---|
| STR | Short-term rental. |
| Base case and weak case | The course's normal year and bad year for a house. For Cedar Ridge, the weak case is $265 a night at 50% occupancy, instead of $300 at 58%. |
| ADR | Average nightly rate: what guests pay per booked night, on average. |
| NOI (net operating income) | Booking revenue minus operating costs, before the loan payment. Cedar Ridge's costs include a 5% capital reserve, so NOI already sets that aside. |
| Debt service (P&I) | The yearly principal and interest on the loan. |
| PITIA | The full monthly payment: principal, interest, property taxes, insurance, and association (HOA) dues. |
| Point | An upfront lender fee equal to 1% of the loan. |
| DSCR loan | A loan the lender qualifies mainly on the property's income compared with its payment. PA.6 covers it. A conventional investment loan is qualified mainly on your household income and debts. |
| Cash to close; total cash in | Cash to close is the down payment, closing costs, and points. Total cash in adds furnishing. |
| Underwriting | Your spreadsheet estimate of what a property will earn and cost. Sam's PA.4 shows it. |

### PA.1.1 What a buy box is for

A buy box is a search specification. It says what you'll buy, where, at what price, and under what conditions. Anything outside the box, you don't look at twice.

It exists to protect you from two things.

The first is drift. You start looking for a three-bedroom house at $380,000. A month later you're touring a $450,000 house with a pool, because the photos were good and the agent said it "books great". Nobody decided to raise the budget by $70,000. It just happened, one listing at a time.

The second is wasted work. Every property you seriously consider costs hours: reading rules, pulling comparable listings (similar rentals nearby), underwriting, calling lenders. Sam's PA.3 lesson builds a pipeline of 5 to 10 qualified properties. Without a buy box, "qualified" means nothing, and the pipeline fills with houses you should never have looked at.

A good buy box is short and specific. It's written before you see listings. And it's hard to change: if you want to change it, you rewrite it on purpose, with numbers.

### PA.1.2 Start with a goal hierarchy

Before property details, rank your goals. People usually want several things from an STR purchase, and they conflict. A ranking tells you which one wins when they do.

The common goals:

- **Cash flow.** Money left after all costs and the loan payment. This lesson counts it by the year.
- **Long-term ownership.** Holding for many years and building equity through loan paydown and possible value change.
- **Personal use.** Using the house yourself some days each year.
- **Tax planning.** Depreciation and possible use of losses, which depend on facts your CPA confirms (lesson 0.2).
- **Portfolio growth.** Using this property as a step toward a second one.
- **Desired involvement.** How many hours you want to put in, and which jobs you want to hand off.
- **Safety.** Protecting your household's savings and income if the property underperforms.

Here's how they conflict in practice:

- Personal use pulls against cash flow. Your own peak-season week is a week you didn't rent.
- Tax planning can pull against everything else. A deal bought "for the depreciation" can lose money every month. The deduction may also be unusable, depending on your household facts.
- Low involvement pulls against cash flow. In 0.2, a full-service co-host taking 18% of booking revenue turned Cedar Ridge's pre-diligence $5,572 a year of cash flow into -$7,798.
- Portfolio growth pulls against safety. Stretching to buy now so you can buy again sooner leaves less reserve for this house.

Rank your goals from 1 to 7. Only the top two or three should shape the search. The rest are tie-breakers. If two goals always move together for you, you can give them one rank; say so in writing. If both owners rank differently, settle it now. A disagreement you skip now tends to come back at the offer.

### PA.1.3 Write down your constraints

Constraints are limits you can't wish away. They come mostly from lesson 0.3.

**Money.** Two caps. First, the maximum cash to close: the down payment, closing costs, and points you pay on closing day. Second, the maximum total cash in: cash to close plus furnishing. Both must leave your property reserve and personal emergency savings untouched.

To find the total cap, start from your liquid savings. Subtract your personal emergency savings and your property reserve. Then subtract the other startup costs in your 0.3 cash stack, the full list of cash a purchase needs. Those are repairs, inspections, permits, and launch months. What's left is the most you can put into the house.

Why two caps? Closing money is due on one day, and furnishing comes in the weeks after. The cash-to-close cap also stops a smaller furnishing budget from turning into a bigger house. If your 0.3 scorecard carried a funding condition, write it here too.

**Financing path.** Which loan types you'll pursue (DSCR, conventional investment, or both) and the down payment you're planning around. The lender makes the eligibility call later, in PA.6 and PA.7. For now, write the path and the assumed terms.

**Distance.** How far the house can be from you. This isn't only about convenience. Distance decides whether you can handle a problem yourself or must pay someone. Write it in driving hours.

**Time.** The hours per week you'll actually give it, from 0.3.

**Minimum reserves.** The property reserve and personal emergency savings you won't spend below. These are floors, not targets.

### PA.1.4 Describe the property

Now the property itself. Keep each line concrete enough that Sam's PA.2 and PA.3 filters can apply it.

**Market type.** Drive-to leisure, urban, event-driven, business travel. Name the type and the demand driver. Sam's PA.2 picks the actual market.

**Property type.** Detached single-family, townhouse, condo, small multi-unit. This matters for legality (condo and homeowners association, or HOA, rules). It matters for lending, because some lenders restrict condos or certain property types. And it matters for insurance.

**Location inside the market.** Rules can change street by street. Zoning districts, HOA boundaries, and permit caps can all differ within one town. Write where you'll look ("within 10 minutes of the lake") and where you won't.

**Price range.** A floor and a ceiling. The floor screens out houses too small or too worn to fit the rest of the box. The ceiling comes from your money constraints and your downside rule, not from what the market charges. Section PA.1.7 shows how to calculate it.

**Guest capacity.** Bedrooms, bathrooms, and how many guests it sleeps. More capacity can mean a higher nightly rate (ADR), and also more wear, more cleaning time, and more noise risk. Local rules may cap occupancy too.

**Amenities.** Separate what guests in this market expect (parking, reliable internet, a working kitchen) from what adds complexity (pools, hot tubs, docks, boats). Every amenity with water or height carries insurance and safety questions. Your insurer's quote will tell you which ones cost extra or aren't covered.

**Operating complexity.** Septic and wells, long private roads, snow access, older systems, big lots. None of these is a deal-breaker by itself. Each adds cost, inspection work, or response time. Decide how much of it you'll accept.

### PA.1.5 Hard requirements

Hard requirements are the lines a property must clear, every one, before you spend real time on it. They aren't preferences. If a property fails one, you pass on it: you walk away.

Five belong in almost every STR buy box:

1. **Legal STR use at the exact address.** Unhosted short-term rental use must be allowed at that address. Unhosted means you don't live there during guest stays. Any HOA or deed restrictions must allow it too. Airbnb itself tells hosts to check local laws and HOA rules before hosting ([Airbnb Help Center, article 1397](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397)). Use the SR.1 checklist and record the official city or county source.
2. **Acceptable insurance.** A written quote from an insurer willing to cover STR use of this house, at a premium your underwriting can carry. Platform protection is not a substitute; PA.12 covers that.
3. **Lender fit.** The property type, location, and projected income fit a lender's written guidelines for the loan you plan to use.
4. **Maximum cash to close and total cash in.** From PA.1.3. No exceptions for "a great house".
5. **Minimum downside performance.** A number for how badly the weak case may perform at your actual loan quote. Set two numbers. The first is a floor on weak-case annual cash flow after debt service (P&I). The second is a minimum base-case ratio of NOI to debt service.

Add a sixth if 0.3 surfaced it. For Maya and Chris, it's "the deal must work with self-management".

### PA.1.6 The pass rule

The pass rule is one or two sentences you agree on in advance. It tells you what to do when a listing doesn't fit.

A good pass rule has four parts:

- **The trigger.** Any failed hard requirement. Or a price above your ceiling that the seller won't bring down.
- **The action.** Pass. A listing priced above the ceiling can still get an offer at or under the ceiling. If the seller won't accept it, pass. Don't "keep an eye on it" unless the price drops to the ceiling.
- **The record.** Log every pass with the reason: which requirement failed.
- **The change process.** The buy box changes only on purpose: in writing, with the new numbers, signed by every owner. Never during a showing.

Why log the pass? Because after 20 passes you'll see a pattern. Maybe every house in your price range fails the insurance quote. That's information for Sam's PA.2 market choice, not a reason to lower your standards.

### PA.1.7 Worked example: Maya and Chris's buy box, tested on Cedar Ridge

Maya and Chris are the couple this course follows. They're two earners with $210,000 in liquid savings, buying a short-term rental. Cedar Ridge is a hypothetical three-bedroom lake house listed at $399,000. In PA.8 they agree to buy it at $385,000.

In real life, they write the box first and meet Cedar Ridge later, in Sam's PA.3. I'm showing both steps here so you can see the box at work. We'll write their box, then check whether Cedar Ridge belongs in it at both prices.

They finished 0.3 with "proceed to PA.1, with conditions". Figures without a tag come from the course's running example. New figures are marked (assumption). The Cedar Ridge results below are pre-diligence figures, and PA.9 revisits them.

**Their goal hierarchy**

| Rank | Goal | How it shapes the search |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Safety: protect the household | Emergency fund stays at $51,000; property reserve stays at 6 months of PITIA ($15,342) |
| 2 | Long-term ownership, 10+ year hold (assumption) | Favor durable houses and stable rules over a short-term price opportunity |
| 3 | Cash flow, with desired involvement: self-managing about 2.7 hours a week (assumption, from 0.3) | Base-case NOI minus debt service stays positive; NOI already includes the capital reserve |
| 4 | Personal use, about 10 days, off-peak | Drive time matters; personal days go in slow weeks |
| 5 | Tax planning | Tie-breaker only; no deal is bought for depreciation |
| 6 | Portfolio growth | Not before Sam's OP.10 performance review and the OP.11 decision on a second property |

Cash flow and desired involvement share rank 3. For them, self-managing with a local cleaner and an on-call handyman is how the cash flow stays positive. So they ranked the two together.

**Their constraints**

- Maximum total cash in, including $32,000 of furnishing: $145,000 (assumption). The math is below.
- Maximum cash to close: $115,000 (assumption).
- Funding condition from 0.3: no offer until liquid savings cover the full stack. Their plan is to save $3,500 a month (assumption, from 0.3) and be funded four months after starting PA.1.
- Financing: DSCR at the example's hypothetical quote (7.25%, 30-year fixed, 1 point, 25% down). On $385,000, that's a $288,750 loan. Debt service (P&I) is $1,970 a month, or $23,637 a year. They've asked for a conventional quote too, for comparison.
- Distance: 2.5 hours' drive or less from home (assumption: they live in the metro that Cedar Ridge's market serves).
- Reserves: 6 months of PITIA as property reserve (assumption), which is $15,342 on Cedar Ridge. Plus $51,000 personal emergency savings (assumption).

**Where the $145,000 cap comes from.** Start with savings and take out everything that must stay untouched. Today they have $210,000. Take away the $51,000 emergency fund and the $15,342 property reserve: $210,000 - $51,000 - $15,342 = $143,658. That's already under $145,000, before the other startup costs.

Their 0.3 stack has $12,614 of those: a $4,000 punch list, $1,500 of inspections, $600 of permits, and $6,514 for two launch months. Take those out too, and $131,044 is left. So today they can't fund the cap. That's the job of the funding condition.

Four months of saving at $3,500 brings them to $224,000. Then $224,000 - $51,000 - $15,342 - $12,614 = $145,044. They round down to $145,000.

**Why a separate cash-to-close cap?** It doesn't bind for them. $145,000 minus $32,000 of furnishing leaves $113,000 for closing, already under $115,000. It would only take over if the furnishing budget dropped below $30,000. That's its job: a cheaper furnishing plan shouldn't turn into a pricier house.

**Turning the money constraints into a price ceiling.** On the DSCR terms, cash to close is 25% down plus 3% closing costs (the course example's assumption). Then add 1 point. A point is 1% of the loan, and the loan is 75% of the price. So the point costs 1% x 75% = 0.75% of the price. Cash to close is 25% + 3% + 0.75% = 28.75% of the price.

- Cash-to-close cap: $115,000 / 0.2875 = $400,000.
- Total-cash-in cap: ($145,000 - $32,000) / 0.2875 = $113,000 / 0.2875 = $393,043.

The tighter cap wins. Their box ceiling is **$393,000 contract price** on these terms. That covers section C, lines 1 to 3. Line 4, the downside rule, comes later, house by house.

**The downside rule.** Maya and Chris set two numbers (assumption):

- Weak-case annual cash flow after debt service (P&I): no worse than -$6,000. That's about $500 a month they could cover from salary without touching reserves (assumption).
- Base-case NOI divided by annual debt service (P&I): at least 1.20. So NOI must be at least 20% above the loan payment, a cushion before cash flow turns negative.

These are their own rules, not a lender's ratio. PA.6 covers how lenders define coverage.

**The buy box itself**

| Field | Maya and Chris |
|---|---|
| Market type | Drive-to lake leisure market; Sam's PA.2 selects the market |
| Location | Within 2.5 hours of home; within a 10-minute drive of lake access (assumption) |
| Property type | Detached single-family; no HOA, or HOA with written STR permission; no condos |
| Size | 3 to 4 bedrooms, 2+ baths, sleeps 6 to 8 |
| Price range | $325,000 (assumption) to $393,000 contract price |
| Financing | DSCR 25% down; conventional quote for comparison |
| Amenities wanted | Parking for 3 cars, reliable internet, outdoor space, year-round road access |
| Complexity limits | No pool; hot tub only if the insurer quotes it; septic and well accepted with inspection and water test; punch list (small repairs before guests arrive) under $4,000 |
| Hard requirements | (1) Legal unhosted STR use at the address, and HOA if any; (2) written STR insurance quote at or under $3,500 a year (assumption); (3) lender fit in writing; (4) cash to close at or under $115,000 and total cash in at or under $145,000; (5) weak case no worse than -$6,000 a year after debt service, base NOI / debt service at least 1.20; (6) works with self-management and a named local cleaner plus backup |
| Pass rule | Pass on any listing that fails a hard requirement. A listing priced above $393,000 can get an offer at or under $393,000; if the seller won't accept it, pass. Log every pass with the reason. The box changes only in writing, signed by both. |

Their $325,000 floor is a judgment call. It screens out listings too small or too worn to meet their size rule and punch-list limit.

**Test 1: Cedar Ridge at the contract price, $385,000.**

| Check | Result | Clears it? |
|---|---|---|
| Cash to close | $110,688, under $115,000 | Yes |
| Total cash in | $142,688, under $145,000 | Yes |
| Weak-case cash flow | Weak NOI $18,162 - $23,637 debt service = -$5,475, better than -$6,000 | Yes |
| Base NOI / debt service | $29,209 / $23,637 = 1.24, above 1.20 | Yes |
| Property fit | 3 bed, 2 bath, sleeps 8, no HOA, drive-to lake market | Yes |
| Insurance | $3,200 a year in the course example, under the $3,500 cap; written quote needed | Pending |
| Legal STR use | Address-level check needed (PA.2, SR.1) | Pending |
| Lender fit | Written lender answer needed (PA.6) | Pending |
| Self-management | Named local cleaner plus backup still to line up | Pending |

At $385,000, Cedar Ridge clears every number in the box. Four evidence checks are still pending: insurance, legality, lender fit, and a named cleaner with a backup. "Pending" doesn't mean it clears. Those rows stay open until the evidence arrives.

**Test 2: Cedar Ridge at the asking price, $399,000.** Same revenue and operating costs as the course example. One change: property tax scales with price at 1% (assumption). That matches the example's $3,850 on $385,000. All figures below are assumptions.

| Check | Result | Clears it? |
|---|---|---|
| Cash to close | $114,713, under $115,000 | Yes, barely |
| Total cash in | $146,713, over $145,000 | **No** |
| Weak-case cash flow | -$6,475, worse than -$6,000 | **No** |
| Base NOI / debt service | 1.19, under 1.20 | **No** |

Base-case cash flow is still positive, $4,572. The base case still looks fine. That's why people talk themselves into houses like this.

At asking price, the same house fails three hard requirements. So the $14,000 negotiation from $399,000 to $385,000 is what makes Cedar Ridge's numbers work for Maya and Chris. If the seller had refused to go below $399,000, their pass rule says walk. PA.8 covers setting that walk-away price.

The downside rule also gives a price ceiling, but only for a specific house. It depends on that house's revenue, so it's section C, line 4. For Cedar Ridge, the weak case reaches -$6,000 at about $392,000 (assumption, same property-tax scaling). That's about $1,000 under the $393,000 box ceiling, and the lowest ceiling wins. So for this house, the limit is about $392,000. At $392,500, Cedar Ridge is under the box ceiling but fails hard requirement 5. Its weak case is about -$6,011.

**Test 3: a house to walk away from.** A four-bedroom house with a pool, listed at $435,000 in the same market (hypothetical). On their DSCR terms, cash to close would be $435,000 x 28.75% = $125,063. Total cash in would be $125,063 + $32,000 = $157,063. It fails the cash caps before anyone looks at revenue. It also has a pool, which is outside their complexity limit. An offer at $393,000 wouldn't fix the pool. Pass, logged as "over cash cap; pool". No underwriting needed.

That's the value of the box. The $435,000 house never takes an evening of their time.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### PA.1.8 Exercise: your buy box template

Fill this in and save it as one page. Bring it to Sam's PA.2 and PA.3.

**Buy box**

Owner(s): ____________ Date written: ____________ Rewrite date (if changed): ____________

**A. Goal hierarchy** (rank 1 to 7; 1 wins conflicts)

| Rank | Goal | How it shapes the search |
|---|---|---|
| | Cash flow | |
| | Long-term ownership | |
| | Personal use (days per year: ___) | |
| | Tax planning | |
| | Portfolio growth | |
| | Desired involvement (hours per week: ___) | |
| | Safety (savings and income protected if the property underperforms) | |

**B. Constraints**

| Field | What to enter | Source or formula | Your entry |
|---|---|---|---|
| Liquid savings available | Total liquid, excluding retirement | Your statements; use the amount at your funding date | |
| Personal emergency savings floor | Months x essential monthly spending | 0.3 | |
| Property reserve floor | Lender months x PITIA, or your own higher target | Lender quote; 0.3 | |
| Other startup costs | Repairs, inspections, permits, launch months | Your 0.3 cash stack | |
| Max total cash in | Liquid savings - emergency floor - property reserve floor - other startup costs | The four lines above | |
| Max cash to close | The most you'll pay on closing day: down payment, closing costs, points | Your choice; it binds only if it's under max total cash in - furnishing | |
| Funding condition | Any gap still to close before an offer | 0.3 scorecard line 1 (full cash stack) | |
| Financing path and assumed terms | Loan type, down %, rate, points | Written quote when available | |
| Max drive time | Hours | Your choice | |
| Time available | Hours per week | 0.3 | |

**C. Price ceiling**

| Line | Formula | Your number |
|---|---|---|
| Cash to close as % of price | Down % + closing % + (points x loan %) | |
| Ceiling from cash-to-close cap | Max cash to close / line above | |
| Ceiling from total-cash-in cap | (Max total cash in - furnishing) / cash-to-close % | |
| Ceiling from downside rule | From the PA.4 model for each house you underwrite (leave blank until PA.4) | |
| **Price ceiling** | Lowest of the lines filled in; line 4 can lower it for a given house | |

**D. Property description**

| Field | Your entry |
|---|---|
| Market type and demand driver | |
| Location rule (inside the market) | |
| Property type (and HOA rule) | |
| Bedrooms / baths / sleeps | |
| Price range (floor to ceiling) | |
| Amenities wanted | |
| Amenities excluded | |
| Complexity limits (septic, well, road, age, pool, hot tub) | |

**E. Hard requirements** (every one must be met)

| # | Requirement | Evidence needed | Who confirms |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Legal STR use at the address, plus HOA | Official source URL and date; written answer if unclear (SR.1) | City/county office; attorney if unclear |
| 2 | Insurable at or under $____ a year | Written quote | Insurance agent |
| 3 | Lender fit | Written guideline or pre-qualification | Lender |
| 4 | Cash to close at or under $____; total cash in at or under $____ | Section C | You |
| 5 | Weak case no worse than $____ a year after debt service; base NOI / debt service at least ____ | PA.4 model at a real quote | You; lender for the quote |
| 6 | (Your own, from 0.3) | | |

**F. Pass rule** (write it in one or two sentences)

____________________________________________

**G. Pass log**

| Date | Address | Reason for pass (which requirement) | Would a price change fix it? |
|---|---|---|---|
| | | | |

### PA.1.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the city or county licensing office that unhosted STR use is allowed at each address that passes your other filters. Use the SR.1 checklist. If the answer is unclear, an STR attorney.

**Confirm with:** your insurance agent the premium and exclusions for STR use on each finalist. Do it before the diligence window, the period after your offer is accepted when you inspect and verify.

**Confirm with:** your lender that the property type, location, and planned use fit their loan, and the terms you've used in section C. Do it before you rely on your price ceiling.

**Confirm with:** your CPA before you put tax planning in your top three goals, so the ranking reflects your household's actual situation.

### PA.1.10 Next lesson

Take your buy box page through Sam's four demos, then back to me:

- Sam's PA.2, Choosing a Market for Ownership: Regulatory-Safe, Cash-Flowing Areas. Check whether each market allows unhosted STR use at all. Note which markets fit your drive time and price range.
- Sam's PA.3, Deal Sourcing: Building a Pipeline of 5-10 Qualified Properties. Use your pass rule as the filter, and keep the pass log going.
- Sam's PA.4, Reading a Full Underwriting Package: Revenue, Expenses, Cap Rate & Cash-on-Cash Return. Test your downside rule on a real listing. This is where you fill in line 4 of section C.
- Sam's PA.5, Cost Segregation & Year-One Depreciation, Explained. It covers the tax timing from 0.2 in detail. Keep tax planning where you ranked it.
- My PA.6, DSCR Loans 101: How They Qualify the Property, Not You. There, the financing line in your buy box becomes a written lender quote.

- [Airbnb Help Center, General info about hosting places to stay (article 1397)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1397): hosts should check local laws and HOA or co-op rules before hosting. Checked 2026-09-25 via web search restricted to airbnb.com.

They include the $115,000 and $145,000 cash caps, the $393,000 box ceiling, and the $392,000 downside ceiling for Cedar Ridge. They also include the -$6,000 and 1.20 rules and the $399,000 and $435,000 tests. So are the 1% tax scaling, location rules, the $3,500 insurance cap, and the $325,000 floor. <a id="lesson-PA.6-dscr-loans-101"></a>
## PA.6  DSCR Loans 101: How They Qualify the Property, Not You

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1: Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.6 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package (Sam records the course's live demos; PA.4 gives the revenue and expense inputs); 0.3 Is Ownership Right for You? A Capital & Readiness Checklist (reserves and readiness); PA.7 DSCR vs. Conventional Financing (the next comparison); PA.8 Offer Strategy & Negotiation for Investment Property (offer ceiling); PA.10 Entity & Tax Structure Setup (LLC decision); OP.11 Scaling Your Portfolio: Refinancing, HELOCs & Property #2 (refinance) |

### PA.6.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can read a DSCR (debt service coverage ratio) loan quote the way the lender reads it. You'll know which income the lender puts on top of the ratio and which payment goes underneath. You'll also see why a gross-based ratio above 1.0 can still leave you losing money. You'll leave with a written question list for two lenders and a table for comparing their written offers on the same house.

### PA.6.1 What the title means, and what it leaves out

The title, "How They Qualify the Property, Not You," is shorthand. It points at the main difference from a regular mortgage. A DSCR lender sizes the loan mostly on the property's rental income, not your pay stubs.

But the lender still looks at you. Kiavi is a lender that publishes its DSCR loan terms online. I use its pages as examples in this lesson because they're public. That isn't an endorsement. Kiavi's [complete guide to DSCR rental loans](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans) says the lender considers the borrower's credit score, the available down payment, and the property's coverage ratio. Expect a DSCR lender to check some mix of these:

- your credit score and credit history
- how much cash you're putting in, which sets your loan-to-value (LTV): the loan as a percent of the price. 25% down means 75% LTV
- your reserves after closing, if that lender requires them
- the property type, condition, and appraisal
- whether short-term rental use is legal at that address
- the entity you'll hold title in, and who signs a personal guarantee

So "qualifies the property" means the property's income replaces your salary (your W-2 income) in the math. It doesn't mean nobody asks about you. Kiavi's terms are one lender's terms. Other lenders publish different minimums, and the same lender can change its terms. Treat every lender page in this lesson as an example, then get your own written quote.

### PA.6.2 The ratio itself: income on top, payment underneath

DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio. In plain words: how many dollars of property income the lender counts for every dollar of loan payment.

The top number (the numerator) is some version of the property's income. The bottom number (the denominator) is some version of the payment. Every lender writes its own definition. Kiavi's material describes a common version: gross rent divided by PITIA, which is principal, interest, property taxes, insurance, and any HOA dues ([Kiavi DSCR guide](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans)). Kiavi's material also says lenders calculate it with different inputs. Some use gross rent. Some use a vacancy-adjusted figure, which is income cut down for the time the property sits empty. Some include items like flood insurance in the payment, while others leave them out.

That flexibility means the same house can produce very different ratios, depending on three choices the lender makes:

1. Which income: gross booking revenue, a discounted version of it, market rent from the appraiser, or net operating income (NOI, revenue minus operating costs).
2. Whose income estimate: your pro forma (your own projected numbers), the appraiser's opinion, a third-party data report, or actual booking history.
3. Which payment: principal and interest only, or full PITIA.

Kiavi's guide says a ratio of at least 1.1 is needed to prequalify (get an early, conditional yes) and describes 1.25 or higher as strong. Your lender's minimum may be different, and pricing often changes with the ratio. Ask for the definition in writing before you compare any two quotes.

### PA.6.3 Cedar Ridge: one house, three ratios

Maya and Chris are buying Cedar Ridge for $385,000 with 25% down. All figures below are assumptions from the Cedar Ridge course example. The hypothetical DSCR quote is 7.25%, 1 point, 30-year fixed.

Start with the payment underneath. The loan is 75% of $385,000, or $288,750. Monthly principal and interest (P&I) is $1,970. Any mortgage calculator gives that from the loan amount, rate, and term. Property tax ($3,850 a year) and STR insurance ($3,200 a year) total $7,050 a year, or $587.50 a month. Cedar Ridge has no HOA dues. That makes monthly PITIA $2,557, or $30,684 a year (12 x $2,557).

Now put three different incomes on top.

**Ratio 1: gross booking revenue / PITIA.** Base-case gross revenue is $74,275. That's 212 booked nights (about 58% occupancy) at a $300 average nightly rate (ADR), the average price per booked night. Those nights bring in $63,600. Add $10,675 of cleaning fees (61 stays at $175) and you get $74,275.

$74,275 / $30,684 = **2.42**

**Ratio 2: gross revenue discounted 20% / PITIA.** Some lenders haircut projected STR income. The 20% haircut here is an assumption, not any lender's published rule.

$74,275 x 0.80 = $59,420. Then $59,420 / $30,684 = **1.94**

**Ratio 3: NOI before property tax and insurance / PITIA.** Net operating income (NOI) is revenue after operating costs. Base-case operating costs are $45,066, so base-case NOI is $74,275 - $45,066 = $29,209. Those operating costs already include property tax and insurance. PITIA includes them too. So add them back to NOI, or they'd be subtracted twice: $29,209 + $3,850 + $3,200 = $36,259.

$36,259 / $30,684 = **1.18**

Same house, same loan, same year. The ratio runs from 1.18 to 2.42. The lender's written definition decides which one counts.

Now run the weak case. The course example's weak case drops ADR from $300 to $265 and occupancy from about 58% to 50%. Gross revenue falls to $57,330 and NOI to $18,162. Here are the three ratios on those inputs:

| Definition | Base case | Weak case |
|---|---:|---:|
| Gross revenue / PITIA | 2.42 | 1.87 |
| Gross revenue x 80% / PITIA | 1.94 | 1.49 |
| NOI before tax and insurance / PITIA | 1.18 | 0.82 |

All figures are assumptions from the Cedar Ridge example.

### PA.6.4 Why a lender ratio above 1.0 doesn't mean you make money

Look at the weak case on the gross definition: 1.87. That sounds comfortable. It says the house brings in almost twice its loan payment.

Now look at what Maya and Chris keep in the weak case. Weak-case NOI is $18,162. Annual principal and interest on the DSCR loan is $23,637. Tax and insurance are already subtracted in NOI, so cash flow is NOI minus principal and interest:

$18,162 - $23,637 = **-$5,475 a year**, about -$456 a month.

So a lender ratio of 1.87 and an owner losing $456 a month are both true at once.

Notice the NOI-based ratio doesn't have this problem. It's above 1.0 in the base case (1.18, with +$5,572 a year of cash flow) and below 1.0 in the weak case (0.82, with -$5,475). It adds tax and insurance to both sides. So it's above 1.0 exactly when NOI covers principal and interest. The gross ratios are the ones that can look healthy while you lose money.

A gross-revenue ratio ignores the platform fee, the cleaner, utilities, supplies, software, repairs, and the capital reserve. That's money you set aside for big replacements like a roof or HVAC. It isn't the same as the lender reserves in PA.6.7. An STR carries far more operating cost than a long-term rental where the tenant pays utilities. On Cedar Ridge, base-case operating costs are $45,066 on $74,275 of revenue. The gross ratio never sees that $45,066.

Even the base case is thin. Base-case cash flow is $29,209 - $23,637 = $5,572 a year, about $464 a month, before any reserves the lender requires you to hold. Both cash-flow figures, $5,572 and -$5,475, are pre-diligence; PA.9 adjusts them.

So use the lender's ratio for one question only: will this lender make this loan at this size? Use your own NOI and cash flow for the other question: should you buy this house? PA.4, Reading a Full Underwriting Package, builds that NOI line by line. Bring its operating-cost total to every lender conversation.

### PA.6.5 How the definition changes the loan size

The definition does more than pass or fail you. It can shrink the loan.

Say a lender uses the NOI-based definition (Ratio 3) and requires a minimum of 1.20 (assumption). The most PITIA it will accept is $36,259 / 1.20 = $30,216 a year. Take out $7,050 of tax and insurance and the most principal and interest it allows is $23,166 a year. At 7.25% over 30 years, that supports a loan of about **$282,989**. Any mortgage calculator, or a spreadsheet's PV function, turns a yearly payment like this into a loan amount.

Maya and Chris asked for $288,750. This lender would cut the loan by about $5,761, and they'd bring that much more cash to closing.

Now take the 80% gross definition with a 1.25 minimum (assumption). The most PITIA it accepts is $59,420 / 1.25 = $47,536. Take out $7,050 and it allows $40,486 a year of principal and interest. On paper, that supports roughly $494,600 of loan, far more than the house is worth. Say that lender's maximum LTV is 75% (assumption). Then its cap is $288,750, and the LTV limit sets the loan size, not the ratio.

So ask each lender for the maximum loan its definition supports on your numbers, not just the rate.

This example used Maya and Chris's own income estimate. The next section shows the lender may use a lower one, which would shrink the loan further.

### PA.6.6 How a lender verifies STR income with no history

Cedar Ridge has no booking history under Maya and Chris. Neither will most houses you buy to convert. So where does the lender's income number come from?

Lenders use a few sources, and each lender picks its own:

- The appraiser's market rent. Kiavi says it requires an appraisal on its rental loans. Its rental loan page says the qualifying rent is the lower of 110% of appraised market rent or a valid lease rent ([Kiavi rental loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/rental)). That structure fits a long-term lease. Ask how the lender applies it to a house with nightly bookings and no lease.
- Short-term rental revenue. Kiavi publishes that it finances short-term rentals in some markets and that borrowers there can qualify on nightly rental revenue ([Kiavi Arizona DSCR programs](https://www.kiavi.com/arizona-dscr-loan-programs)). That page doesn't say which data source or haircut it applies. Ask.
- Third-party revenue estimates. Some lenders accept a report from an STR data provider. Ask which provider, which comparable set, and whether they discount it.
- Actual history. On an existing STR, a lender may ask for 12 or 24 months of platform statements or tax returns. The seller's history may not transfer to you, and it may not reflect your pricing or costs.

The lender's number will rarely match your pro forma. It shouldn't. The lender is protecting a loan, so it will usually lean lower on income and ignore upside. When the lender's income is lower than yours, don't argue the loan up. Ask whether your pro forma is the one that's too high.

**Confirm with:** each lender, in writing, which income source it uses for a no-history STR purchase, any haircut, and whether the appraiser must be STR-experienced.

### PA.6.7 The other terms that decide the real cost

The rate gets all the attention. Six other terms decide what the loan actually costs and how much room you have later.

**Down payment and LTV.** Kiavi's guide says DSCR loans typically need a larger down payment than a mortgage on a home you live in. It's often 20% to 25% ([Kiavi DSCR guide](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans)). The figure depends on property, lender, and borrower. Maximum LTV can drop for lower credit scores, lower ratios, or STR use. Cedar Ridge assumes 25%, which is $96,250.

**Points.** A point is an upfront fee equal to 1% of the loan, usually paid to lower the rate. The [CFPB explains points and lender credits](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-should-i-use-lender-credits-and-points-also-called-discount-points-en-136/): points cost more upfront and less over time; credits do the reverse. On Cedar Ridge, 1 point is $288,750 x 1% = $2,888. Closing costs rise from $11,550 (3% of price) to $14,438.

**Prepayment penalty.** Many DSCR loans charge a fee if you pay the loan off early, including through a refinance or sale. A common shape is a step-down. Take a 3-2-1 structure (an example, not a standard). The penalty is 3% of the balance in year one, 2% in year two, 1% in year three, then nothing. Kiavi publishes 3-, 5-, and 7-year step-down options for its rental loans ([Kiavi rental loans for investors](https://www.kiavi.com/rental-loans-for-investors)). Other lenders use flat percentages, months of interest, or no penalty at a higher rate.

The course example assumes Maya and Chris refinance at month 24 to pull cash back out. OP.11, Scaling Your Portfolio: Refinancing, HELOCs & Property #2, covers how. The DSCR balance then is $282,951.

Suppose the loan carried a 3-2-1 schedule. This is a what-if; the Cedar Ridge quote doesn't state a penalty. A payoff in month 24 is still in year two:

$282,951 x 2% = **$5,659 penalty** (assumption: 3-2-1 schedule)

The planned refinance shows $15,725 of cash out. That's what's left after the new, larger loan pays off the old one and the refinance costs. Subtract the penalty and it drops to $10,066. Wait one more month, into year three, and the penalty falls to 1%. State law and loan type can limit prepayment penalties. Ask the lender how its penalty applies in your state.

**Reserves.** Some lenders require cash left in the bank after closing, usually measured in months of PITIA. Kiavi's DSCR page describes no cash reserve requirement ([Kiavi DSCR loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr)). Confirm that with the lender, since terms change. Other lenders do require reserves, so ask. Six months of PITIA on Cedar Ridge (assumption) is 6 x $2,557 = $15,342. Lesson 0.3, Is Ownership Right for You? A Capital & Readiness Checklist, covered why that cash is on top of your cash to close.

**Entity and guarantor.** Many DSCR lenders let you close in an LLC. Kiavi's site says LLC borrowing is available for its DSCR rental loans, with requirements that vary ([Kiavi DSCR loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr)). A lender may also ask the owners to sign a personal guarantee, which makes you personally liable if the LLC defaults. Ask who must guarantee, and whether title must be in the LLC before closing. PA.10, Entity & Tax Structure Setup, covers the entity decision itself.

**Permit and property eligibility.** A lender may refuse STR income where short-term rental use isn't allowed. It may also ask for proof of the permit. Some lenders exclude condos with rental restrictions, rural acreage, or unusual property types. Bring your STR compliance checklist from SR.1 (Regulatory compliance checklist, by state and city) to the first call.

### PA.6.8 Maya and Chris's liquidity after the DSCR loan

Put these terms against the buyers' actual cash. Maya and Chris have $210,000 in liquid savings (course example).

| Line | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Down payment (25%) | $96,250 |
| Closing costs incl. 1 point | $14,438 |
| Cash to close | $110,688 |
| Furnishing budget | $32,000 |
| Total cash in | $142,688 |
| Lender reserves, 6 months PITIA (assumption) | $15,342 |
| Savings left for everything else | $51,970 |

$210,000 - $142,688 - $15,342 = $51,970. The $15,342 of reserves stays in their account. But the lender expects it untouched, so it isn't spare cash. The remaining $51,970 still has to cover their personal emergency fund and a weak first season. The weak case loses $5,475 a year. PA.8, Offer Strategy & Negotiation for Investment Property, turns this table into their offer ceiling.

### PA.6.9 The questions you send to two lenders

Send the same written questions to at least two lenders, on the same house, on the same day if you can. Rates change daily, so quotes from different days can differ for reasons that have nothing to do with the lender.

1. What is your exact DSCR formula? Which income goes on top, and which payment goes underneath?
2. For a short-term rental with no history, which income source do you use? Do you discount it? By how much?
3. What minimum ratio do you require for this loan, and does the rate or maximum LTV change with the ratio?
4. What is the maximum loan you'll offer on this address with my numbers?
5. What credit score tier am I in, and how does it change the rate or LTV?
6. What are the rate, points, and all lender fees? Please send a written quote or Loan Estimate.
7. What prepayment penalty applies? Show the schedule by year.
8. How many months of reserves do you require after closing, and which accounts count?
9. Can I close in an LLC? Who must sign a personal guarantee?
10. What proof of legal STR use or a permit do you need, and when?
11. Does your appraiser need STR experience? Who orders the market-rent or revenue report?
12. How long is the rate lock, and what does an extension cost?

Three terms in that list need a quick definition. Your credit tier is the score band the lender prices you in. A Loan Estimate is the standard cost form lenders give on consumer mortgages; PA.6.10 explains why a DSCR lender may not use it. A rate lock holds your quoted rate for a set number of days while you close.

### PA.6.10 Comparing two written offers

Put the two written answers side by side. The illustration below shows the format. Lender A is the course example's DSCR quote. Lender B is invented for this exercise.

| Term | Lender A (course example) | Lender B (assumption) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Max loan on Cedar Ridge | $288,750 (75% LTV cap, assumption) | about $276,094 |
| Points | 1.0 | 0.0 |
| Prepayment | ask | 3-2-1 |
| Reserves | ask | 6 months PITIA |
| Guarantee required | ask | yes |
| Rate | 7.25% | 7.50% |
| DSCR definition | Gross / PITIA (assumption) | NOI-based / PITIA |
| Minimum ratio | ask | 1.20 |

All Lender B figures are assumptions. So are Lender A's definition and LTV cap. Lender A's loan is the amount Maya and Chris asked for, capped by LTV, not by the ratio. Lender B's maximum loan uses the NOI method from PA.6.5. It allows at most about $23,166 a year of principal and interest. At 7.50% over 30 years, that works out to about $276,094.

The 3-2-1 penalty in PA.6.7 was a what-if. Lender A's real prepayment schedule is still unknown, so its cell says "ask."

Read this table from the top down. First, does the lender make the loan you need at the size you need? Lender B's loan is about $12,656 short, so Maya and Chris would need that much more cash. Second, what's the full upfront cost, including points and fees? Third, what does it cost you to leave early? Rate comes after those. A lower rate with a longer penalty may cost more than a higher rate if you plan the year-2 refinance in OP.11. Kiavi, for example, publishes 5- and 7-year options.

The [CFPB's guide to comparing loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/) tells borrowers to request several Loan Estimates and focus on the fees that vary by lender. One catch for DSCR loans: federal rules treat credit to buy a rental you won't live in as business-purpose credit ([Regulation Z commentary to § 1026.3](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-3/)). So a DSCR lender may give you a term sheet instead of a Loan Estimate. Ask for every fee in writing either way.

### PA.6.11 Exercise: your DSCR lender worksheet

Fill this in for a property you're considering. If you don't have one yet, use a live listing, or rerun Cedar Ridge as practice. "Your number" is your own math. The Lender columns hold each lender's written answers. Leave a cell blank where a row doesn't apply. Use your PA.4 underwriting package for your revenue and operating-cost lines.

| Line | Your number | Lender 1 | Lender 2 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Purchase price | | | |
| Loan amount requested | | | |
| Monthly P&I | | | |
| Monthly tax + insurance + HOA | | | |
| Annual PITIA | | | |
| Lender's income figure (and source) | | | |
| Lender's DSCR (their formula) | | | |
| Minimum ratio required | | | |
| Max loan the lender will offer | | | |
| Your base-case NOI (PA.4) | | | |
| Your weak-case NOI | | | |
| Your cash flow, base (NOI minus P&I) | | | |
| Your cash flow, weak | | | |
| Points and lender fees ($) | | | |
| Prepayment schedule | | | |
| Reserves required ($) | | | |
| Guarantee / entity terms | | | |
| Permit proof required | | | |
| Date of quote, rate lock length | | | |

Decision rule: if the lender's ratio passes but your weak-case cash flow is negative, write down how you'll cover the shortfall and for how long. If you can't answer that, you're not ready to make the offer in PA.8.

### PA.6.12 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** each lender, the DSCR formula, income source for a no-history STR, minimum ratio, maximum loan, points, fees, reserves, and prepayment schedule, all in writing and dated.

**Confirm with:** the lender and your attorney, whether a prepayment penalty is enforceable in your state and how it applies to a refinance or sale.

**Confirm with:** your attorney, what a personal guarantee commits you to and whether title must sit in the LLC at closing.

**Confirm with:** the local licensing office, that STR use and any permit the lender asks for are available at this exact address (use your SR.1 checklist).

**Confirm with:** your CPA, how points and loan costs are treated for tax purposes on a rental.

### PA.6.13 Next lesson

You now have one financing path priced out. PA.7 puts that DSCR quote next to a conventional quote on the same house and the same buyers. Bring your filled worksheet, because the conventional side will ask for your income documents, not just the property's.

### PA.6 sources

- [Kiavi: The Complete Guide to DSCR Rental Property Loans](https://www.kiavi.com/the-complete-guide-to-dscr-rental-property-loans): borrower credit, down payment, and DSCR all considered; DSCR as gross rent / PITIA with lender variation; 1.1 to prequalify; 20-25% typical down payment. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: Rental Property Loans](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/rental): qualifying rent is the lower of 110% of appraised market rent and valid lease rent; appraisal required on rental loans; FICO and leverage as qualifiers. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: DSCR Loans for Real Estate Investors](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr): LLC borrowing available; no cash reserve requirement. Confirm both with the lender. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: Arizona DSCR loan programs](https://www.kiavi.com/arizona-dscr-loan-programs): Kiavi finances STRs in some markets and borrowers can qualify on nightly rental revenue. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [Kiavi: Rental Loans for Real Estate Investors](https://www.kiavi.com/rental-loans-for-investors): 3-, 5-, and 7-year step-down prepayment options. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (kiavi.com).
- [CFPB: points and lender credits](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/how-should-i-use-lender-credits-and-points-also-called-discount-points-en-136/): what a point is and the trade-off. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- [CFPB: Compare and negotiate your loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/): request several Loan Estimates, rates change daily, compare fees that vary. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- [CFPB: Regulation Z commentary, § 1026.3 exempt transactions](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-3/): credit to acquire non-owner-occupied rental property is deemed business purpose. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (consumerfinance.gov).
- Cedar Ridge course example numbers: reference/recurring-examples.md (Example B1, B2, B4, B5, B7). Derived figures (loan sizes, penalty, liquidity) computed 2026-09-25.

<a id="lesson-PA.7-dscr-vs-conventional"></a>
## PA.7  DSCR vs. Conventional Financing: Which Path Fits Your Deal

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 1: Property Acquisition |
| Code | PA.7 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | PA.6 DSCR Loans 101 (how DSCR loans are sized and what to ask a DSCR lender); Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package (Sam runs the course's live demos; PA.4 is where your NOI comes from); PA.8 (setting your offer ceiling); PA.10 (LLC vs. personal names on title); OP.11 (refinancing and buying property #2) |

### PA.7.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can put a DSCR quote and a conventional quote side by side for the same house and the same buyer. You'll see the difference in cash to close, monthly payment, five-year cost, paperwork, and flexibility. You'll use a decision tree to pick which route to pursue first, and you'll know that the lender makes the final eligibility call.

### PA.7.1 Two routes, one house

A DSCR loan sizes the loan mostly on the property's income. DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio: the property's income divided by its loan payment. A ratio of 1.0 means the income just covers the payment. PA.6 covers how lenders calculate it. A conventional loan sizes it mostly on you: your documented income, your existing debts, your credit, and your assets. The property still matters, but your household is the main borrower.

That one difference changes almost everything else. It changes which documents you gather and how long underwriting takes. It changes whether you can close in an LLC, and what happens to your borrowing capacity for the next property.

Here's the side-by-side for Cedar Ridge, a $385,000 house. All figures are assumptions from the course's running Cedar Ridge example. Both quotes are hypothetical. DSCR is at 7.25% with 1 point (1% of the loan, paid at closing). Conventional is at 6.875% with no points. Both are 30-year fixed with 25% down, so the comparison stays equal. A conventional investment loan can allow less down; see PA.7.2. Total cash in adds the $32,000 furnishing budget. The cash flow rows are pre-diligence figures, and PA.9 revisits them.

| | DSCR quote | Conventional quote |
|---|---:|---:|
| Loan amount | $288,750 | $288,750 |
| Down payment | $96,250 | $96,250 |
| Monthly P&I | $1,970 | $1,897 |
| Monthly PITIA | $2,557 | $2,484 |
| Annual debt service (P&I) | $23,637 | $22,763 |
| Closing costs incl. points | $14,438 | $11,550 |
| Cash to close | $110,688 | $107,800 |
| Total cash in (with furnishing) | $142,688 | $139,800 |
| Principal paid in year 1 | $2,795 | $3,005 |
| Annual cash flow, base case | $5,572 | $6,446 |
| Annual cash flow, weak case | -$5,475 | -$4,601 |
| Annual cash flow, base with 18% co-host (if a full-service local co-host takes 18% of booking revenue; see OP.7) | -$7,798 | -$6,924 |
| Cash-on-cash, base case | 3.9% | 4.6% |

A few terms from the table. P&I is principal and interest. PITIA is principal, interest, taxes, insurance, and association dues: the full monthly payment. Cedar Ridge has no HOA, so there are no dues in its PITIA. Annual debt service is one year of P&I. NOI (net operating income) is rental revenue minus operating costs, before the loan payment. Taxes and insurance already sit inside NOI, so annual cash flow here is NOI minus P&I. Cash-on-cash is first-year cash flow divided by total cash in.

Reserves are not in these totals. Either lender may require months of PITIA on top of cash to close.

Two things stand out. The conventional quote wins on every line that differs. And neither quote fixes the weak case. The base case assumes a $300 average nightly rate (ADR) and 58% occupancy (the share of nights booked). When ADR drops to $265 and occupancy to 50%, both loans lose money. The financing choice only changes the size of the loss.

The co-host row is a warning too. Paying a full-service co-host 18% turns even the base case negative on both loans. If you plan to hand the house to a co-host, run that case before you buy. PA.8 sets the offer ceiling with the weak case in view, and OP.7 covers the staffing choice.

### PA.7.2 How a conventional lender qualifies you

On a conventional loan, the lender underwrites your household. I'll use Fannie Mae's Selling Guide as the example because it's public. Lenders sell many loans to buyers like Fannie Mae, and the loan buyer sets the rules. So your loan may follow Fannie Mae, the guide of another loan buyer, or a lender's own portfolio rules. Ask which guide applies.

Four things carry most of the weight.

The first is your debt-to-income ratio (DTI): your monthly debt payments divided by your gross monthly income. Fannie Mae's [DTI section, B3-6-02](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-6-02/debt-income-ratios) sets 36% as the maximum for manually underwritten loans. Manual means a human underwriter applies the rules by hand, rather than Fannie Mae's automated system. It allows up to 45% when the borrower meets the credit score and reserve requirements in the Eligibility Matrix. For loans run through Desktop Underwriter (DU), Fannie Mae's automated system, the maximum is 50%. Ask your lender which route your file will take.

The second is documented income. W-2 wages, tax returns, and pay stubs. Self-employment income usually needs more paperwork and a longer history. The lender decides which income counts and how it's averaged.

The third is the occupancy type. Fannie Mae classifies a property as a principal residence, a second home, or an investment property ([B2-1.1-01 Occupancy Types](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b2-1.1-01/occupancy-types)). Investment property loans carry an extra price adjustment (an LLPA, or loan-level price adjustment), which is one reason investment rates run higher. For a one-unit investment property purchase, Fannie Mae's [Eligibility Matrix](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/media/20786/display) shows a maximum LTV of 85%. LTV (loan-to-value) is the loan as a share of the price, so 85% means at least 15% down. Credit score and other factors can lower that 85% maximum, which means more than 15% down.

Maya and Chris plan about 10 personal-use days a year at Cedar Ridge. Personal use is one of the things that separates a second home from an investment property, which is why the number matters. Don't guess the occupancy type from that number. Tell the lender your real plan and let the lender classify it. Fannie Mae's [general rental income section, B3-3.8-01](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-01/rental-income) says rental income from a principal residence or second home generally can't be used to qualify. It lists narrow exceptions. So the occupancy type also decides whether short-term rental (STR) income is even on the table.

The fourth is reserves and your other properties. Reserves are savings the lender wants you to still have after closing. Fannie Mae's [reserve rules, B3-4.1-01](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements) add reserve requirements in one situation. The borrower has multiple financed properties, and the new loan is on a second home or investment property. The requirement grows with the number of financed properties. Fannie Mae also sets rules on how many financed properties one borrower can have ([B2-2-03](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b2-2-03/multiple-financed-properties-same-borrower)). Maya and Chris would have two: their home and Cedar Ridge.

### PA.7.3 Maya and Chris's DTI, before any STR income

Here's a rough DTI check, and it's only an illustration. The lender runs the real one.

Maya and Chris earn $185,000 a year combined (from the Cedar Ridge example). That's $185,000 / 12 = $15,417 a month.

I need two numbers the Cedar Ridge example doesn't give. Say their primary home payment (principal, interest, tax, insurance) is $2,400 a month (assumption). Car and card payments are $600 a month (assumption).

Today: ($2,400 + $600) / $15,417 = **19.5%**

Add the full Cedar Ridge conventional PITIA of $2,484 and count zero rental income:

($2,400 + $600 + $2,484) / $15,417 = $5,484 / $15,417 = **35.6%**

At 35.6%, they'd sit just under the 36% manual-underwriting limit, even with no income credit for the STR. They'd be well under the 45% and 50% limits. Their real DTI depends on their actual debts. The limit depends on credit, reserves, and which underwriting system the lender uses. So treat this as a screening check.

This matters for the decision tree later. Through DU, or with the credit and reserves for 45%, the STR income question may not decide their loan. On a manual file, 35.6% leaves only 0.4 points of room. That's what "near the limit" means in the decision tree, and it's why Maya and Chris also take one Branch C step. For a buyer already over the limit, the STR income question can decide the loan.

### PA.7.4 Fannie Mae's new short-term rental income guidance

You'll hear people say conventional loans can't use Airbnb income. Don't repeat that. It's out of date.

On September 2, 2026, Fannie Mae published [Announcement SEL-2026-08](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/news-events/announcement-sel-2026-08-selling-guide-updates) ([PDF](https://singlefamily.fanniemae.com/media/48051/display)). It restructured the rental income chapter and added a section titled [B3-3.8-03, Rental Income from the Subject Property: Short-Term Rental](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-3.8-03/rental-income-subject-property-short-term-rental). The announcement says it introduces new requirements for using short-term rental income, including alternative documentation options.

As of September 2026, the announcement says lenders are encouraged to put the changes in place immediately. Lenders must apply them to all loans with application dates on or after November 1, 2026. So on a loan application dated before November 1, 2026, the answer depends on whether your lender has already adopted the new section. Ask the loan officer directly. From November 1, 2026 on, the question is whether your transaction fits the section.

The exact conditions in B3-3.8-03 were not confirmed for this lesson. It may allow documented STR income on certain one-unit investment properties. Your lender needs to confirm which occupancy types it covers, and whether it covers purchases, refinances, or both. The lender also needs to confirm the operating history it requires and which documents it accepts. Those conditions decide whether Cedar Ridge qualifies. Here's the conditional version:

- If the lender confirms Cedar Ridge's transaction fits B3-3.8-03, it may count documented STR income under that section's calculation rules.
- If the property is classified as a second home, rental income generally can't be used to qualify (B3-3.8-01).
- If the transaction doesn't fit the section, the lender may still approve the loan on your W-2 income alone. The DTI check above suggests that's possible for Maya and Chris.

**Confirm with:** the lender, whether B3-3.8-03 applies to this transaction and application date, and exactly which STR documents it will accept. The full lender list is in PA.7.11.

### PA.7.5 Five-year cost, not just monthly payment

The monthly payments differ by $73. That looks small. Over five years it adds up, and the points add more on day one.

Here are both loans over 60 months. All figures are assumptions from the two Cedar Ridge quotes.

| Over 5 years | DSCR | Conventional | Difference |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Points paid at closing | $2,888 | $0 | $2,888 |
| Other closing costs (3% of price) | $11,550 | $11,550 | $0 |
| Total P&I paid | $118,187 | $113,813 | $4,374 |
| Interest paid | $101,956 | $96,502 | $5,454 |
| Principal paid | $16,231 | $17,311 | -$1,080 |
| Loan balance at month 60 | $272,519 | $271,439 | $1,080 |
| Interest + points + closing costs | $116,394 | $108,052 | **$8,342** |

The DSCR loan costs Maya and Chris about $8,342 more over five years. That's $5,454 of extra interest plus $2,888 of points. They'd also owe $1,080 more at the end. That isn't an extra cost on top. $4,374 more in payments plus $1,080 more owed at payoff equals the $5,454 of extra interest. Don't count it twice.

Cash flow tells the same story from the operating side. Holding revenue flat for five years (assumption):

- Base case: DSCR $5,572 x 5 = $27,860. Conventional $6,446 x 5 = $32,230.
- Weak case: DSCR -$5,475 x 5 = -$27,375. Conventional -$4,601 x 5 = -$23,005.

The $4,370 base-case gap ($32,230 - $27,860) is the same payment difference seen from the operating side. Don't add it to the $8,342.

Now add the exit. In the Cedar Ridge example, Maya and Chris sell at the end of year 5. Say the DSCR loan has a 5-4-3-2-1 step-down penalty (assumption; an example structure, not a standard). That's 5% of the balance if you pay off in year 1 and 4% in year 2. It steps down to 1% in year 5. A payoff in month 60 is still in year five, so the penalty is 1%:

$272,519 x 1% = **$2,725**

That's on top of the $8,342, so about $11,067 in total if this penalty applies.

With a 3-2-1 schedule (3% in year 1 down to 1% in year 3; assumption), there'd be no penalty at year five. Ask the conventional lender to confirm in writing whether any prepayment penalty applies to its loan.

### PA.7.6 Comparing the written quotes

The CFPB's [guide to comparing loan offers](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/) tells you to request several Loan Estimates and focus on the fees that vary by lender. It also warns that rates change daily, so quotes issued on different days may differ for market reasons. Get both quotes on the same day if you can.

Compare these lines on each written quote:

1. Interest rate, and whether it's locked, for how long.
2. APR where available. The CFPB explains that the [APR includes the rate plus points and certain other charges](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-the-difference-between-a-mortgage-interest-rate-and-an-apr-en-135/), so it's usually higher than the rate. It helps compare loans with different rates and fees.
3. Points and lender credits, in dollars.
4. Estimated cash to close. The CFPB's [Loan Estimate explainer](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/loan-estimate/) says it includes your down payment and closing costs, minus your deposit and any seller credits.
5. Monthly payment, both principal and interest and full PITIA.
6. Reserves required after closing, in dollars.
7. Prepayment penalty, year by year.
8. What the lender will require at refinance. Ask how long you must own the property first (seasoning). Ask the maximum loan-to-value on a cash-out refinance, where the new loan is larger and you take the difference in cash. And ask how it will count STR income then.

One wrinkle. Federal rules treat credit to buy a rental property you won't occupy as business-purpose credit ([Regulation Z commentary to § 1026.3](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/interp-3/)). That can mean a lender isn't required to give you a Loan Estimate on this kind of loan. Some lenders will anyway. Others send a term sheet, a short summary of the loan's rate, fees, and terms. Either way, ask for every rate, fee, and penalty in writing, in the same layout, so the comparison is fair. A DSCR term sheet may not show an APR. That's why the five-year table above matters.

### PA.7.7 Flexibility and downside

Price isn't the only difference. Here's what each route tends to give and take. Confirm every line with the actual lender.

| Question | DSCR (typical, varies by lender) | Conventional (typical, varies by lender) |
|---|---|---|
| Whose income qualifies? | Mostly the property's | Mostly yours, plus rental income where eligible |
| Close in an LLC? | Often allowed (one lender's example: [Kiavi](https://www.kiavi.com/loans/dscr)) | Ask; often in personal names |
| Paperwork | Shorter: no tax returns at Kiavi, per its site | Full income and asset documentation |
| Effect on your DTI now | Not used to qualify this loan | Central to approval |
| Effect on the next loan | A future conventional lender will count this loan under its own rules | Also counted under the future lender's rules |
| Prepayment penalty | Common | Ask |
| Rate and cost | Usually higher | Usually lower for strong borrowers |
| Reserves | Lender-specific | Rise with number of financed properties (B3-4.1-01) |

Fannie Mae's [rules on other real estate owned, B3-6-06](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-6-06/qualifying-impact-other-real-estate-owned) govern how a future conventional lender counts Cedar Ridge. That matters when Maya and Chris apply for property two. Keep clean records from day one. That means booking statements and the loan payment history. It also means the Schedule E your CPA files (the tax-return form that reports rental income and expenses). OP.11 picks this up.

### PA.7.8 Decision tree

Use this tree to decide which lender to call first. It is a way to order your calls. The lender makes the actual eligibility decision.

**Branch A: buying your first rental, with documented W-2 income.**
1. Is your DTI, with the full new payment and no rental income, under the lender's limit with some room to spare? If yes, get a conventional quote first. If you're over the limit or within a point or two of it, also follow Branch C. Your loan may depend on how the lender counts STR income.
2. Will the lender classify the property as an investment property? If yes, go to step 3. If no, ask what that means for rental income and occupancy rules.
3. Can you close the way you need to (in an LLC now, if you need one) on the conventional loan? If yes, go to step 4. If no, get a DSCR quote.
4. Is the conventional approval in writing? Until it is, keep a DSCR quote live as your backup.

**Branch B: refinance of an STR you already own.**
1. Do you have 12 months or more of platform statements and a filed Schedule E? Ask the conventional lender whether B3-3.8-03 or its own rules would count that income.
2. Does your current loan have a prepayment penalty? Price it in dollars before you compare.
3. Will the refinance leave the property's weak-case cash flow at or above the minimum you set for yourself? PA.8 walks through setting that weak-case rule. If not, the refinance is making the property riskier.
4. Compare the conventional refinance with a DSCR refinance on the same day, same loan amount.

**Branch C: complex or thin income documentation.**
This covers self-employed borrowers with a short history, recent job changes, or large write-offs. It also covers any DTI over the limit or within a point or two of it.
1. Ask a conventional lender for a pre-underwrite of your income (an early underwriter review of your income documents) before assuming you don't qualify.
2. If your documented income won't carry the payment, a DSCR loan may fit. Expect a higher cost and plan for it in PA.8.
3. Check that the property's weak-case cash flow works on DSCR pricing. The DSCR route removes the income test. It doesn't remove the risk.

### PA.7.9 Worked example: which path for Maya and Chris

Run Cedar Ridge through Branch A.

Step 1. DTI with the full conventional PITIA and no STR income: 35.6% (illustration, assumptions). That's well under the 45% and 50% limits, so they get a conventional quote first. On a manual-underwriting file, though, it's only 0.4 points under 36%. So they also ask the lender for a pre-underwrite (Branch C, step 1) before relying on it.

Step 2. Occupancy type. They plan about 10 personal-use days a year and rent the rest. They tell the lender that plan and ask for the classification in writing. This lesson assumes investment property.

Step 3. LLC. They'd like liability separation, but the lender's rules and PA.10's entity work aren't done yet. They ask the conventional lender whether an LLC can hold title and note the answer.

Step 4. Cost. Conventional saves $2,888 at closing and about $8,342 over five years (computed above). Add up to $2,725 more if the DSCR loan carries a 5-4-3-2-1 penalty. Base-case cash flow is $874 a year better: $6,446 - $5,572.

Step 5. Backup. They keep the DSCR quote live. If the conventional underwriting stalls on occupancy, reserves, or documentation, they switch without losing the house.

Their decision, written down: pursue conventional first and keep DSCR as the backup. Use the DSCR numbers in PA.8 until the conventional approval is in writing. That last choice is deliberate. You set your offer ceiling on the more expensive loan you might end up with.

### PA.7.10 Exercise: your financing comparison

Fill in one column per written quote. Use your PA.6 worksheet for the DSCR side.

No property or quotes yet? Practice with Cedar Ridge. Fill Quote 1 with the DSCR numbers and Quote 2 with the conventional numbers from PA.7.1 and PA.7.5.

For five-year interest on your own quotes, ask each lender for an amortization schedule. That's the month-by-month split of each payment into interest and principal. Add up the interest for months 1 to 60.

| Line | Quote 1: ______ | Quote 2: ______ |
|---|---:|---:|
| Lender, loan type, date of quote | | |
| Rate / APR (if shown) | | |
| Points ($) and lender credits ($) | | |
| Loan amount and LTV | | |
| Monthly P&I / monthly PITIA | | |
| Estimated cash to close | | |
| Reserves required ($) | | |
| Prepayment penalty by year | | |
| Occupancy type the lender assigned | | |
| Rental income counted? Which method? | | |
| Your DTI per the lender | | |
| Title: personal names or LLC? | | |
| 5-year interest + points + closing costs | | |
| Base-case annual cash flow (your NOI from Sam's PA.4 package, minus P&I) | | |
| Weak-case annual cash flow | | |
| Refinance terms the lender would expect | | |

Then answer in one line each:

1. Which branch of the decision tree are you on?
2. Which quote costs less over your expected hold?
3. Which quote keeps weak-case cash flow closer to breakeven?
4. What would make you switch to the backup quote?

### PA.7.11 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** the conventional lender, the occupancy classification, whether the loan follows Fannie Mae's guide, whether B3-3.8-03 applies to this transaction and application date, your DTI, the reserves required for all financed properties, and whether an LLC can hold title.

**Confirm with:** the DSCR lender, the items in PA.6's question list, especially the prepayment schedule and maximum loan.

**Confirm with:** your CPA, how you'll document STR income on your tax return so a future lender can use it. Also ask how the points you pay are treated on your taxes.

**Confirm with:** your attorney, what signing in personal names versus an LLC means for liability and title (PA.10).

### PA.7.12 Next lesson

You've picked a financing route and a backup. PA.8 uses the more expensive of the two quotes to set Maya and Chris's walk-away price before they negotiate. Bring your comparison table and Sam's PA.4 underwriting package.

URL brief-provided; section title confirmed via WebSearch (selling-guide.fanniemae.com) 2026-09-25; full text not readable (fetch blocked).
