<a id="lesson-OP.9-guest-experience-review-strategy"></a>
## OP.9  Guest Experience & Review Strategy in the AI Ranking Era

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 2, Operations launch |
| Code | OP.9 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's OP.1 Listing Creation, OP.2 2026 Listing Optimization: Structured Data & the Algorithm, OP.3 direct booking, OP.4 tech stack, OP.5 Dynamic Pricing (channel setup and pricing tools); OP.6 room inventory; OP.7 role map; OP.8 SOPs; retained 9.4 How to Avoid Parties & Smoking; Sam's OP.10 The 30-Day and 90-Day Performance Review (uses the tracker built here) |

### OP.9.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can tell what Airbnb says affects search apart from claims about "the algorithm." You'll map each published factor to something you control at your house. You'll set up guest checkpoints from booking to after checkout. You'll write a review request that follows Airbnb's review policy, and a process for answering criticism. And you'll start a monthly tracker that Sam's OP.10 review uses.

Who's who in this lesson. The example house is Cedar Ridge, a hypothetical lake rental owned by Maya and Chris. Maya answers guest messages, so she's the "message owner" in the tables. Chris is her backup, and he gets the repair quotes. When a table says "Owners," it means Maya and Chris as the property owners. Sam is the other instructor in this course. He teaches the live demo lessons, including listing setup (OP.1 and OP.2) and pricing (OP.5).

### OP.9.1 What Airbnb actually publishes about search

The title of this lesson says "AI ranking era," so let's be exact about what anyone knows. Airbnb publishes an explanation of [how search results work](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/39). That page is the source for everything this lesson says about ranking. I'm not promising a rank boost from any AI trick, keyword pattern, or structured-data method that Airbnb doesn't document. If someone sells you one, ask them for the Airbnb page that supports it.

As for the "AI ranking era," Airbnb doesn't publish whether or how AI changes its ranking. This lesson sticks to what Airbnb does publish, and to the guest experience that shows up in reviews.

Airbnb's search page (checked September 2026; see Sources) names four main factors:

- Quality, popularity, price, and location heavily influence how a listing appears in search.
- Quality looks at listing content like photos and videos, guest ratings and reviews, communication between hosts and guests, and listing characteristics.
- Popularity looks at how guests engage with a listing: how often they save it to a wishlist, book it, and message the host.
- Price considers total price and how it compares with other listings in the area. Listings priced below comparable listings with similar characteristics tend to rank higher.
- Location matters a lot. Listings in places guests like to stay tend to rank higher.

The page adds two more points:

- The system encourages variety, so guests see different hosts, characteristics, and prices.
- Availability matters. The more dates a listing is open, the more likely it shows up. More flexibility on length of stay also helps a listing fit more trips.

Notice what's missing. The page doesn't give weights, and it doesn't say which features count more this year than last. It doesn't mention any special field that AI reads. Sam's OP.2 lesson covers listing optimization and structured data in detail. This lesson covers the operating side: the parts guests experience and then write about.

Several of those factors are set before a guest arrives. Location was fixed when Maya and Chris bought the house. Price is Sam's OP.5 territory. Ranking isn't the only goal of price, and OP.5 covers that trade-off. So don't cut your price just for search. Popularity grows from the others: a clear first photo and title, an accurate total price, and fast replies (Sam's OP.1 and OP.2). Day to day, you control quality: accurate content, clean stays, clear and fast communication, and good reviews. You also control availability: open dates, and stay lengths that fit your short-term rental permit.

### OP.9.2 Airbnb's ground rules set the floor

Airbnb's [ground rules for home hosts](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2895) focus on four things:

1. Accuracy. The listing should describe the home and the amenities available from check-in to checkout. Change an accepted booking's details only with the guest's consent.
2. Reservation commitment. Honor accepted reservations and provide a reliable check-in. Don't cancel confirmed reservations except for valid reasons outside your control.
3. Communication. The host or co-host (someone who helps run the listing) should be available to respond to inquiries and unexpected issues before and during stays.
4. Cleanliness. Clean between every stay to a high standard.

The same page says Airbnb enforces these rules. Actions can include education, warnings, and requiring hosts to remove content that misrepresents the listing. These four rules cover the same ground guests tend to write about in reviews. So the ground rules are the minimum, and reviews measure how far above it you land.

### OP.9.3 Mapping each factor to something you control

This table maps what Airbnb publishes to what the Cedar Ridge team does.

| Published factor or rule | What you control | Where it's covered, or who does it |
|---|---|---|
| Quality: photos and listing characteristics | Photos match the house today; every amenity listed is present and working; sleeping arrangement exact | OP.6 room inventory; Sam's OP.1 |
| Quality: ratings and reviews | Clean, accurate, well-communicated stays; neutral review requests; fixes for repeat complaints | OP.8 SOPs; this lesson |
| Quality: host-guest communication | Named message owner; response target; a person checks automated messages | OP.7 role map; OP.8 message sequence |
| Popularity: saves, bookings, messages | Clear first photo and title, accurate total price, fast answers to inquiries | Sam's OP.1 and OP.2; message SOP |
| Price: total price vs. comparables | Nightly rate, cleaning fee, and minimum stay together; pricing review | Sam's OP.5 |
| Availability and stay flexibility | Calendar open far enough ahead; minimum stays that fit demand and your permit | Sam's OP.5; permit conditions |
| Ground rule: accuracy | Update the listing the day something changes (grill broken, bunk replaced) | Message owner |
| Ground rule: reservation commitment | No host cancellations except for safety or reasons outside your control | Owners |
| Ground rule: cleanliness | Cleaner photographs every room after cleaning; a missed clean gets escalated | OP.8 |

Accuracy runs both ways. Airbnb's resource center notes that guests can [filter by amenities](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/the-amenities-guests-want-25). List what you have, so filtered searches can find you. Remove what you don't have, the same day it breaks.

### OP.9.4 Guest checkpoints: before, during, after

A guest journey is the sequence of moments where a guest decides whether this stay is going well. Plan a checkpoint at each one: a moment where the team checks something rather than waiting for a complaint.

| Stage | Checkpoint | What you check | Who |
|---|---|---|---|
| Booking | Guest count and purpose | Party size matches the listing; no sign of an event | Message owner |
| 7 days out | Trip prep sent | Directions and seasonal notes current | Message owner |
| Day before | Check-in details | Correct code; house ready per photo review | Message owner |
| Arrival, same evening | "Anything missing?" | Guest got in; first impression; quick fixes | Message owner |
| Mid-stay (4+ nights) | Optional check | Supplies, questions | Message owner |
| Checkout day | Checkout steps sent | Instructions match the cleaner's needs | Message owner |
| After checkout, within 24 hours | Cleaner's walk-through notes | Damage, left items, anything guests struggled with | Cleaner, then owners |
| After checkout, day 2-3 | Thank-you and review request | Neutral wording (see OP.9.5) | Message owner |
| Weekly | Friction log review | Recurring questions and complaints | Owners |

Two gaps to plan for. If a booking comes in less than 7 days out, send the trip-prep message at booking. If you see signs of an event at booking, follow the retained lesson 9.4 How to Avoid Parties & Smoking.

The arrival-evening message matters most. Problems caught that night get fixed during the stay. Problems found after checkout go straight into a review.

The friction log is the simplest tool on the list. Every time a guest asks a question, mark it. "Where's the water shutoff?" is harmless once. By the second time, it goes on the fix list, and the house manual needs a line. "How do the bunk lights work?" asked twice means the lights need a label. Many negative reviews start as a repeated question nobody fixed.

### OP.9.5 Asking for reviews within the rules

After checkout, Airbnb prompts both guest and host to leave a review. According to Airbnb's review pages, both have 14 days after checkout to submit one ([reviews for homes](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/13)).

Airbnb's policy on [authentic and trustworthy reviews](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2673) sets the limits on how you ask:

- Hosts and guests may not coordinate with, manipulate, extort, incentivize, or pressure another person to influence a review.
- Reviews may not be given or withheld in exchange for something of value. That includes a discount, a refund, a reciprocal positive review, or a promise not to take negative action.
- Neither side may threaten a negative review to get unwarranted compensation, a refund, or another incentive.

So the review request has to be neutral. It invites a review and asks for honest feedback. It doesn't offer anything, hint at a trade, or tell the guest what rating to give. I'd go a step further than the policy text: don't ask for "five stars" at all. Naming the rating reads as pressure, and the policy prohibits pressure.

A compliant request, sent two or three days after checkout. That gap gives the cleaner's notes time to come in, so you know about any problem before you ask:

> Hi [first name], thanks for staying at Cedar Ridge. If you have a few minutes, we'd appreciate an honest review on Airbnb. If anything wasn't right, please tell us directly too, so we can fix it for the next guests.

Private feedback is in addition to the review, never instead of it. The request asks for both.

Things never to write or say:

- "Leave us 5 stars and we'll give you a discount next time." (Incentive.)
- "We'll return your deposit once you've left a review." (Trade.)
- "We gave you a great review; we hope you'll do the same." (Reciprocal pressure.)
- Any goodwill gesture tied to review timing, such as a credit in the same message as the review request. In OP.8, Maya's $100 internet credit went out during the stay, with no mention of reviews. Keep it that way.

The host's own review of the guest follows the same rules. Write it on the facts of the stay. Never hold it back to influence what the guest writes.

### OP.9.6 Responding to criticism

Airbnb lets hosts post a [public response](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/32) to a published review, as long as the response follows the reviews policy. The response appears on the listing with the review, so you're writing for future guests, not arguing with the past one.

A process that holds up:

1. Wait a day (house rule). Don't reply while you're annoyed.
2. Check the facts. Pull the turnover photos, the message thread, and the friction log for that stay.
3. Sort the complaint: fair and fixable, fair but a known trade-off, or factually wrong. Examples of each follow below.
4. Fix what you can before you respond. Never claim a fix that isn't done.
5. Respond briefly. Thank them, acknowledge the specific point, and state the fix or the fact. Keep guest details private. Never bring up something the guest did unless it's needed to correct a factual claim.

Here's how each kind of complaint plays out. These are examples, not Cedar Ridge reviews.

- Fair and fixable: the hot bunk room in the worked example. Respond with what you've fixed.
- Fair but a known trade-off: say a guest writes that the house is far from town. Thank them, and point to where the listing already says so.
- Factually wrong: say a guest writes that an amenity wasn't listed when it was. State the fact calmly. Dispute only if the review also breaks the policy.

Treat fair, fixable criticism as free inspection data.

Save disputes for policy violations. Airbnb says a published review stays up unless its author asks for removal within 30 days or it violates the policy. Other circumstances can also affect it. Hosts can [dispute a review](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3582) that violates the policy, but a request doesn't guarantee removal. A retaliatory or extortion-based review may qualify. A three-star review that says the bunk room was hot doesn't. Don't dispute every negative review. Disputing fair criticism wastes time, and it doesn't fix the house.

<!-- Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one. -->

### OP.9.7 The metrics tracker

Track the things you can act on, monthly. Each metric needs a definition, a source, a target, and an owner. The targets below are house rules for Cedar Ridge (assumptions), not Airbnb thresholds. Anything marked "house rule" in this lesson is a Cedar Ridge choice, not an Airbnb requirement. Set your own.

| Metric | Definition | Source | Target (house rule) | Who |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| First response time | Minutes from a guest's first message to a reply from a person, or from a message template the message owner has already reviewed (OP.8) | Platform inbox or property management software (PMS) | Median under 15 minutes, 7 a.m. to 10 p.m.; every reply in those hours under 60 minutes. After 10 p.m., an auto-reply gives the after-hours number | Message owner |
| Complaint reasons | Count by category: cleanliness, accuracy, check-in, amenities, noise, communication | Friction log, reviews, messages | Any category seen twice in a quarter has a fix, an owner, and a date | Owners |
| Cleanliness misses | Turnovers failing photo review, plus guest reports of cleaning problems | OP.8 records | Zero; any miss gets a root-cause note | Owners, cleaner |
| Host cancellations | Confirmed reservations the host cancelled, with reason | Platform | Zero outside safety events | Owners |
| Rating distribution | Count of 5, 4, 3, 2, 1-star overall ratings, not just the average | Reviews | Read every review below 5 stars | Owners |
| Review rate | Reviews received / completed stays | Platform | Record only; no target, because pushing it risks the no-pressure rule | Owners |
| Repeat-stay signals | Returning guests, "we'll be back" mentions, direct-booking inquiries from past guests | Messages, reviews, Sam's OP.3 channel | Record and thank; never trade for reviews | Message owner |

Rating distribution matters more than the average. An average of 4.6 can hide one 3-star review that describes a real problem. Read the low ones first. The two review thresholds work together. Read every review below five stars. Any review below four stars also gets a check of the turnover photos and message thread within 48 hours (house rule).

### OP.9.8 Worked example: Cedar Ridge's first 90 days

The base case expects 61 stays a year, about 5.1 a month (61 / 12 = 5.08). In the first 90 days, 61 × 90 / 365 = 15.04, so about 15 stays. Actual launch months may be slower. If about 70% of guests leave a review (assumption), that's 15 × 0.7 = 10.5, call it 10 reviews in 90 days. A full year is 61 × 0.7 = 42.7, about 43 reviews.

With about 10 reviews, each one is a tenth of what a new guest reads.

**Tracker at day 90 (all data are assumptions for teaching).**

| Metric | Result |
|---|---|
| Stays completed | 15 |
| Reviews received | 10 |
| Rating distribution | 7 five-star, 2 four-star, 1 three-star |
| Average | (7 × 5 + 2 × 4 + 1 × 3) / 10 = 46 / 10 = 4.6 |
| First response times (15 threads) | Median 9 minutes; 14 of 15 under 60 minutes; one at 90 minutes (after 10 p.m.) |
| Complaint mentions (reviews and messages) | Bunk room too warm (2), check-in directions unclear (1), internet outage (1) |
| Cleanliness misses | 0 photo-review failures; 0 guest reports |
| Host cancellations | 0 |
| Repeat-stay signals | 2 "we'll be back" mentions; 1 inquiry about next summer |

**Reading it.** The average looks fine, but the distribution and complaint log say more. Two guests mentioned a warm bunk room: one in the three-star review, one in a message. That's a repeat, so it goes on the fix list. The lower level has no dedicated cooling in the bunk room (assumption). Chris gets a quote for a ceiling fan or a portable cooling unit. Either costs more than $200 (assumption), the most the team can spend on one item without the owners' OK (set in OP.6 and OP.7). So it goes on the owner decision list: the purchases Maya and Chris decide at their month-end review (OP.8). Until then, the house manual adds a line on thermostat settings for the lower level. The listing adds a note, because accuracy covers comfort too.

The unclear check-in directions come from a mapping app that routes guests down a closed lake road (assumption). Maya adds a photo of the correct turnoff to the 7-day message and the house manual. The overnight 90-minute reply came after 10 p.m., outside Maya's messaging hours, so it falls outside the target. It shows the team needs an after-hours auto-reply. They keep the rule that safety messages go to the after-hours phone, and the auto-reply gives that number. The internet outage from OP.8 already has a backup-router quote on the owner decision list.

**The three-star review.** Suppose it reads: "Great lake access and clean house, but the bunk room was really hot and the kids didn't sleep well."

A public response that follows the process:

> Thanks for staying with us and for the specific feedback on the bunk room. You're right that the lower level ran warm. We've added cooling to the bunk room and updated the house manual with thermostat tips for the lower level. We're glad you enjoyed the lake.

That response is only accurate once the cooling is actually installed. If the owners haven't approved it yet, wait to post. Or post a version that says only what's done: the manual line and the listing note. Don't dispute this review. It's fair, specific, and useful.

**What goes to Sam's OP.10.** The 90-day tracker, the complaint log with fixes and dates, and the response-time data. Add the pending owner decisions (backup internet, bunk room cooling). Sam's OP.10 review compares actual performance with the underwriting. These records explain the "why" behind his numbers.

### OP.9.9 Exercise: your guest experience tracker

**Part A: factor map for your listing.** For each factor from Airbnb's search page, name one thing you'll check monthly.

| Factor | What I control | Monthly check | Who |
|---|---|---|---|
| Quality: photos and listing accuracy | | | |
| Quality: reviews | | | |
| Quality: communication | | | |
| Popularity | | | |
| Price (with Sam's OP.5) | | | |
| Availability and stay flexibility | | | |

**Part B: review request.** Write yours, then check it.

| Check | Yes / No |
|---|---|
| Offers nothing of value | |
| Doesn't mention a specific rating | |
| Doesn't link to any refund, deposit, discount, or future stay | |
| Invites private feedback about problems | |
| Sent after checkout, not tied to a goodwill gesture | |

**Part C: monthly tracker.**

| Month | Stays | Reviews | 5 / 4 / 3 / 2 / 1 | Median first response (min) | Complaints by reason | Cleaning misses | Host cancellations | Repeat signals | Fix list (item, owner, date) |
|---|---:|---:|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| | | | | | | | | | |

**Decision rules.**

- A complaint reason that appears twice in a quarter goes on the fix list with an owner and a date.
- Read every review below five stars. A review below four stars also gets a read of the turnover photos and message thread within 48 hours (house rule).
- Respond publicly after the facts are checked. Claim only fixes that are complete.
- Dispute a review only if it breaks Airbnb's reviews policy. Save the policy section you're relying on.

### OP.9.10 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** Airbnb's current search, ground rules, and reviews policy pages before you rely on any wording here. The wording and features change.

**Confirm with:** your local licensing office that any minimum-stay or open-date change you make to help search stays within your permit's conditions.

<!-- Production note: Sam confirms before recording that these claims don't overlap or conflict with his OP.2 lesson on listing optimization and structured data. -->

### OP.9.11 Next lesson

Next in order is Sam's OP.10 The 30-Day and 90-Day Performance Review. Bring him your 90-day tracker, complaint log, and pending owner decisions. Watch for how he compares actual revenue, occupancy, and expenses with the PA.4 underwriting. That's the spreadsheet estimate of what the property would earn and cost, made before buying. Then come back to OP.11 Scaling Your Portfolio: Refinancing, HELOCs & Property #2. There I'll use that review to decide whether Cedar Ridge has earned a second property, or needs another season first.

### OP.9 sources

- [Airbnb: How search results work (help 39)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/39). Quality, popularity, price, location; variety; availability; length-of-stay flexibility. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch (airbnb.com) summary; direct fetch blocked.
- [Airbnb: Ground rules for home hosts (help 2895)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2895). Accuracy, reservation commitment, communication, cleanliness; enforcement. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Authentic and trustworthy reviews (help 2673)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2673). No incentives, extortion, pressure, or trades. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Reviews for homes (help 13)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/13). 14-day review window after checkout. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary (attribution to this page from search results).
- [Airbnb: Responding to a review (help 32)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/32). Public responses allowed if they follow the reviews policy. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb: Remove or dispute a review (help 3582)](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3582). 30-day author removal; disputes for policy violations; no guarantee. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [Airbnb Resource Center: The amenities guests want](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/the-amenities-guests-want-25). Guests filter by amenities. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- Cedar Ridge base case: 61 stays a year (the course's shared example numbers, `reference/recurring-examples.md`). 90-day tracker values and review rate are assumptions computed in python on 2026-09-25.

<a id="lesson-OP.11-scaling-your-portfolio"></a>
## OP.11  Scaling Your Portfolio: Refinancing, HELOCs & Property #2

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 2, Operations launch |
| Code | OP.11 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's OP.10 The 30-Day and 90-Day Performance Review (baseline); Sam's PA.4 Reading a Full Underwriting Package (original underwriting); PA.6 and PA.7 (financing); OP.7 (team); resource SR.3 (tax timing); next lesson OP.12 |

### OP.11.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can build a trailing-12-month report for your first property. That's the most recent 12 months of actual results. You set it beside the original underwriting, the forecast you made before you bought. You can price a cash-out refinance and a home equity line of credit (HELOC) in dollars per month and per year, including a weak year. Then you write go tests for property two and apply them. Go tests are a short list of checks property one and your cash must all pass before you shop. The result is a dated yes, not yet, or no, with the numbers that produced it.

### OP.11.1 Property two is a decision about property one

Most people start the second-property conversation with a listing. I want you to start it with a bank statement.

The case for buying a second property rests on one claim: the first one works about the way you said it would. If that claim is true, a second similar property is a repeat of a tested plan. If it is false, a second property doubles an untested plan and adds a second loan.

So the first job is proof. You need to know what property one actually earned, what it actually cost, and how much cash you have left after both. The underwriting from Sam's PA.4 lesson was a forecast. (Sam runs the live demos in this course; PA.4 is where you built that forecast.) Twelve months of operating records are evidence. This lesson is about reading the evidence before you commit more money.

The second job is liquidity. Buying property two usually means pulling cash from somewhere: savings, a refinance of property one, or a loan against your own home. Each source changes your monthly obligations and what you can survive. A second property funded by borrowed money needs a wider margin than one funded by savings.

### OP.11.2 Reading trailing performance against underwriting

Sam's OP.10 lesson shows the 30-day and 90-day review. Bring that report here. It is your baseline for the categories you track and how you pull the data. The trailing-12 is the same review stretched to a full year, so every season is in it.

Build it from records, not memory. Pull payouts and fees from each booking platform's earnings reports. Pull expenses from the property's bank account and card. Pull booked nights, stays, and average nightly rate from your property management software (PMS) or the platform calendar. If your bookkeeping mixes property one with personal spending, fix that first; the trailing-12 is only as clean as the account behind it.

Set the report up in three columns: the underwriting you bought on, the actual trailing 12 months, and the variance between them. You're looking for four things.

First, split the revenue miss into rate and occupancy. The rate is your average nightly rate (ADR), the average price per booked night. A lower nightly rate and a lower occupancy point to different fixes. Rate problems often trace to pricing settings or competition. Occupancy problems can trace to calendar gaps, minimum stays, reviews, or demand. Sam's OP.5 lesson covers pricing tools; you only need to know which lever moved.

Second, find the expense lines that grew. Utilities, insurance renewals, and repairs are the usual ones. One large repair in the year may be a one-off. A pattern of repairs is a budget problem.

Third, check whether you actually funded the capital reserve. Underwriting often includes a line such as 5% of gross for future replacements. If that money stayed in the operating account and got spent, your cash flow looks better than it is.

Fourth, compare the loan payment (debt service). Principal and interest (P&I) are fixed on a fixed-rate loan. Your lender may also collect property tax and insurance through escrow. That's a monthly add-on the lender holds and pays for you. Those amounts can change at renewal or reassessment. In the worksheet, keep tax and insurance in operating costs and debt service as P&I only. Then NOI is computed the same way every time.

The number that matters most is net operating income (NOI): revenue minus operating costs, before the mortgage. NOI is what a lender and a future buyer look at. Cash flow is NOI minus debt service. If trailing NOI is well under the underwritten base case, the second property should wait until you know why. You set what "well under" means in your go tests. Maya and Chris use 90% in the worked example.

### OP.11.3 Refinance math

A cash-out refinance replaces your current loan with a bigger one and hands you the difference, minus costs. It can feel like free money from appreciation. In practice you take on a new loan with a new payment, a new term, and new closing costs.

Here is the arithmetic in order:

1. New loan amount = appraised value × the lender's maximum loan-to-value (LTV). LTV is the loan as a share of the appraised value.
2. Equity removed = new loan amount − current loan balance.
3. Cash out = equity removed − refinance closing costs.
4. New payment = the principal and interest on the new loan at the new rate and term.
5. New cash flow = NOI − new annual principal and interest.

Every input comes from a written lender quote, not a rule of thumb. The appraisal may come in lower than your estimate. The lender may cap LTV below what you expect for a short-term rental (STR). The lender may also apply its own coverage ratio. That's how many dollars of property income the lender counts for every dollar of loan payment. PA.6 shows three ways lenders compute it. Compare quotes the way PA.7 taught, using the [CFPB's guide to comparing Loan Estimates](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/) where the lender issues one.

Watch three things.

The term reset. A new 30-year loan restarts the payoff schedule, and early payments are mostly interest. Your payment may look similar, but you are paying down principal more slowly.

Costs relative to cash. When the equity you remove is small, closing costs can eat a large share of it.

The weak year. Run the new payment against your weak-case NOI as well as the base case. The refinance makes the loan larger, so it makes a weak year worse.

### OP.11.4 HELOC mechanics and the risk to your home

A home equity line of credit is a revolving line secured by a home you already own, usually your primary residence. The [CFPB's HELOC explainer](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/) describes two phases. During the draw period (the CFPB gives 10 years as an example) you can borrow up to your limit. Many plans set minimum payments based on the balance. When the draw period ends, the repayment period begins. Depending on the plan, you may be allowed to repay over time or you may owe the whole balance at once ([CFPB HELOC brochure](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_heloc-brochure.pdf)).

HELOCs usually carry a variable rate. The rate is based on a publicly available index, such as a prime rate. That means your payment can change from month to month ([CFPB](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/)). Price the line at today's rate and at a higher stress rate.

Two risks deserve plain words.

Your home is the collateral. The CFPB warns that if you can't repay what you borrowed, you could lose your home ([CFPB HELOC booklet](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/201603_cfpb_booklet_heloc.pdf)). When a short-term rental funded this way underperforms, the house you live in is on the line too.

The line can shrink when you need it. The same CFPB booklet says plans generally let the lender freeze or reduce the line in two situations. One is a significant decline in the home's value. The other is when the lender reasonably believes a material change in your finances will stop you from paying ([CFPB HELOC booklet](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/201603_cfpb_booklet_heloc.pdf)). If you planned to use an undrawn HELOC as your emergency reserve, a significant drop in your home's value can remove it. For that reason, don't count undrawn HELOC capacity as a reserve.

The lender must disclose the draw and repayment periods, fees, how the minimum payment is figured, and how the rate can change ([CFPB](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/)). Read those disclosures before you model anything.

There is also a tax question for your CPA. IRS Publication 936 limits the home mortgage interest deduction on a home equity loan or line. The money must buy, build, or substantially improve the home securing it ([IRS Pub. 936](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p936)). Using the line for a rental down payment does not meet that test. Whether the interest can be deducted another way, such as a rental expense, depends on how the CPA traces the loan proceeds. Don't assume either answer in your model.

### OP.11.5 Same market, new market, and team capacity

Once the numbers work, the operating question starts. Can your team run two properties as well as it runs one?

A second property in the same market can share things. The same cleaner, handyman, and supply run may cover both. You already know the permit process, the seasonality, and the guest profile. You can reuse your OP.8 playbook with small edits. The risk is concentration: one bad season, one new ordinance, or one storm now hits both properties.

A second property in a new market spreads that risk. It also resets the learning curve. You'll need a new market check (Sam's PA.2), a new address-level permission review (resource SR.1), a new local team, and new vendor backups. Your first-year results from property one don't transfer to a market you've never operated in.

Either way, test team capacity against the OP.7 role list. The roles are turnover cleaner, backup cleaner, handyman, emergency contact, guest messaging, inspections, bookkeeping, and supplies. For each one, write who covers it for property two and who backs them up. Say one cleaner covers both houses and both have Saturday turnovers. You likely need a second cleaner before you buy, unless one person can finish both turnovers in the same checkout window.

Then set up systems that work across properties. That means one bookkeeping file with a tag per property and one calendar view. Add one guest-message template set with property details, and one reserve policy. If a co-host helps, give them the least access they need. [Airbnb lets you set co-host permission levels](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534), and the listing owner remains responsible for what a co-host does.

### OP.11.6 Worked example: Cedar Ridge at the end of year 2

Maya and Chris are the example couple used throughout this course. They bought Cedar Ridge, a hypothetical lake-market house, for $385,000. They used a DSCR loan at 7.25% for 30 years, with 25% down. DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio. The lender sized the loan mostly on the property's rental income, not their pay (PA.6). It's now the end of year 2. They're thinking about a second lake-market house. They have three funding ideas: savings, a cash-out refinance of Cedar Ridge, and a HELOC on their primary home.

**Step 1. Trailing 12 months vs underwriting.** The trailing-12 below is a hypothetical year I built for teaching. The underwriting column is their original plan (base case B1 in the course example file).

All figures are assumptions. Trailing-12: average nightly rate (ADR) $289, occupancy 55%, 201 booked nights, 57 stays. Gross booking revenue also includes the $175 cleaning fee guests pay on each stay. So it's more than ADR × nights: $289 × 201 = $58,089, plus 57 × $175 = $9,975, gives $68,064.

| Line | Original plan (B1) | Trailing 12 (assumption) | Variance |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Gross booking revenue | $74,275 | $68,064 | -$6,211 |
| Platform service fee (15.5%) | -$11,513 | -$10,550 | +$963 |
| On-call handyman / inspection retainer | -$1,800 | -$1,800 | $0 |
| Cleaner pay | -$9,150 | -$8,550 | +$600 |
| Utilities, internet, trash | -$5,400 | -$5,850 | -$450 |
| Supplies | -$1,525 | -$1,596 | -$71 |
| Software | -$1,200 | -$1,200 | $0 |
| Repairs and maintenance | -$3,714 | -$5,200 | -$1,486 |
| Capital reserve (5% of gross, funded) | -$3,714 | -$3,403 | +$311 |
| Property tax | -$3,850 | -$3,850 | $0 |
| STR insurance | -$3,200 | -$3,450 | -$250 |
| **Operating costs** | **-$45,066** | **-$45,449** | **-$383** |
| **NOI** | **$29,209** | **$22,615** | **-$6,594** |
| DSCR loan principal and interest | -$23,637 | -$23,637 | $0 |
| **Cash flow** | **$5,572** | **-$1,022** | **-$6,594** |

One cost isn't in these lines: a $400-a-year permit renewal they found during due diligence (PA.9). Including it, trailing NOI is $22,215 and cash flow is -$1,422. The tables leave it out so they line up with the original plan. Every cash-flow gap below grows by $400, and no test result changes.

Read it the way section OP.11.2 described.

Revenue came in at 91.6% of the original plan ($68,064 / $74,275). Both levers moved. The rate is $11 below the $300 underwritten, and occupancy is 3 points below the 58% underwritten. Costs are only $383 higher in total. The platform fee and cleaner pay fell with lower revenue, while repairs, utilities, insurance, and supplies rose. Supplies ran about $28 a stay ($1,596 / 57) against the $25 planned. Restock prices went up, or the cleaner is restocking more per stay. Either way, it's worth a look.

NOI came in at 77.4% of underwriting ($22,615 / $29,209). An 8.4% revenue miss became a 22.6% NOI miss. Most costs stay put when revenue drops. Insurance, property tax, software, the handyman retainer, and most utilities don't shrink when you book fewer nights.

Cash flow went from a planned $5,572 to -$1,022. Maya and Chris covered the gap from savings. The capital reserve was funded, which is good. It means the -$1,022 is honest.

The repairs line includes a water heater replacement (assumption). If that's a one-off, next year's repairs may fall back toward $3,714. They should still find out why rate and occupancy slipped before counting on a better year.

**Step 2. The refinance.** Value has grown 3% a year (assumption) from the $385,000 purchase price to $408,446 ($385,000 × 1.03 × 1.03).

- New loan at 75% LTV: $408,446 × 0.75 = $306,334.
- Equity removed: $306,334 − $282,951 balance = $23,383.
- Cash out: $23,383 − $7,658 refinance costs = $15,725. The $7,658 is 2.5% of the new loan (assumption). Your written quote replaces it.
- New principal and interest at 7.0% for 30 years: $2,038 a month, or $24,457 a year.
- Change in annual debt service: $24,457 − $23,637 = $820 more a year.

The costs take 32.8% of the equity removed ($7,658 / $23,383). The new 7.0% rate is a bit lower than the original 7.25%, so the payment only rises $820. But the loan is bigger and the 30-year clock restarts.

Cash flow after the refinance, against three cases. The original weak case is the bad year they underwrote before buying: $265 ADR and 50% occupancy.

| Case | NOI | New P&I | Cash flow |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Original plan (base case) | $29,209 | -$24,457 | $4,752 |
| Original weak case (B2) | $18,162 | -$24,457 | -$6,295 |
| Trailing 12 (assumption) | $22,615 | -$24,457 | -$1,842 |

The refinance delivers $15,725. That's about 11% of the $142,688 total cash it took to buy and launch Cedar Ridge (down payment, closing costs, and furnishing). It also turns the actual year's cash flow from -$1,022 to -$1,842.

**Step 3. The HELOC on their primary home.** Their home is worth $520,000 with a $260,000 mortgage. Suppose a lender allows combined loan-to-value (CLTV) of 80% (assumption; the lender sets the real limit). CLTV is your first mortgage plus the HELOC, as a share of the home's value.

- Maximum total debt: $520,000 × 0.80 = $416,000.
- Maximum line: $416,000 − $260,000 = $156,000.

Suppose they draw $100,000 (assumption) toward property two. I priced it at two variable rates (assumptions). During the draw period I assumed interest-only payments. For the repayment period I assumed a 20-year payoff.

| $100,000 drawn | 8.0% rate | 10.0% rate |
|---|---:|---:|
| Interest-only payment, monthly | $667 | $833 |
| Interest-only cost, annual | $8,000 | $10,000 |
| 20-year repayment payment, monthly | $836 | $965 |
| 20-year repayment payment, annual | $10,037 | $11,580 |

Now test what that does to a second house. Suppose property two is a Cedar Ridge twin with the same price and DSCR terms (assumption). Its pre-diligence base cash flow is $5,572 and its weak case is -$5,475 (original financing, B4). The HELOC payment is a household bill. I charge it to property two here because property two is the reason for the loan. Subtract the HELOC interest:

| Property two cash flow after HELOC interest | 8.0% | 10.0% |
|---|---:|---:|
| Base case: $5,572 − interest | -$2,428 | -$4,428 |
| Weak case: -$5,475 − interest | -$13,475 | -$15,475 |

A base case that was thin becomes negative once the down payment is borrowed. The weak case becomes a five-figure annual gap, and their primary home secures part of the money.

One more stress: say their home's value drops 10% to $468,000 (assumption). The 80% limit falls to $374,400. With $100,000 drawn, they owe $360,000 ($260,000 + $100,000), which is 76.9% CLTV. They're still under 80%, but the room left on the line falls from $56,000 to $14,400. The CFPB booklet in OP.11.4 also lists a significant value decline itself as a reason a lender may freeze or reduce the line. So even that $14,400 may not be there.

**Step 4. Liquidity.** After buying and launching Cedar Ridge, Maya and Chris had $80,471.50 left (PA.12 walks through each step). That figure is before two years of saving, spending, and the trailing shortfall (assumption). To keep the example simple, I hold it at $80,471.50. In your own worksheet, use today's actual balance, after any shortfalls you covered.

Their $51,000 emergency fund and the $15,342 property reserve sit inside that balance. The property reserve is six months of Cedar Ridge PITIA. PITIA means principal, interest, property tax, insurance, and association dues. Cedar Ridge has no HOA, so the dues are zero. Six months is $15,342 ($2,557 × 6). A second financed property would need its own reserve. A lender may require more ([Fannie Mae reserve requirements](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements) show how conventional-loan reserves can extend past the down payment).

In the go tests below I test the HELOC route. The other two ideas can't fund the twin alone. Savings of $80,471.50 can't cover $142,688, and the refinance yields only $15,725. Here's the reserve test for a twin funded with a $100,000 draw (assumption):

- Twin's total cash in (assumption: same price and terms as Cedar Ridge): $142,688.
- From savings: $142,688 − $100,000 HELOC = $42,688.
- Savings left: $80,471.50 − $42,688 = $37,783.50.
- Needed: six months of PITIA on two houses ($2,557 × 6 × 2 = $30,684), plus the $51,000 emergency fund. That's $81,684.
- Short by: $81,684 − $37,783.50 = $43,900.50.

Adding the refinance's $15,725 would leave $53,508.50, still short. It would also add $820 a year to Cedar Ridge's payment. So I leave it out.

**Step 5. Apply the go tests.** Here are the tests they wrote before looking at listings. The thresholds are their choices (assumptions), not industry standards.

| Test | Their threshold | Cedar Ridge result | Pass? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Trailing-12 NOI vs underwritten base | At least 90% | 77.4% | No |
| Property one covers its own debt service | Cash flow ≥ $0 without top-ups from savings | -$1,022 | No |
| Cash reserves after buying property two | 6 months PITIA per financed property plus personal emergency fund, none borrowed | $37,783.50 left vs $81,684 needed: $43,900.50 short (Step 4) | No |
| Property two weak case with financing at stress rate | Loss they can cover from W-2 (salary) income for 12 months without new debt | -$15,475 at 10% | Unknown: needs their household budget (not scored) |
| Team backups | Named backup for every OP.7 role for both houses | One cleaner, no backup | No |

The -$15,475 uses interest-only payments. Once the HELOC's repayment period starts, the annual cost rises to $11,580 at 10%. Then the gap grows to about -$17,055 (-$5,475 − $11,580). Maya and Chris wrote five go tests. The worksheet in OP.11.7 adds market and systems rows.

Here's how I read any set of go tests. Check the No rule first. No means property one can't cover its own debt service even in its underwritten base case, or the household can't carry property two's weak case. Then hold one property. Not yet means any test fails and the No rule doesn't apply. Fix it and recheck on a set date. A failure in the first three tests always means at least not yet. Those rows describe property one and your cash, and a new listing can't fix either. Proceed means every test passes.

Result: not yet. Cedar Ridge's pre-diligence base case covers its loan with $5,572 to spare, and the household test is still open. So the No rule doesn't apply. Maya and Chris don't need a new property. They need a better year from the one they have. They find the cause of the rate and occupancy gap with Sam's OP.5 tools. They confirm the water heater was a one-off, and they hire a backup cleaner. Then they rerun this worksheet after the next 90-day review.

The refinance and HELOC were available, and neither drove the answer. Borrowing capacity is not the same as a reason to borrow.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### OP.11.7 Exercise: property-two readiness worksheet

Fill this in for your own first property. Use the trailing 12 months ending last month. Date every source.

**Part A. Trailing-12 vs underwriting**

| Line | Underwriting | Trailing 12 | Variance | Source and date | Cause (one line) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| Booked nights / stays | | | | | |
| ADR | | | | | |
| Occupancy | | | | | |
| Gross booking revenue | | | | | |
| Platform fees | | | | | |
| Cleaning and supplies | | | | | |
| Utilities | | | | | |
| Repairs and maintenance | | | | | |
| Capital reserve actually funded | | | | | |
| Property tax and insurance | | | | | |
| Other operating costs | | | | | |
| **NOI** | | | | | |
| Debt service (P&I) | | | | | |
| **Cash flow** | | | | | |
| Trailing NOI ÷ underwritten NOI | | | | | |

**Part B. Funding options for property two**

| Item | Savings only | Cash-out refinance | HELOC | Notes |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Cash available | | | | Written quote date |
| Closing costs or fees | | | | |
| Net cash available | | | | |
| New or added annual payment, today's rate | | | | |
| Same payment at stress rate (variable only) | | | | Rate used |
| Property one cash flow after this option (refinance only), trailing case | | | | Savings and HELOC leave it unchanged |
| Property one cash flow after this option (refinance only), weak case | | | | |
| Added annual cost to the household or property two (HELOC) | | | | Interest-only and repayment |
| Collateral at risk | None added | Property one | Your home | |
| Can the lender freeze or cut the line? | n/a | n/a | Check plan terms | |

**Part C. Your go tests** (write them before you look at listings)

| Test | Your threshold | Your result | Pass? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Trailing-12 NOI vs underwritten base | | | |
| Property one cash flow without top-ups | | | |
| Cash reserves after purchase, excluding undrawn credit | | | |
| Property two weak case including financing cost at stress rate | | | |
| Market choice: same market concentration or new market learning | | | |
| Team: named primary and backup for each OP.7 role, both properties | | | |
| Systems: books, calendar, messaging, reserves set up per property | | | |

Two rows have no example in OP.11.6. For market choice, write which risk you accept (both houses hit together, or a new learning curve) and one condition that would stop you. Systems pass when books, calendar, messaging templates, and reserve policy each have a named setup for both houses.

**Decision:** Proceed to a property-two buy box (PA.1, your written criteria for what to buy) / Not yet: fix property one and recheck on (date) / No: hold one property. Date: ______. Signed by each owner: ______.

Read the decision the same way as in OP.11.6. Check the No rule first. No means property one can't cover its own debt service even in its underwritten base case, or the household can't carry property two's weak case. Not yet means any test fails and the No rule doesn't apply; set a recheck date. A "No" in any of the first three rows always means at least not yet. Those rows describe property one and your cash, and a new listing can't fix either. Proceed means every test passes.

### OP.11.8 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** your bookkeeper or CPA that the trailing-12 matches the property's books and bank records, and that repairs vs improvements are classified correctly.

**Confirm with:** the refinance lender, in writing: appraised value, maximum LTV for a short-term rental, and the coverage ratio definition. Also get rate, points (upfront fees paid to lower the rate), closing costs, reserves, and any prepayment penalty on the existing loan.

**Confirm with:** the HELOC lender, in the plan disclosures: maximum CLTV, draw and repayment periods, index and margin, and any rate caps. Also get the minimum payment formula, fees, and the conditions that let the lender freeze or reduce the line.

**Confirm with:** your CPA how HELOC or refinance interest will be treated, given IRS Publication 936 and how you use the proceeds. Ask how a second property affects your depreciation and loss position (resource SR.3).

**Confirm with:** the local licensing office or an STR attorney, for property two's exact address, that the planned use is permitted (resource SR.1).

**Confirm with:** your insurer that property two can be covered for short-term rental use, and whether a combined policy changes deductibles or terms.

### OP.11.9 Next lesson

Scaling is one way to change the investment. The other ways are to refinance and keep one property, to sell, or to exchange into something different. OP.12 Exit Strategy: Hold, Refinance, Sell, or 1031 Exchange takes the same Cedar Ridge numbers to year 5. Bring your trailing-12 report; it becomes the starting point for your hold-or-exit worksheet.

### OP.11 sources

- [CFPB: What is a home equity line of credit (HELOC)?](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/ask-cfpb/what-is-a-home-equity-line-of-credit-heloc-en-107/): draw period (10-year example), minimum payments, variable rate tied to a public index, required disclosures. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary (consumerfinance.gov). Direct fetch blocked.
- [CFPB: What you should know about HELOCs (brochure)](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_heloc-brochure.pdf): repayment may be over time or all at once when the draw period ends. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [CFPB: What you should know about home equity lines of credit (booklet)](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/201603_cfpb_booklet_heloc.pdf): lender may freeze or reduce the line if the home's value declines significantly or finances change materially; risk of losing the home. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS Publication 936](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p936): home equity interest deductible as mortgage interest only if proceeds buy, build, or substantially improve the home securing the loan. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary (irs.gov).
- [CFPB: Compare Loan Estimates](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/owning-a-home/compare/compare-loan-estimates/): comparing written quotes. Brief-provided, not independently opened.
- [Fannie Mae Selling Guide B3-4.1-01, Minimum Reserve Requirements](https://selling-guide.fanniemae.com/sel/b3-4.1-01/minimum-reserve-requirements): reserves can be required beyond the down payment. Brief-provided, not independently opened.
- [Airbnb: co-host permissions](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/1534): permission levels; listing owner responsible for co-hosts. Brief-provided, not independently opened.
- Cedar Ridge figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md` (B1, B2, B4, B7). Trailing-12, HELOC, and pass-rule figures are new assumptions computed in Python on 2026-09-25 from `tools/examples.py`.

<a id="lesson-OP.12-exit-strategy"></a>
## OP.12  Exit Strategy: Hold, Refinance, Sell, or 1031 Exchange

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Two: STR Concierge / Phase 2, Operations launch (final lesson of the track) |
| Code | OP.12 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | OP.11 (trailing-12 and refinance math); Sam's OP.10 The 30-Day and 90-Day Performance Review; Sam's PA.5 Cost Segregation & Year-One Depreciation, Explained; PA.10 (entity and CPA questions); resource SR.3 (depreciation and recapture inputs) |

### OP.12.0 Learning outcome

After this lesson you can put four options side by side for a property you own. They are hold, refinance, sell, or sell through a 1031 exchange. You'll compare them on after-tax cash, time to complete, future debt, ongoing work, and opportunity cost. You'll know how the gain on a sale is built, which parts depreciation touches, and which answers only your CPA can give. You'll leave with a decision worksheet you can hand to that CPA and to a qualified intermediary. That's the independent company that holds your sale money during a 1031 exchange.

### OP.12.1 One question behind all four options

Every exit decision comes down to one question. Is the equity in this property doing more for you here than it would do somewhere else?

Equity is the value you could turn into cash: market value minus the loan balance, minus the costs of getting it out. Holding keeps that equity in the house. Refinancing pulls some of it out as a new loan. Selling turns it into cash, minus taxes. A 1031 exchange moves it into another investment property and may defer the tax.

Each option mixes five things differently. They are after-tax cash, time to complete, future debt, the work you keep doing, and what you give up (opportunity cost). No option wins on all five. You're choosing a trade-off you can live with.

You'll make this call more than once. Use it whenever your 90-day or annual review shows a real change. That review comes from the 30-Day and 90-Day Performance Review lesson (OP.10, taught by Sam). Examples: a big capital repair, a new local rule, a life change, or equity that has outgrown the cash flow.

### OP.12.2 Start with an honest equity number

Four inputs start every exit analysis.

The updated value. Get it from a broker's opinion of value, recent comparable sales, or an appraisal. Don't use the price you'd like. Short-term rental (STR) houses sometimes sell with furniture and bookings. Ask the broker whether buyers in your market pay for that.

The loan balance. Take it from the servicer's latest statement, not your own amortization schedule. The servicer is the company that collects your loan payments. If you plan to sell, request a payoff quote; it can include items the statement doesn't show.

Capital repairs coming in the next few years. A roof, HVAC system, or deck replacement changes the hold case. It can also change the sale price if a buyer's inspector finds it.

The cost of selling: agent commissions, transfer taxes, title and closing fees, and any seller concessions (costs you agree to cover for the buyer). The Cedar Ridge example later in this lesson uses 7% of value (assumption). Your agent can give you a line-by-line net sheet.

Value − loan balance − selling costs = net proceeds before income tax. That is not your spendable number yet.

### OP.12.3 Hold, refinance, or sell: the before-tax comparison

Holding is the default. It needs no transaction, but it has a cost you don't see on a statement. Cedar Ridge, the example later in this lesson, earns about $5,172 a year in cash flow after the mortgage (assumption). That's on about $142,560 of equity, so roughly 3.6%. If you could sell and put the after-tax cash somewhere safer at a similar return, holding carries more risk for the same money. That comparison is your opportunity cost.

Holding makes sense when the property still covers its costs in a weak season, the capital repairs are funded, and the work is manageable. It also makes sense when a sale would trigger a large tax bill you'd rather defer. The property should still meet the goals you set in PA.1 (your buy box).

Refinancing lets you keep the property and pull out cash. The previous lesson, OP.11, showed the math: new loan, costs, new payment, and the weak-season test. That test asks whether the property still pays all its bills, including the mortgage, in a bad year. A refinance doesn't trigger a sale, so it doesn't trigger tax on a gain. It does add debt and payments. Refinance when you have a specific, better use for the cash and the property can carry the bigger loan in a weak year.

Selling ends the risk and the work. It turns equity into cash you can redeploy or keep. It also brings the tax consequences in the next section. Sell when the property no longer fits your goals or the local rules have tightened. Also sell when a large repair doesn't pay back, or the equity has a clearly better use.

### OP.12.4 How the gain on a sale is built

You don't need to calculate your own tax. You do need to know how the calculation is built, so you can collect the right records and read your CPA's answer. Every rule below depends on your facts and the tax year. Treat it as the structure of the conversation, not a result.

**Gain = amount realized − adjusted basis.** Amount realized is roughly the sale price minus selling costs. Adjusted basis starts with what you paid plus certain acquisition costs, goes up by improvements, and goes down by depreciation. [IRS Publication 544](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p544) describes adjusted basis this way, including decreases for depreciation.

**Depreciation reduces basis whether or not you claimed it.** Suppose you didn't claim all the depreciation you were entitled to. [IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527) says you still reduce basis by the full amount you could have deducted. The IRS phrase is depreciation "allowed or allowable." Skipping depreciation doesn't protect you at sale. It only means you lost the deduction. Publication 527 also says basis goes down by any special depreciation allowance (bonus depreciation) you claimed. Bonus depreciation means deducting a large share, or all, of an asset's cost in its first year, if it's eligible. Basis also goes down by any section 179 deduction, another way to expense an asset up front.

One more term before the split. The cost-segregation lesson (PA.5, taught by Sam) showed how a study can move part of the building into 5-, 7-, and 15-year property. If it's eligible, bonus depreciation can deduct it in year one.

**The gain then gets split into pieces that can be taxed differently.** Your CPA does this split. Here is the structure:

1. Personal property is generally section 1245 property. That includes furniture and many building parts a cost-segregation study moves to 5- or 7-year lives. Land improvements on 15-year lives can be treated differently, so your CPA classifies each asset. [Publication 544](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p544) says gain on section 1245 property is treated as ordinary income to the extent of depreciation allowed or allowable. The ordinary part is the lesser of that depreciation or the gain on those assets. Those assets can show a gain because part of the sale price gets assigned to them, while depreciation has shrunk their basis. Example: say furniture fully depreciated to $0 is assigned $6,000 of the sale price (assumption). That produces $6,000 of ordinary income. So the recapture depends on how much of the sale price is allocated to those items. That allocation is a CPA and appraisal question.
2. The building itself is section 1250 property. Gain tied to straight-line depreciation on it is called unrecaptured section 1250 gain. [IRS Topic 409](https://www.irs.gov/taxtopics/tc409) says that portion is taxed at a maximum 25% rate.
3. Any remaining gain is section 1231 gain under [Publication 544](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p544). Your CPA nets it with your other gains and losses for the year. The rate depends on that netting and your income, so the example below uses a placeholder rate.

Two more items belong on the CPA list. First, if depreciation losses from the property were suspended under the passive activity rules, ask how a sale affects them ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Suspended means you couldn't use the losses yet, so they carried forward. Second, ask which state income taxes and any other federal taxes apply to your gain. I have not modeled either here.

Cost segregation matters at this step. Its faster deductions reduce basis faster. At sale, more of the gain can land in the ordinary-income bucket. The study mostly changes timing. It can still be worth it, as the Cedar Ridge example in OP.12.6 shows. Resource SR.3 is the tool for that conversation.

### OP.12.5 The 1031 exchange: what qualifies and the clock

A like-kind exchange under section 1031 lets you sell one investment property and buy another. You defer the gain instead of paying tax on it now. Deferral is not forgiveness. Ask your CPA how the deferred gain affects the replacement property's basis and future depreciation.

**What qualifies.** Since 2018, section 1031 applies only to real property held for use in a trade or business or for investment. Real property held primarily for sale doesn't qualify ([IRS like-kind exchange tips](https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/like-kind-exchanges-real-estate-tax-tips)). Both the property you sell and the one you buy must be held that way. The IRS says property used primarily for personal use, like a primary residence, second home, or vacation home, does not qualify. Exchanges of machinery, equipment, vehicles, and other personal property generally no longer qualify after 2017. That means the furniture in an STR generally can't be part of the like-kind exchange.

**The two deadlines.** You have 45 days from the sale of your property to identify possible replacement properties. The identification must be in writing, signed by you, and delivered to a person involved in the exchange, such as the qualified intermediary. You must receive the replacement property by the earlier of two dates. One is the 180th day after the sale. The other is the due date, including extensions, of your tax return for the year of the sale ([IRS like-kind exchange tips](https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/like-kind-exchanges-real-estate-tax-tips); [Form 8824 instructions](https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i8824)). Treat these deadlines as strict.

**The qualified intermediary (QI).** In a deferred exchange you use a QI to hold the sale proceeds. Your own agent can't serve as the QI. The IRS list includes your real estate agent or broker, accountant, attorney, and employee. It also covers anyone who acted in those roles for you in the previous two years ([IRS like-kind exchange tips](https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/like-kind-exchanges-real-estate-tax-tips)). The IRS also warns that some intermediaries have gone bankrupt or failed to perform, and that cost taxpayers their deadlines. Vet the QI: ask how funds are held, what bonding or insurance exists, and who signs withdrawals.

**Reporting.** You report the exchange on [Form 8824](https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i8824). Ask your CPA how any cash you receive, or a smaller replacement loan, would be treated on that form.

**The vacation-home caveat.** An STR owner who also stays at the property has a personal-use question. The IRS issued Revenue Procedure [Rev. Proc. 2008-16](https://www.irs.gov/pub/irs-drop/rp-08-16.pdf) as a safe harbor. Under it, the IRS will not challenge whether a dwelling unit with occasional personal use qualifies as property held for investment. The safe harbor has qualifying-use standards in its section 4.02. Summaries of that section describe a holding period around the exchange, a minimum number of fair-rental days, and a cap on personal use. I couldn't confirm the exact counts from the IRS text, so I don't state them here. Have your CPA check them against section 4.02 before you rely on them. Outside the safe harbor, qualification depends on your facts. Don't assume a property you use personally qualifies automatically.

### OP.12.6 Worked example: Cedar Ridge at the end of year 5

Cedar Ridge is the course's example house. Maya and Chris bought it for $385,000 with a $288,750 DSCR loan. DSCR stands for debt service coverage ratio: the lender qualifies the loan mainly on the property's income compared with its payment. They put about $142,688 of cash in at closing, including furniture. Now it's the end of year 5, and they never refinanced. Every figure below is an assumption. Tax rates are placeholders for the CPA to replace.

All cash-flow figures below include the $400-a-year permit renewal found during due diligence (PA.9).

**Step 1. Equity and before-tax proceeds.**

- Value: $446,321 (3% a year growth, assumption).
- Loan balance: $272,519. They paid down $16,231 of principal in five years.
- Selling costs at 7%: $31,242.
- Net proceeds before income tax: $446,321 − $272,519 − $31,242 = $142,560.

For comparison, they put about $142,688 of cash in at closing. After five years of paydown and 3% growth, their equity net of selling costs is almost exactly what they started with.

**Step 2. Hold.** The example assumes net operating income stays flat. Net operating income (NOI) is booking revenue minus operating costs, before the mortgage. On the original financing, that gives base-case cash flow of $5,172 and weak-case cash flow of -$5,875 a year. On $142,560 of net equity, the base case earns 3.6% ($5,172 / $142,560). That's before any appreciation, which is not spendable income.

**Step 3. Refinance at year 5 instead.** They use the same refinance terms as earlier in the course: 75% loan-to-value, 2.5% costs, and a 7.0% rate (all assumptions). Loan-to-value (LTV) is the new loan as a share of the property's value.

- New loan: $446,321 × 0.75 = $334,741.
- Costs: $8,369.
- Cash out: $334,741 − $272,519 − $8,369 = $53,853.
- New principal and interest: $26,724 a year ($2,227 a month, 30-year amortization, assumption).
- Cash flow after: base $2,085 ($28,809 NOI − $26,724); weak -$8,962 ($17,762 NOI − $26,724).

They'd get $53,853 now and keep the property. Their base-case margin would shrink to $2,085 a year, and a weak year would cost $8,962.

**Step 4. Sell: build the gain two ways.** The amount realized is $446,321 − $31,242 = $415,079. The adjusted basis depends on how they depreciated. I simplified: full-year straight-line for five years, with the building on a 39-year life. This example assumes short average stays make it transient (nonresidential) use. A 27.5-year residential life would give larger yearly figures. Your CPA decides which applies. The real first year also depends on the month each asset was placed in service and the timing convention your CPA uses.

| | Scenario A: no cost-seg study (furniture still expensed with bonus) | Scenario B: cost-seg study + 100% bonus |
|---|---:|---:|
| Starting basis (price + $6,000 capitalized costs) | $391,000 | $391,000 |
| Of which land, not depreciable (20%, assumption) | $78,200 | $78,200 |
| Of which building | $312,800 | $312,800 |
| Furnishings basis | $32,000 | $32,000 |
| Depreciation on 5/7/15-year building parts (bonus) | $0 | -$68,816 |
| Depreciation on furnishings (bonus, if eligible) | -$32,000 | -$32,000 |
| Building straight-line, 5 years | -$40,105 ($8,021 × 5) | -$31,280 ($6,256 × 5) |
| **Total depreciation** | **-$72,105** | **-$132,096** |
| **Adjusted basis** | **$350,895** | **$290,904** |
| Amount realized | $415,079 | $415,079 |
| **Gain** | **$64,184** | **$124,175** |

The land and building rows show what's inside the $391,000; they aren't subtracted again. The building's yearly figure is $312,800 ÷ 39 = $8,021 in Scenario A. In Scenario B, the study moved $68,816 out, so $243,984 ÷ 39 = $6,256.

Now the split. The recapture on section 1245 items depends on how much of the price is allocated to them. I assumed $6,000 for the used furniture in both cases, plus $30,000 for the reclassified building parts in Scenario B. These allocations are assumptions only. I also treated all $30,000 as section 1245 property for simplicity; the CPA may classify the 15-year parts differently.

| Piece of the gain | Scenario A | Scenario B | Placeholder rate |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Section 1245 recapture (ordinary) | $6,000 | $36,000 | 32% (assumption) |
| Unrecaptured section 1250 gain | $40,105 | $31,280 | 25% (maximum per Topic 409) |
| Remaining section 1231 gain | $18,079 | $56,895 | 15% (assumption) |
| **Illustrative federal tax** | **$14,658** | **$27,874** | |
| **Net proceeds after illustrative tax** | **$127,902** | **$114,686** | |

Scenario B's section 1250 figure is smaller because the study moved $68,816 out of the building and into faster property. That depreciation shows up in the section 1245 row and the remaining gain instead.

Check the logic in Scenario B. They took $59,991 more depreciation over five years than in Scenario A. At 32%, that's up to $19,197 of earlier tax savings, but only if the losses were usable when taken. At sale, the illustration shows $13,216 more tax. The study moved deductions forward. It didn't erase the tax. Net, Scenario B comes out up to about $5,981 ahead ($19,197 − $13,216), plus the use of that money for five years. That's why a study can still be worth it. But if the early losses weren't usable, B is simply $13,216 worse off at sale.

On the after-tax figures, holding earns 4.0% on Scenario A's $127,902 and 4.5% on Scenario B's $114,686. B's return looks higher only because B's after-tax cash is smaller. The property earns the same either way. The equity you'd actually walk away with is smaller than the equity on paper.

**Step 5. The 1031 path.** Suppose they sell on October 20, 2031, and want to exchange (dates are assumptions).

- Day 45, the written identification deadline: December 4, 2031.
- Day 180: April 17, 2032. Check whether their 2031 return is due before April 17, 2032. If it is, that earlier date is the real deadline unless they file an extension. Their CPA confirms which date applies.

If the exchange qualifies, the gain on the real property is deferred. The furniture sits outside the like-kind exchange, so the CPA handles its $6,000 allocation separately. Their 10 personal-use days a year go to the CPA to check against the section 4.02 personal-use cap. They need their calendar records for the two years before the sale, and a plan for the two years after, to show the CPA. The CPA confirms the actual periods against section 4.02.

The exchange also carries next-property assumptions. The replacement property needs its own address-level permission check (resource SR.1) and underwriting (Sam's PA.4). It also needs financing (the DSCR lessons, PA.6 and PA.7) and a team (OP.7). All of that must be done within 180 days. That clock argues for having the replacement shortlist ready before the sale closes.

**Step 6. The decision.** Here is how the options line up for Maya and Chris.

| Option | Cash now (illustrative) | Future debt | Annual cash flow, base / weak | Work | Main risk |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|
| Hold | $0 | $272,519 | $5,172 / -$5,875 | Same | Weak seasons; capital repairs |
| Refinance | $53,853 | $334,741 | $2,085 / -$8,962 | Same | Bigger loan in a weak year |
| Sell | $127,902 (Scenario A) or $114,686 (Scenario B) | $0 | n/a | Ends | Tax due; redeploying cash |
| 1031 exchange | About $142,560 rolled into the next property (the $6,000 furniture allocation is handled outside the exchange; CPA) | New loan | Depends on replacement | Restarts | Deadlines; QI; qualification |

Notice that both Hold and Refinance lose money in a weak year. By the test in OP.12.3, holding only makes sense if they keep reserves for about $5,875 of weak-year losses. A refinance would need reserves for about $8,962.

Which is right depends on their buy-box goals from PA.1 and their answers in the worksheet below. The numbers make the trade-offs visible. They don't pick for you.

[Recording note: Preston can add a real example here if he has one.]

### OP.12.7 Exercise: exit decision worksheet

Fill one column per option. Attach your trailing-12 from OP.11 (your last 12 months of actual income and expenses). Write the source and date next to each figure.

**Part A. Starting facts**

| Item | Your figure | Source and date |
|---|---:|---|
| Current value (broker opinion, comps, or appraisal) | | |
| Loan balance or payoff quote | | |
| Capital repairs due in the next 3 years | | |
| Selling costs (agent net sheet) | | |
| Net proceeds before income tax | | |
| Trailing-12 NOI | | |
| Annual debt service | | |
| Personal-use days, each of the last 2 years | | |
| Depreciation taken to date, by asset class (from tax returns) | | |
| Suspended passive losses, if any (from tax returns) | | |

**Part B. Options side by side**

| | Hold | Refinance | Sell | 1031 exchange |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Cash received now, before tax | | | | |
| Estimated tax (from CPA) | | | | |
| Cash after tax | | | | |
| Debt after the transaction | | | | |
| Annual cash flow, base case | | | | |
| Annual cash flow, weak case | | | | |
| Months to complete | | | | |
| Hours a month of your time afterward | | | | |
| Return on after-tax equity (annual base cash flow ÷ the after-tax cash you'd have if you sold today, from the Sell column) | | | | |
| What the cash would do instead (opportunity cost) | | | | |

For Hold and Refinance, divide by the Sell column's cash after tax. That's the money you're choosing to leave in the property. For the 1031, use the replacement property's projected cash flow over the same equity.

**Part C. 1031 checklist** (only if you choose the exchange)

| Item | Answer | Who confirms |
|---|---|---|
| The property you're selling held for business or investment? | | CPA |
| Personal use within the Rev. Proc. 2008-16 standards, or other support? | | CPA |
| QI chosen; not a disqualified agent; funds held how? | | Attorney, CPA |
| Sale closing date | | Title/escrow |
| Day 45 (written identification) | | QI |
| Day 180 or return due date, whichever is earlier | | CPA |
| Replacement properties identified, with SR.1 permission check | | Licensing office, attorney |
| Replacement price and loan planned; effect of any cash or smaller loan | | CPA, lender |
| Furniture and other personal property: handled outside the exchange | | CPA |
| Who must approve (all owners, lender, any partners) | | Owners |

**Decision:** Hold / Refinance / Sell / Exchange. Reason in one sentence: ______. Recheck date: ______. Approved by: ______.

### OP.12.8 Closing the loop on the track

This is the last lesson in STR Concierge. Look at where the worksheet's inputs came from.

Every input comes from work you've already done. That includes your investment goals (your buy box) and your weak-season test. It also includes your depreciation choices with your CPA, your inspection and maintenance log, and your monthly and quarterly reviews.

An exit is only as good as the records behind it. Keep four things from day one. They are the closing documents, every depreciation schedule your CPA files, receipts for improvements and repairs, and a calendar of rental and personal-use days. With those, any of the four options is a short conversation. Without them, even a good sale becomes a reconstruction project.

If you sell or exchange, the course path starts over. A new property goes back to the buy box in PA.1, then market and deal review, financing, diligence, closing, launch, and the 30/90-day review. If you hold, the next decision point is your next annual review.

### OP.12.9 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** a broker or appraiser the current market value, and whether furniture or future bookings add anything a buyer will pay for.

**Confirm with:** the loan servicer a written payoff quote and any prepayment penalty.

**Confirm with:** your CPA the adjusted basis, depreciation allowed or allowable by asset, and the allocation of sale price to section 1245 items. Also confirm the split of gain, the rates, state tax, any suspended passive losses, and any other federal taxes on the gain.

**Confirm with:** your CPA, QI, and attorney everything in Part C, the 1031 checklist.

**Confirm with:** the refinance lender, in writing, value, LTV, rate, costs, and reserves if you choose to refinance.

**Confirm with:** the licensing office or an STR attorney that any replacement property's exact address permits your planned use.

### OP.12.10 Next lesson

This closes STR Concierge. If your decision is to hold, go back to Sam's OP.10 review each quarter and rerun this worksheet at least once a year. If you're buying again, by exchange or with sale proceeds, start at PA.1 Discovery: Defining Your Buy Box with updated goals. For the tax side, open resource SR.3 and fill in your actual depreciation history before you meet your CPA.

### OP.12 sources

- [IRS Publication 544, Sales and Other Dispositions of Assets](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p544): adjusted basis decreases by depreciation; allowed or allowable; section 1245 gain ordinary to the extent of depreciation (lesser of depreciation or gain); remaining gain is section 1231 gain. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary (irs.gov).
- [IRS Publication 527, Residential Rental Property](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): depreciation reduces basis; reduce basis by depreciation you could have deducted; basis reduced by special depreciation allowance and section 179. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS Topic 409, Capital gains and losses](https://www.irs.gov/taxtopics/tc409): unrecaptured section 1250 gain taxed at a maximum 25% rate. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925): passive activity rules (suspended losses question). Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary for related rules; treatment on sale not confirmed (see sources log, Unresolved).
- [IRS: Like-kind exchanges, real estate tax tips](https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/like-kind-exchanges-real-estate-tax-tips): real property only after 2017; business or investment use; personal-use property including vacation homes does not qualify; 45-day and 180-day rules; QI; disqualified agents; QI failure warning. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS: Instructions for Form 8824](https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i8824): reporting; 45-day and 180-day timing. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS Rev. Proc. 2008-16](https://www.irs.gov/pub/irs-drop/rp-08-16.pdf): safe harbor for dwelling units with occasional personal use; qualifying-use standards in section 4.02. Existence and purpose checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary (irs.gov); specific day and month counts seen only in non-IRS summaries (Unresolved).
- Cedar Ridge figures: `reference/recurring-examples.md` (B1, B4, B6, B7, B8); $400 permit renewal from PA.9. Year-5 refinance, gain split, allocations, and placeholder rates are new assumptions computed in Python on 2026-09-25.
