# Part Three: Shared Resources

<a id="lesson-SR.1-regulatory-compliance-checklist"></a>
## SR.1  Regulatory compliance checklist, by state and city

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Three: Shared Resources |
| Code | SR.1 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Roadmap Module 3 (3.1, Sam's 3.2 live regulation search, 3.3, 3.4); STR Concierge PA.1 and Sam's PA.2 Choosing a Market for Ownership: Regulatory-Safe, Cash-Flowing Areas |

### SR.1.0 What this resource does

This checklist turns "is this legal?" into a written decision for one exact address. You fill it in before you sign a lease or make an offer. At the end it says permitted, prohibited, or unresolved, and it shows the official page behind every answer. It also records who checked each line, on what date, and what still needs a professional.

It does not rate a whole state or city as "legal" or "Airbnb-friendly". Rules attach to the address, the building, the zoning district, the permit category, and your plan for the unit. Two units on the same street can get different answers. So the sheet starts with the plan, then walks each layer of permission. "By state and city" means the sheet walks the state, county, and city layers for your address. It is not a list of rules for each state. The NYC and LA tests below show how two cities answer the same plan.

Sam records the live demos in this course. Use this sheet with his lesson 3.2 (How to Research Regulations Before You Sign Anything) for the live search method. Owners in STR Concierge, the ownership program in Part Two, also use it with Sam's PA.2 (Choosing a Market for Ownership: Regulatory-Safe, Cash-Flowing Areas) when screening markets. You can start the sheet before you watch either one. Sam shows you where to click. This sheet is where you write down what you found and what you decided.

**Words used in this sheet**

| Term | Plain meaning |
|---|---|
| Short-term rental (STR) | Renting a home to guests for short stays. Each city sets its own cutoff (see the 30-night note in SR.1.2). |
| Operator | The person running the rental. City rules often call this person the "host". |
| Hosted / unhosted | Hosted means the operator is physically present during stays. Unhosted means nobody lives on site. |
| Arbitrage | Renting a unit from a landlord and re-renting it short-term. |
| Channel | Where the guest books: Airbnb, Vrbo, Booking.com, or your own direct site. |
| Remit | Collect a tax from the guest and pay it to the tax agency. |
| Contingency | A clause in a purchase offer that lets the buyer walk away if a condition isn't met by a set date. |
| Not reached | A line you skipped because an earlier line already decided the result. |

### SR.1.1 How to fill it in

Work top to bottom. Each section feeds the next.

1. **Write the plan first (Section A).** Record whether it's hosted or unhosted, whole unit or a room, the typical stay length, the maximum guests, and who lives there. Most rules turn on these facts, so a vague plan gives you a vague answer.
2. **Check each layer of permission (Section B).** State, county, city, zoning, building, rent regulation, HOA or condo, lease, insurance, and (for owners) the lender. For every line record the official source URL, the date you checked, the result in one sentence, and the reviewer.
3. **Record the permit details (Section C).** Category, prerequisites, caps, the primary-residence test, renewal, transferability, local contact, safety rules, and the rule for showing a permit number in ads.
4. **Record tax registrations and who remits on each channel (Section D).** "Remits" means collects the tax and pays it to the agency. Permit registration and tax registration are separate filings. Keep both separate from federal income tax, which is a CPA question.
5. **Send written questions for anything unclear (Section E).** Use the sample email in SR.1.7. Save the reply as a PDF with the date.
6. **Log what is still open (Section F).** Every open item gets an owner (you, attorney, CPA, insurer, licensing office) and a due date.
7. **Apply the decision rules (Section G).** Write the decision, the reason, and a recheck date.

A few habits make the sheet trustworthy.

- **Official sources only for rules.** City, county, and state government pages, the adopted ordinance, and the tax agency. A platform help page can tell you what the platform does (for example what taxes it collects). It is not the final word on what the city allows. Airbnb says as much in its [regulations overview](https://www.airbnb.com/resources/hosting-homes/a/learn-about-hosting-regulations-7): it is a starting point.
- **Quote, don't paraphrase, the line that decides the case.** Paste the sentence from the official page into the "Result" cell and put the URL beside it.
- **Date every check.** Rules change, so a result without a date is a guess.
- **Name a reviewer.** "Self" is fine for a first pass. Any line that decides the deal should also show a second reviewer, such as a local short-term rental (STR) attorney.
- **Save evidence.** Save the page as a PDF, keep screenshots of the permit portal, and file written answers from the city. Store them in one folder named with the address.

### SR.1.2 Decision rules

These rules decide the box you tick in Section G. Apply them in order and stop at the first one that fits.

| Rule | Condition | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Any official source clearly bars the planned use at this address (for example, the plan is unhosted and the city allows only hosted stays; or the plan is a non-primary residence and the city requires a primary residence) | **Prohibited** for this plan |
| 2 | The lease, HOA, condo documents, or building rules bar short-term stays, or (for a leased unit) the landlord has refused or will not give written permission | **Prohibited** until written permission exists |
| 3 | A required permit is capped, closed, or not available to your category of operator (for example, permits cannot transfer to a new operator) | **Prohibited** for now; note when the cap or waitlist reopens |
| 4 | Any deciding line in Sections B to D is blank, marked "to verify", or rests only on a platform page or a verbal answer; or, for a leased unit, the landlord has not yet answered in writing | **Unresolved** |
| 5 | Every deciding line has an official source, a date within the last 90 days, a written answer where the source was unclear, and no open item in Section F that could change the answer | **Permitted** for this plan, subject to the listed conditions |

Three more rules keep the decision honest.

- **"Permitted" applies to the plan you wrote in Section A.** Change the plan (more guests, more nights, a different stay length) and you rerun the sheet.
- **"Unresolved" means do not commit.** You can keep negotiating. A renter does not sign the lease or pay a deposit. A buyer can sign an offer only if it includes a regulatory contingency. Don't waive that contingency, or let it expire, while the sheet says unresolved.
- **A "prohibited" result can point to a different plan.** For example, stays of 30 nights or more fall outside some cities' short-term rules. Cities draw this line differently. NYC's registration law covers stays of fewer than 30 consecutive days. LA's occupancy tax covers stays of 30 days or less. So copy the exact wording from your city's page. A longer-stay plan is a different business with different rules, a different lease clause, and different numbers. Start a new sheet for it.

Recheck 90 days after the decision, and again 30 days before any permit renewal. 90 days is my teaching default, not a legal rule. Check sooner if the city has a pending ordinance.

### SR.1.3 The template

Copy the tables below for each address. The "Status" column takes one of four values: **Done** (official source, dated), **Written answer** (reply from the office saved), **To verify**, or **N/A** (with a reason).

#### Section A. Property and plan

| Field | What to enter | Source or formula | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Full street address and unit | Street, unit, city, ZIP | Lease draft or listing | |
| Parcel number (APN / BBL / PIN) | The assessor's parcel ID number; the name varies by place | County assessor or city property portal | |
| Jurisdiction | Inside a city, or unincorporated county land outside city limits; special districts | County GIS (online map) or city boundary map | |
| Zoning district | Code shown on the official zoning map | City or county zoning map | |
| Property type | Single-family, duplex, 3+ unit building, condo, ADU (accessory dwelling unit), mixed use | Assessor record; building certificate if any | |
| Year built | Year | Assessor record (matters for rent-control coverage in some cities) | |
| Ownership or lease | Own, lease, or co-host for owner | Deed or lease | |
| Is it anyone's primary residence? | Who lives there and for how many months a year | Your plan | |
| Hosted or unhosted | Host present during stays, yes or no | Your plan | |
| Whole unit or rooms | Whole unit / private room / shared room | Your plan | |
| Typical and minimum stay | Nights | Your plan | |
| Maximum guests | Number | Your plan and bed count | |
| Nights per year planned | Number | Your plan | |
| Channels | Airbnb, Vrbo, Booking.com, direct | Your plan | |
| Prepared by / date | Name, date | | |

#### Section B. Layers of permission

Every row needs all five columns filled. "Result" is one sentence plus the quoted line that decides it.

| Layer | What to check | Official source URL | Date checked | Result | Reviewer |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| State | State STR law, state lodging-tax registration, any state law that overrides local rules (preemption) | | | | |
| County | County STR ordinance (if unincorporated), county lodging tax | | | | |
| City | City STR ordinance or registration law; definitions of "short-term rental", "host", "primary residence" | | | | |
| Zoning | Is short-term rental a permitted, conditional, or prohibited use in this district? | | | | |
| Building | Building class, prohibited-building lists, fire and occupancy rules for the building type | | | | |
| Rent regulation | Is the unit covered by rent control or rent stabilization? Some cities bar STRs in covered units | | | | |
| HOA / condo | CC&Rs (the HOA's recorded rules), bylaws, rules; minimum lease terms; rental caps | HOA documents (not a government site; record where you got them) | | | |
| Landlord / lease | Written permission for short-term stays, sublet clause, guest rules | Signed lease or addendum (see lesson 5.3, Sublease Addendum Deep Dive) | | | |
| Insurance | Policy covers short-term guests at this address | Insurer's written confirmation (see lesson 2.4, Insurance & Liability Protection for Arbitrage Operators) | | | |
| Lender (owned property) | Mortgage allows the use; occupancy covenant (the loan's rule on how the owner must use the home) if any | Loan documents | | | |

#### Section C. Permit or registration details

| Field | What to enter | Official source URL | Date checked | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Permit / registration category | Name of the category that fits the Section A plan | | | |
| Issuing office and contact | Office name, phone, email, portal link | | | |
| Prerequisites | Documents: ID, proof of residence, landlord consent, safety inspection, tax certificate | | | |
| Fee and term | Application fee, how long it is valid | | | |
| Caps or limits | Nights per year, number of permits, density caps, waitlist | | | |
| Primary-residence test | Definition, months per year, proof required | | | |
| Hosted-only or guest limits | Host present rule; maximum guests | | | |
| Renewal | Frequency; what triggers non-renewal | | | |
| Transferability | Does the permit stay with the owner, the host, or the property? Can a new operator use it? | | | |
| Required local contact | Responsible person, response time, posting rules | | | |
| Safety rules | Smoke and CO alarms, extinguisher, exit map, inspection | | | |
| Advertising rules | Must the permit number appear in every listing? Platform verification? | | | |
| Penalties | Fine schedule, platform delisting | | | |

#### Section D. Occupancy-tax registrations and channel remittance

Record who collects and who remits on each channel. A platform may collect some taxes and not others. The operator may still need to register and file returns. Lesson 3.3 (Occupancy Taxes & Platform-Verified Registration) covers this in depth.

| Tax (state / county / city / district) | Registration number and agency | Rate and base (does it include cleaning fees?) | Stays covered (e.g., 30 nights or fewer) | Airbnb collects? | Vrbo collects? | Direct bookings | Filing frequency; must you file in a month with no bookings? | Source URL and date |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| | | | | | | Operator collects and remits | | |

#### Section E. Written questions sent to the city

| # | Question (quote the rule you are asking about) | Sent to (office, email) | Date sent | Reply date | Answer (quote) | Saved as |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | | | | | | |

#### Section F. Unresolved items

| # | Item | Why it is open | Who clears it | Due date | Blocks the decision? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | | | Attorney / CPA / insurer / lender / licensing office / self | | Yes / No |

#### Section G. Decision

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Decision | Permitted / Prohibited / Unresolved |
| Decision rule applied (SR.1.2) | Rule number |
| One-sentence reason with source | |
| Conditions (if permitted) | Night caps, guest caps, renewal date, posting rules |
| Decided by / reviewed by | |
| Decision date | |
| Recheck date | Decision date + 90 days, and 30 days before permit renewal |

### SR.1.4 Test 1: Alder Street as if it were in New York City

Alder Street is a hypothetical two-bedroom, two-bath apartment (Unit 4B) in a four-unit building, leased from a small landlord. It sleeps 5. You do not live there. The plan is to rent the whole unit: 3.0-night average stays, 255 booked nights a year (course example; all figures are assumptions). The course example leaves the city unnamed on purpose. Here we drop the unit into New York City to test the template against the official rules.

#### Section A (NYC test)

| Field | Entry | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Address | 218 Alder Street, Unit 4B (hypothetical; placed in NYC for this test) | N/A (hypothetical address) |
| Property type | Four-unit residential building | Done |
| Own or lease | Lease, from a small landlord | Done |
| Primary residence of operator? | No. Operator lives elsewhere | Done |
| Hosted or unhosted | Unhosted | Done |
| Whole unit or rooms | Whole unit | Done |
| Typical stay | 3.0 nights | Done |
| Maximum guests | 5 | Done |
| Nights per year planned | 255 | Done |
| Channels | Airbnb, Vrbo | Done |

#### Section B and C (NYC test)

The course team checked all rows on 2026-09-25. We confirmed these rules from search results on nyc.gov but did not open every page. Open each page yourself before you rely on it. Reviewer for this test: course team. Real use needs an NYC attorney as second reviewer.

In this demo I summarize each rule to fit the table. On your own sheet, paste the exact sentence from the page, as SR.1.1 says. The last column shows each line's effect on the plan. "Fails the plan" means that line alone makes the plan prohibited. "Applies" means the rule covers this unit but doesn't decide the result by itself. "Not reached" means we skipped the line because an earlier line already decided the result.

| Check | Official source | Result (summarized here; quote it on your sheet) | Effect on the plan |
|---|---|---|---|
| City registration law | [OSE: What is Local Law 18](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/hosting-STRs/what-is-local-law-18.page) | Local Law 18 of 2022 requires anyone renting housing for fewer than 30 consecutive days to register with the Mayor's Office of Special Enforcement (OSE) and get a registration number. | Applies |
| Host present / whole unit | [OSE: Tips for Hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page) | The host must be present during stays under 30 days. Hosts cannot rent an entire apartment or home for fewer than 30 days, and OSE reports this applies regardless of the number of units in the building. | Fails the plan |
| Guest limit | [OSE: Registration Law](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/registration.page) | No more than two paying guests (OSE excludes children under 4 from the count). | Fails the plan: 5 guests |
| Who can register | [OSE: FAQ for prospective hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-prospective-hosts.page) | The applicant must be a natural person and the permanent occupant of the unit. OSE cannot approve a listing that offers an unhosted stay, an entire unit, or more than two guests. | Fails the plan |
| Rent regulation / building lists | [OSE: Registration Law](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/registration.page); [Prohibited Buildings List portal](https://strr-portal.ose.nyc.gov/s/introduction?language=en_US) | Rent-regulated and NYCHA units cannot register. OSE denies registrations in buildings on the Prohibited Buildings List. | Not reached (earlier line already decided) |
| Platform enforcement | [OSE: What is Local Law 18](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/hosting-STRs/what-is-local-law-18.page) | Booking platforms may not process transactions for short-term rentals that are not registered. | Applies (enforcement) |
| Fee and term | [OSE: FAQ for prospective hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-prospective-hosts.page) | Application fee $145, paid at application, non-refundable. Registration term: to verify on the FAQ page. | Not reached (earlier line already decided) |
| Full rules | [OSE Final Rules (PDF)](https://www.nyc.gov/assets/specialenforcement/downloads/pdfs/FINAL-RULES-GOVERNING-REGISTRATION-AND-REQUIREMENTS-FOR-SHORT-TERM-RENTALS.pdf) | Read before any real application. | Reference |
| Lease / landlord | Signed lease | Not reached. The city rule already fails the plan. | Not reached |
| Insurance | Insurer | Not reached. | Not reached |

#### Section D (NYC test)

| Tax | Entry |
|---|---|
| State and city occupancy taxes | Not reached, because the plan is prohibited. For reference, [Airbnb's New York tax article](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2319) lists the New York taxes Airbnb collects, and [Airbnb's New York, NY page](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/868) covers city hosting rules. Confirm rates with the New York State Department of Taxation and Finance and NYC Department of Finance only if a permitted plan emerges. |

#### Section G (NYC test)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Decision | **Prohibited** |
| Rule applied | Rule 1 |
| Reason | OSE bars whole-unit, unhosted stays under 30 days and limits stays to two guests with the host present ([OSE Tips for Hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page)). The Alder Street plan is whole unit, unhosted, 5 guests, 3-night stays. |
| Alternative plan to test separately | 30-night-plus furnished rental. New sheet, new lease terms, new numbers. |
| Recheck date | 2026-12-24 (decision date + 90 days), or sooner if the City Council amends Local Law 18 |

Because the plan came first, the NYC test ends at Section B. You don't spend a week on tax rates for a use the city bars.

### SR.1.5 Test 2: Alder Street as if it were in Los Angeles

Same unit, same plan, now placed inside the City of Los Angeles.

#### Section B and C (LA test)

The course team checked all rows on 2026-09-25 (confirmed from search results on planning.lacity.gov, housing.lacity.gov, and finance.lacity.gov, not by opening every page). Reviewer for this test: course team. Real use needs a Los Angeles attorney. As in the NYC test, the rules are summarized here; quote them on your own sheet.

| Check | Official source | Result (summarized here; quote it on your sheet) | Effect on the plan |
|---|---|---|---|
| City ordinance | [LA City Planning: Home-Sharing](https://planning.lacity.gov/project-review/home-sharing); [LAHD: Home Sharing Ordinance](https://housing.lacity.gov/articles/home-sharing-ordinance) | The Home-Sharing Ordinance (adopted December 2018) restricts short-term rentals to the host's primary residence. Hosts register with City Planning. | Applies |
| Primary-residence test | [LA City Planning: Home-Sharing FAQ (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/HomeSharing/adopted/faq.pdf); [Home-Sharing Administrative Guidelines (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/homesharing/adopted/AdminGuidelines.pdf) | Hosts affirm in a signed declaration that they live in the unit more than six months of the year. A second unit is not a primary residence even if the host owns it. | Fails the plan (operator lives elsewhere) |
| Night cap | [What is the Home-Sharing Program?](https://planning.lacity.gov/blog/what-home-sharing-program) | Standard Home-Sharing allows up to 120 days a year. Extended Home-Sharing allows more, with extra criteria, including at least six months of valid registration. | Would also fail: 255 nights |
| Rent Stabilization | [Home-Sharing FAQ (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/HomeSharing/FAQ.pdf); [LAHD: What is covered under the RSO](https://housing.lacity.gov/residents/what-is-covered-under-the-rso) | Units under the Rent Stabilization Ordinance cannot be used for Home-Sharing. The city's housing department says RSO covers most non-single-family rental units built before October 1, 1978. A four-unit building needs its year built and RSO status checked. | To verify (year built) |
| Renter hosts | [Home-Sharing FAQ (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/HomeSharing/FAQ.pdf) | A renter who hosts needs written, notarized landlord approval submitted with the registration. | Not reached |
| One listing | [Airbnb: Los Angeles, CA](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864) (platform summary of the city rule; confirm in the ordinance) | A host can hold only one registration and operate one listing at a time in the City. | Not reached |
| Non-primary residences | [Vacation Rentals fact sheet (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/ca33f8db-de3d-4629-b927-da7b7fe950fa/Vacation_Rental_Fact_Sheet.pdf); [Draft Vacation Rental Ordinance (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/8bd7ac77-a2da-412d-a758-aa85e71500f5/Draft_Vacation_Rental_Ordinance.pdf) | City Planning has published a draft Vacation Rental Ordinance for non-primary residences. The search did not show it as adopted. | To verify (status) |
| Fee and renewal | [Home-Sharing FAQ (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/HomeSharing/adopted/faq.pdf) | The FAQ states $89 per registration or renewal, renewed annually. The FAQ may be older than the current fee schedule. | To verify (current fee) |
| Safety | [Home-Sharing Guest Code of Conduct (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/beb7a92d-7b2a-4efc-817e-a8f7fc655ce7/HSO_Code_of_Conduct.pdf/1000); [Updated draft ordinance (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/HomeSharing/UpdatedDraftOrd.pdf) | Working fire extinguishers, smoke detectors, carbon monoxide detectors, exit route information, and emergency contact information. Hosts give guests a Code of Conduct. | Not reached |
| Advertising | [LA City Planning: Home-Sharing](https://planning.lacity.gov/project-review/home-sharing); [Airbnb: Los Angeles, CA](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864) | Hosts post the Home-Sharing registration number on every listing. Airbnb requires the number before publishing a listing for stays of 1 to 29 days. | Not reached |
| Required local contact | Home-Sharing guidelines | Emergency contact information required. Response-time rule not confirmed. | To verify |

#### Section D (LA test)

I filled this section only to show how it reads. The plan already fails Section B, so in real use you would stop there.

| Tax | Registration | Rate and base | Airbnb | Vrbo | Direct | Filing | Source, date |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| City Transient Occupancy Tax (TOT) | TOT Registration Certificate from the LA Office of Finance, within 30 days of starting | 14% of rent, for stays of 30 days or less | Airbnb collects and remits city TOT; Airbnb's article states the base includes cleaning fees | To verify in Vrbo's tax pages | Operator collects and remits | Monthly return, due by the 25th of the following month; host files and deducts platform-remitted tax | [LA Office of Finance: TOT requirements](https://finance.lacity.gov/transient-occupancy-tax-requirements); [Home Sharing and the TOT](https://finance.lacity.gov/blog/home-sharing-and-transient-occupancy-tax); [Airbnb: California collection](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2297); 2026-09-25 |
| Other state or county taxes | To verify | To verify | To verify | To verify | To verify | To verify | Airbnb notes hosts must assess other obligations ([Airbnb 2509](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509)) |

#### Section G (LA test)

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Decision | **Prohibited** |
| Rule applied | Rule 1 |
| Reason | The Home-Sharing Ordinance limits short-term rental to the host's primary residence, where the host lives more than six months a year ([Home-Sharing FAQ](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/HomeSharing/adopted/faq.pdf)). The operator does not live at Alder Street. The 255-night plan also exceeds the 120-day standard cap. |
| Open item that could reopen it | Whether any non-primary-residence path (the draft Vacation Rental Ordinance) has been adopted. Ask City Planning in writing (see SR.1.7). |
| Recheck date | 2026-12-24 |

Compare the two tests. Both say prohibited, for different reasons. New York fails the plan on host presence and guest count. Los Angeles fails it on primary residence, and the 255 nights also exceed the 120-day standard cap. If you had only written "LA: legal with a permit," you would have missed both of those conditions.

**Six common mistakes to look for on any sheet**

- Writing a city label, like "LA: legal with a permit", instead of a decision for one address and plan.
- Researching before Section A is written, so the answer is as vague as the plan.
- Treating a platform help page or a phone call as the final word on what the city allows.
- Paraphrasing the rule instead of pasting the sentence that decides it, with the URL.
- Leaving a result undated, or a deciding line with no second reviewer.
- Signing, releasing a deposit, or waiving a contingency while the sheet says unresolved.

### SR.1.6 Cedar Ridge: how an unresolved sheet looks

Cedar Ridge is a hypothetical three-bedroom house in a drive-to lake market, about two hours from a metro area. Maya and Chris would own it, use it about 10 days a year, and rent it the rest of the time. It sleeps 8 and has no HOA (all figures are assumptions from the course example). The course example names no state or county, so every rule line stays blank. This is what a sheet looks like on day one of due diligence, while the purchase offer still has its regulatory contingency open.

#### Section A (Cedar Ridge)

| Field | Entry | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Address, parcel, jurisdiction | To verify | To verify |
| Zoning district | To verify | To verify |
| Property type | Single-family house, 3 bed, 2 bath | Done |
| Own or lease | Own (purchase in progress) | Done |
| Primary residence of operator? | No. Second home, about 10 personal days a year | Done |
| Hosted or unhosted | Unhosted | Done |
| Whole unit | Yes | Done |
| Typical stay | 3.5 nights | Done |
| Maximum guests | 8 | Done |
| Nights per year planned | 212 | Done |
| Channels | Airbnb, Vrbo; direct booking later | Done |

#### Sections B to D (Cedar Ridge)

| Layer | Official source URL | Date checked | Result | Reviewer | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| State | | | | | To verify |
| County (is the parcel unincorporated?) | | | | | To verify |
| City or town | | | | | To verify |
| Zoning: STR permitted, conditional, or prohibited | | | | | To verify |
| Septic or occupancy limits tied to bedrooms | | | | | To verify |
| HOA | N/A: no HOA per listing; confirm with title search | | | | To verify |
| Lender occupancy covenant (the loan's rule on how the owner must use the home) | Loan documents | | | Lender | To verify |
| Insurance | Insurer's written confirmation of STR coverage | | | Insurer | To verify |
| Permit category, cap, waitlist | | | | | To verify |
| Transferability: does the seller's permit transfer on sale? | | | | Licensing office | To verify |
| Local contact rule | | | | | To verify |
| Safety inspection | | | | | To verify |
| Permit number in ads | | | | | To verify |
| State lodging tax registration | | | | | To verify |
| County / city lodging tax | | | | | To verify |
| Airbnb collects which taxes here? | [Airbnb 2509](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509) | | | | To verify |
| Vrbo collects which taxes here? | Vrbo tax help pages | | | | To verify |
| Direct bookings | Operator collects and remits all applicable taxes | | | CPA | To verify |

The 18 blank lines collapse into five open items, grouped by who can answer them. The planning or licensing office covers the zoning and permit lines. The CPA covers the tax lines. The lender and the insurer each cover their own line. In a real sheet, replace "before contingency deadline" with the actual date.

#### Section F and G (Cedar Ridge)

| # | Item | Who clears it | Due date | Blocks the decision? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Zoning use for STR at this parcel | Local licensing or planning office, in writing | Before contingency deadline | Yes |
| 2 | Permit transfer on sale, or new-application wait time | Licensing office, in writing | Before contingency deadline | Yes |
| 3 | Lodging-tax registrations and which platform remits | CPA and state/county tax agency | Before first booking | No (does not change legality) |
| 4 | Lender occupancy covenant fits 10 personal days | Lender, in writing | Before rate lock (when the lender fixes your interest rate) | Yes |
| 5 | Insurance covers STR guests | Insurer, in writing | Before closing | Yes |

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Decision | **Unresolved** |
| Rule applied | Rule 4 |
| Reason | Deciding lines in Sections B and C are blank. |
| Action | Keep the offer's regulatory and inspection contingencies open until items 1, 2, 4 and 5 have written answers. PA.1 (Discovery: Defining Your Buy Box) and Sam's PA.2 cover how to screen the market before you get this far. |
| Recheck date | Each open item's due date; full recheck 90 days after the decision |

An unresolved sheet still tells you which emails to send (four that block the decision, one to the CPA that doesn't) and which contingency dates matter.

### SR.1.7 Sample email to a licensing office

Send this when an official page does not answer a deciding question. Keep each email to one address and one plan. If you only need one answer, ask only that question. Quote the rule you are asking about. Ask for the answer in writing. Offices answer faster when they can reply "yes", "no", or "see section X".

> **Subject:** Short-term rental question for [full address], parcel [number]
>
> Hello,
>
> I am researching whether short-term rental is allowed at [full address], parcel [number], in the [zoning district] district. I have read [ordinance or page name and URL], checked on [date].
>
> My planned use: the [whole unit / private room], [hosted / unhosted], stays of [X] nights on average, up to [X] guests, about [X] nights a year. [The unit is / is not] my primary residence. I [own / lease] the property.
>
> Could you confirm in writing:
>
> 1. Is this use allowed at this parcel under current rules? If so, which permit or registration category applies?
> 2. Is there a cap, waitlist, or pending change that would affect a new application?
> 3. If the property is sold, does an existing permit transfer to the buyer, or must the buyer apply again?
> 4. Which safety inspection and local-contact rules apply?
> 5. Which lodging-tax registrations does your office or another office require for this use?
>
> If another office handles any of these questions, could you tell me which one?
>
> Thank you,
> [Name, phone, email]

When the reply comes in, paste the key sentence into Section E, save the email as a PDF, and update the Status column. A phone call is useful for finding the right office. A phone answer alone does not clear a deciding line under Rule 5; ask the person to confirm by email.

### SR.1.8 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** a local short-term rental attorney, that the decision is right for this address, this plan, and this lease or contract.

**Confirm with:** the local licensing or planning office, in writing, the permit category, caps, transferability, and any pending ordinance.

**Confirm with:** the state, county, and city tax agencies (and your CPA), which lodging-tax registrations you need and what each platform remits for you.

**Confirm with:** your insurer, in writing, that the policy covers short-term guests at this address.

**Confirm with:** your landlord (arbitrage) or HOA and lender (ownership), in writing, that short-term stays are allowed.

### SR.1.9 Where this resource is used

In the Roadmap, fill Section A after 3.1 (How STR Regulation Actually Works), once you know how licensing, zoning, and primary-residence rules work. Run Sections B and C alongside Sam's 3.2 search. Complete Section D with 3.3 (Occupancy Taxes & Platform-Verified Registration). Lesson 3.4 (Red Flags: Markets, Cities & Leases to Avoid in 2026) screens deals as reject, investigate, or advance, and your Section G result is its first gate. No deal yet? Practice on a live listing in a market you're considering. In STR Concierge, run the sheet on each finalist market during Sam's PA.2 and again on the exact parcel before the offer's contingencies expire. SR.5 (Tool comparison guide) picks up once a unit clears this sheet. There you choose the tools that display the permit number and route taxes by channel.

### SR.1 sources

- [NYC OSE: What is Local Law 18](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/hosting-STRs/what-is-local-law-18.page): registration for stays under 30 days; platform transaction ban for unregistered listings. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search (page not opened directly).
- [NYC OSE: Tips for Hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/tips-for-hosts.page): host present, no whole-unit stays under 30 days, two-guest limit. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [NYC OSE: Registration Law](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/registration-law/registration.page): permanent occupant, rent-regulated and NYCHA exclusions, Prohibited Buildings List. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [NYC OSE: FAQ for prospective hosts](https://www.nyc.gov/site/specialenforcement/faq/faq-for-prospective-hosts.page): $145 non-refundable fee; eligibility; registration term. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [NYC OSE Final Rules (PDF)](https://www.nyc.gov/assets/specialenforcement/downloads/pdfs/FINAL-RULES-GOVERNING-REGISTRATION-AND-REQUIREMENTS-FOR-SHORT-TERM-RENTALS.pdf): full rule text. Listed in search results 2026-09-25; not opened.
- [NYC OSE Prohibited Buildings List portal](https://strr-portal.ose.nyc.gov/s/introduction?language=en_US): building list. Listed in search results 2026-09-25.
- [LA City Planning: Home-Sharing](https://planning.lacity.gov/project-review/home-sharing): program page, registration number posting. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.

Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [LA Home-Sharing Administrative Guidelines (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/ordinances/docs/homesharing/adopted/AdminGuidelines.pdf): declaration, multiple-listing disclosure. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [LA Home-Sharing Guest Code of Conduct (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/beb7a92d-7b2a-4efc-817e-a8f7fc655ce7/HSO_Code_of_Conduct.pdf/1000): safety equipment, code of conduct. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [LA Vacation Rentals fact sheet (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/ca33f8db-de3d-4629-b927-da7b7fe950fa/Vacation_Rental_Fact_Sheet.pdf) and [Draft Vacation Rental Ordinance (PDF)](https://planning.lacity.gov/odocument/8bd7ac77-a2da-412d-a758-aa85e71500f5/Draft_Vacation_Rental_Ordinance.pdf): draft non-primary-residence rules. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search; adoption status unresolved.
- [LAHD: Home Sharing Ordinance](https://housing.lacity.gov/articles/home-sharing-ordinance): primary-residence limit. Provided with the course brief; not opened directly.
- [LAHD: What is covered under the RSO](https://housing.lacity.gov/residents/what-is-covered-under-the-rso): RSO coverage. Listed in search results 2026-09-25.
- [LA Office of Finance: TOT requirements](https://finance.lacity.gov/transient-occupancy-tax-requirements) and [Home Sharing and the TOT](https://finance.lacity.gov/blog/home-sharing-and-transient-occupancy-tax): 14% TOT, 30-day definition, registration certificate, monthly filing, deducting platform-remitted tax. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [Airbnb: Los Angeles, CA](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/864): permit number required before publishing; one registration and one listing. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [Airbnb: Occupancy tax collection in California](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2297): Airbnb collects LA city TOT, base includes cleaning fees. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [Airbnb: Occupancy tax collection in New York](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2319) and [New York, NY](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/868): New York tax collection; NYC rules page. Checked 2026-09-25 via official-domain search.
- [Airbnb: Areas where tax collection is available](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/2509): hosts may still owe other taxes. Checked 2026-09-25 via search.
- [Airbnb: Add rental permits to your listing](https://www.airbnb.com/help/article/3953): where the permit number goes. Checked 2026-09-25 via search.

<a id="resource-SR.3-cost-segregation-estimator"></a>
## SR.3  Cost segregation & tax-impact estimator

| Tracker | Detail |
|---|---|
| Part / module | Part Three: Shared resources |
| Code | SR.3 |
| Production type / owner | New / Preston |
| Connects to | Sam's PA.5 Cost Segregation & Year-One Depreciation, Explained (walkthrough); PA.10 (entity and CPA questions); OP.12 (recapture at sale); Cedar Ridge course example (B6) |

### SR.3.0 What this resource does

This is a scenario workbook for one property. It estimates how much depreciation a cost-segregation study might move into year one, and what that could mean for tax. It's built for the conversation with your CPA, not to replace it.

In plain words: when you buy a rental, you can't deduct the building's cost all at once. You deduct it a slice at a time over 27.5 or 39 years. That paper write-off for wear on the building is depreciation. Land is never depreciated. A cost-segregation study splits the building into parts. Some parts, such as land improvements, qualify for 5-, 7-, or 15-year lives. When bonus depreciation (an extra first-year deduction) applies, those short-life parts, plus your furniture, can be deducted in full in year one.

The running example is Cedar Ridge, a hypothetical three-bedroom lake house. Maya and Chris, a two-earner household with about $185,000 of W-2 income, bought it for $385,000 and furnished it for $32,000. In the base case, a study adds about $67,000 of year-one deduction. At a 32% tax rate (assumption), that's worth about $21,000 before a $4,500 study fee (assumption). That holds only if the loss is usable this year, and SR.3.5 shows why that's the biggest open question.

The workbook does four jobs. It collects every input a CPA will ask for, with a source and a status beside each. It separates ordinary depreciation from accelerated depreciation, so you can see what the study actually changes. It shows the illustrative tax effect only after the loss limits you enter, so a big deduction you can't use doesn't look like cash. And it lists every assumption that is still unverified, so nothing gets treated as settled by accident.

Sam's PA.5 lesson walks through a study and a year-one example in more depth. Use this workbook after that lesson, when you have a real property and a real quote from a study provider. No property yet? Build the workbook anyway and enter the Cedar Ridge inputs as practice. If you'd like to see the numbers before the build, read the worked example in SR.3.6 first.

One warning up front. Whether any of this applies depends on the property, the dates, and how you use it. It also depends on your participation, your income, and the tax year. The workbook makes those inputs visible. Your CPA decides them.

### SR.3.1 Workbook layout

Build it as a spreadsheet with five tabs, using the tables in SR.3.2 to SR.3.4. The formulas use these tab names and cell addresses. Shaded input cells are the only cells anyone types into.

| Tab | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Inputs | Every assumption, with what to enter, source, status, and date checked |
| Calc | Formulas only; no typing |
| Outputs | The separated results you bring to the CPA, including the open items pulled from the Inputs status column |
| Scenarios | Low / base / high side by side |
| Sources | Every rule the workbook relies on, with link and date |

Five status values are used everywhere:

- **Verified**: document in hand.
- **CPA to supply**: your CPA's call.
- **Unverified**: an estimate, a placeholder, or a document you don't have yet.
- **Study**: from the cost-segregation report.
- **Example input**: a teaching figure, used only in the Cedar Ridge tables.

A real owner would replace every Example input with a figure from reports and mark it Verified. Two kinds of cells don't take a status: the model switches (B35, B36), and the formula in B21, which reads "Formula (CPA confirms)".

### SR.3.2 Inputs tab

Column A is the field name. Column B is the value. Columns C to F hold what to enter, the source, the status, and the date checked. Rows 1 and 2 hold the tab title and the column headers, so the first field sits in B3. Don't insert rows for the section headings below. Every formula assumes B3 to B36 run without gaps, so mark sections with cell color or a note in column G. The values shown are the Cedar Ridge base case. All of them are assumptions for teaching.

**Section 1. Property and dates**

| Cell | Field | What to enter | Source or formula | Cedar Ridge base | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| B3 | Property address | Full street address | Deed | Hypothetical | Example input |
| B4 | Taxpayer(s) and entity | Who owns title and files the return | Deed; PA.10 decision | Maya and Chris | CPA to supply |
| B5 | Tax year analyzed | The year the deduction would be claimed | CPA | 2026 (assumption) | CPA to supply |
| B6 | Binding written contract date | Date the purchase contract became binding | Signed contract (Verified once in hand) | After Jan. 19, 2025 (assumption) | Unverified |
| B7 | Acquisition (closing) date | Closing date | Settlement statement (Verified once in hand) | After Jan. 19, 2025 (assumption) | Unverified |
| B8 | Placed-in-service date | Date the property was ready and available for rent | Listing live date, first available night; CPA confirms | June 2026 (assumption, the B5 tax year; used in the mid-month test) | CPA to supply |
| B9 | Purchase price | Contract price | Settlement statement | $385,000 | Verified |
| B10 | Capitalized acquisition costs | Closing costs your CPA adds to basis | Settlement statement; CPA | $6,000 | CPA to supply |
| B11 | Land allocation % | Share of basis that is land | Assessor ratio, appraisal, or study; CPA picks method | 20% | Unverified |
| B12 | Land allocation method | Text: assessor, appraisal, study | Document name and date | Assumption | Unverified |
| B13 | Improvements before placed in service | Capital work done before first rental | Invoices | $0 | Verified |
| B14 | Furnishings and equipment | Furniture, appliances, and equipment bought separately | Receipts; OP.6 room inventory | $32,000 | Verified |
| B15 | Study fee | Quoted fee for the cost-segregation study | Provider's written quote | $4,500 (assumption) | Unverified |
| B16 | Study provider and method | Firm, preparer, and method named in the report | Study report | To come | Study |

For Cedar Ridge, I've marked the price, early improvements, and furnishings Verified, as if the settlement statement and receipts are in hand. The example gives no exact dates, so B6 and B7 stay Unverified. The contract date matters because a contract signed on or before January 19, 2025 can change the bonus rate (Rule 1).

**Section 2. Study results**

| Cell | Field | What to enter | Source or formula | Cedar Ridge base | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| B17 | Building % reclassified to 5-year property | From the study's asset summary | Study | 15% (assumption) | Study |
| B18 | Building % reclassified to 7-year property | From the study | Study | 0% (assumption) | Study |
| B19 | Building % reclassified to 15-year property | From the study (often land improvements) | Study | 7% (assumption) | Study |
| B20 | Recovery period, remaining building | 27.5 or 39 years; CPA decides | CPA | 39 | CPA to supply |

B17 + B18 + B19 = 22%, the Cedar Ridge base case's reclass share. Replace the percentages with the study's dollar figures divided by the building basis once you have the report. A recovery period is the number of years the tax rules spread a cost over. The study's parts get 5, 7, or 15 years. The rest of the building stays on 27.5 or 39.

**Section 3. Bonus depreciation**

| Cell | Field | What to enter | Source or formula | Cedar Ridge base | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| B21 | Bonus % by law (screen) | Formula; see SR.3.5 rule 1 | `=IF(AND(B6>DATE(2025,1,19),B7>DATE(2025,1,19),B8>DATE(2025,1,19)),1,"CPA")` | 100% | Formula (CPA confirms) |
| B22 | Bonus % override or election | Leave blank unless the CPA enters a different rate or an election | CPA | Blank | CPA to supply |

B21 is a formula, so leave it unshaded and don't type in it. If your CPA gives a different rate or an election, it goes in B22.

**Section 4. Use, participation, and loss limits**

| Cell | Field | What to enter | Source or formula | Cedar Ridge base | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| B23 | Days rented at a fair rental price | Nights booked at market rates | Platform and PMS (property management software) reports | 212 | Example input |
| B24 | Personal-use days | Days used by you, family, or others at below-market rent | Your calendar; CPA applies the definition | 10 | CPA to supply |
| B25 | Number of stays | Count of separate rentals in the year | Platform reports | 61 | Example input |
| B26 | Days in all rental periods | Total days across all stays | Platform reports | 212 | Example input |
| B27 | Owner hours on the activity | Total from your hour log | Contemporaneous log (Verified once reviewed) | Enter | Unverified |
| B28 | Hours by others (cleaner, co-host, manager) | Estimate from invoices and schedules | Vendor records | Enter | Unverified |
| B29 | Material participation test relied on | Text; which Pub 925 test | CPA | CPA | CPA to supply |
| B30 | Real estate professional status | Yes / No | CPA | No (assumption) | CPA to supply |
| B31 | Modified adjusted gross income (MAGI) | Household figure | CPA | About $185,000 (assumption, based on W-2 income) | CPA to supply |
| B32 | Share of year-1 loss usable this year | 0% to 100% after the passive rules and the other loss limits your CPA checks (at-risk, basis, excess business loss) | CPA | 100% | CPA to supply |
| B33 | Marginal federal rate | Rate the CPA says the deduction offsets | CPA | 32% (assumption) | CPA to supply |
| B34 | State rate | Placeholder until the CPA answers | CPA | 0% (placeholder) | CPA to supply |

B23, B25, and B26 hold hypothetical teaching figures, so their status reads Example input. For a real property, pull them from your platform and PMS reports and mark them Verified.

The base case sets B32 to 100%. That assumes your CPA rules the whole year-one loss usable this year. Rule 5 shows one way that could happen. The average stay is 7 days or less, and Maya and Chris would need an hour log that meets a material participation test. Their hours (B27) aren't entered yet, so this is the single biggest open assumption in the example. Lesson 0.3's time estimate (assumption) even has their cleaner logging more hours than they do. That's the case Rule 5 warns about. If the loss turns out to be passive, use the passive row in SR.3.6: $0 of tax effect this year.

**Section 5. Model switches**

| Cell | Field | What to enter | Cedar Ridge base |
|---|---|---|---|
| B35 | First-year convention switch | 1 = full year (the Cedar Ridge base case, for simplicity); 0 = mid-month from B8 | 1 |
| B36 | Apply rental-use fraction | 1 = divide expenses by rental days; 0 = no division | 0 (base case) |

The base case takes a full first year of building depreciation to keep the math simple. With a June start, the real first year is shorter. The mid-month row in SR.3.6 shows the difference: $3,389 for the building instead of $6,256.

### SR.3.3 Calc tab: formulas

Every row below is one cell in column B of the Calc tab. The right column shows the Cedar Ridge base result. Calc and Inputs both use column B, so the same address means different things on each tab. Inside the formulas, a plain address like B9 is a Calc cell, and Inputs!B9 is an Inputs cell. In the text, I always say which tab.

| Cell | Line | Formula | Cedar Ridge base |
|---|---|---|---:|
| B3 | Depreciable basis, land and building | `=Inputs!B9+Inputs!B10+Inputs!B13` | $391,000 |
| B4 | Land (never depreciated) | `=B3*Inputs!B11` | $78,200 |
| B5 | Building | `=B3*(1-Inputs!B11)` | $312,800 |
| B6 | 5-year building components | `=B5*Inputs!B17` | $46,920 |
| B7 | 7-year building components | `=B5*Inputs!B18` | $0 |
| B8 | 15-year components | `=B5*Inputs!B19` | $21,896 |
| B9 | Total reclassified | `=SUM(B6:B8)` | $68,816 |
| B10 | Remaining building (27.5 or 39 years) | `=B5-B9` | $243,984 |
| B11 | Bonus-eligible basis | `=B9+Inputs!B14` | $100,816 |
| B12 | Bonus % used | `=IF(Inputs!B22<>"",Inputs!B22,IF(ISNUMBER(Inputs!B21),Inputs!B21,0))` | 100% |
| B13 | Accelerated depreciation (bonus) | `=B11*B12` | $100,816 |
| B14 | Short-life basis left for regular MACRS | `=B11-B13` | $0 |
| B15 | Regular MACRS year 1 on B14 | CPA enters from the depreciation schedule | $0 |
| B16 | Straight-line per full year, remaining building | `=B10/Inputs!B20` | $6,256 |
| B17 | First-year fraction | `=IF(Inputs!B35=1,1,(12-MONTH(Inputs!B8)+0.5)/12)` | 1.00 |
| B18 | Ordinary building depreciation, year 1 | `=B16*B17` | $6,256 |
| B19 | Year-1 depreciation before use division | `=B13+B15+B18` | $107,072 |
| B20 | Rental-use fraction | `=IF(Inputs!B36=1,Inputs!B23/(Inputs!B23+Inputs!B24),1)` | 1.000 |
| B21 | Estimated year-1 deduction | `=B19*B20` | $107,072 |
| B22 | Average guest stay (days) | `=Inputs!B26/Inputs!B25` | 3.48 |
| B23 | Seven-day flag | `=IF(B22<=7,"Avg stay 7 days or less: CPA applies Pub 925 rental-activity exception and participation tests","Avg stay over 7 days: CPA checks other exceptions")` | Flag shows |
| B24 | Home-use flag | `=IF(Inputs!B24>MAX(14,0.1*Inputs!B23),"Used as a home: Pub 527 limits may apply","Not used as a home under the day test")` | Not used as a home |
| B25 | Deduction usable this year | `=B21*Inputs!B32` | $107,072 |
| B26 | Deduction carried forward or suspended | `=B21-B25` | $0 |
| B27 | Illustrative tax effect | `=B25*(Inputs!B33+Inputs!B34)` | $34,263 |
| B28 | Illustrative tax effect minus study fee | `=B27-Inputs!B15` | $29,763 |
| B29 | Year 1 with no study | `=Inputs!B14*B12+(B5/Inputs!B20)*B17` | $40,021 |
| B30 | Added year-1 deduction from the study | `=B19-B29` | $67,051 |
| B31 | Added illustrative tax effect from the study | `=B30*B20*Inputs!B32*(Inputs!B33+Inputs!B34)` | $21,456 |
| B32 | Adjusted basis after year 1, all assets | `=B3+Inputs!B14-B21` | $315,928 |
| B33 | Accelerated depreciation pool (bonus + MACRS on short-life assets; CPA classifies 1245 vs 1250) | `=(B13+B15)*B20` | $100,816 |
| B34 | Section 1250 straight-line pool, year 1 | `=B18*B20` | $6,256 |

Notes on the formulas:

- Basis is your cost for tax purposes. Calc B3 adds the price, the capitalized costs, and any early improvements.
- Calc B4 and B5 are the heart of the land rule. Land sits outside every depreciation line. No formula below Calc B5 touches B4.
- Calc B12 falls back to 0 if the screen in Inputs B21 returns "CPA". That's on purpose. If the date screen fails, the workbook shows no bonus until the CPA enters a rate in Inputs B22.
- Calc B14 and B15 exist for any year the bonus rate is below 100%. The remaining short-life basis then follows regular MACRS, the IRS's standard depreciation system described in Publication 946. The CPA's software computes it. Don't estimate it by hand.
- Calc B16 is straight-line: the same amount each full year.
- Calc B17 is a simplified mid-month fraction for the first year. It follows the rule that real property uses a mid-month convention ([IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946)). The base case sets Inputs B35 to 1, a full year, for simplicity. The CPA's schedule is the final figure.
- Calc B20 is the rental-use fraction (not Inputs B20, the building life). It applies only if the CPA says expenses must be divided between rental and personal use. The Cedar Ridge base case does not divide, so it uses 1.

### SR.3.4 Outputs tab

Keep these lines separate. Don't add the tax effect to operating cash flow in another model. It's a one-time estimate that depends on your CPA's rulings. It isn't income the property earns, and a lender underwrites cash flow, not deductions.

| Output | Cell reference | Cedar Ridge base | What it means |
|---|---|---:|---|
| Ordinary depreciation, year 1 | `=Calc!B18*Calc!B20` | $6,256 | Straight-line on the remaining building |
| Accelerated depreciation, year 1 | `=(Calc!B13+Calc!B15)*Calc!B20` | $100,816 | Bonus on reclassified parts and furnishings |
| Estimated year-1 deduction | `=Calc!B21` | $107,072 | Before loss limits |
| Usable this year | `=Calc!B25` | $107,072 | After the CPA's usable share |
| Carried forward or suspended | `=Calc!B26` | $0 | Not lost, but not cash this year |
| **Illustrative** tax effect | `=Calc!B27` | $34,263 | Only if every input holds |
| Added deduction from the study | `=Calc!B30` | $67,051 | Compared with no study |
| Adjusted basis after year 1 | `=Calc!B32` | $315,928 | Starting point for any future gain |
| Accelerated depreciation pool | `=Calc!B33` | $100,816 | Mostly section 1245 items; ordinary-income recapture exposure at sale, up to the gain on those assets (OP.12). The CPA classifies 15-year land improvements |
| Section 1250 straight-line pool | `=Calc!B34` | $6,256 | Unrecaptured section 1250 gain exposure at sale, taxed at a maximum 25% (OP.12) |
| Flags | `=Calc!B23` and `=Calc!B24` | Shown | Items the CPA must rule on |
| Unverified assumptions | Filter Inputs where status is not "Verified" | See SR.3.10 | The open list |

Recapture, in plain words: when you sell, part of the depreciation you took can be taxed. That's why the Outputs tab keeps the two pools. OP.12 works through it.

Put this line at the top of the Outputs tab in bold: "Illustrative only. Every figure depends on inputs your CPA has not yet confirmed. Not tax advice."

Six mistakes turn an honest estimate into a number that looks like cash:

1. Adding the tax effect to operating cash flow or to a lender's math.
2. Treating the estimated deduction as cash before the CPA sets the usable share.
3. Typing into the Calc tab. Only the shaded input cells take typing.
4. Guessing a CPA cell instead of leaving it blank and marking its status.
5. Applying bonus to land or to the whole building. Land never depreciates.
6. Counting furniture twice: once in Inputs B14 and again inside the study.

### SR.3.5 Decision rules built into the workbook

The workbook builds in eight rules. Rules 1, 2, 4, 5, and 6 work like yes-or-no gates. Rules 3, 7, and 8 cover the building life, late-study filings, and basis.

**Rule 1. Bonus rate depends on dates and eligibility.** The IRS says the One, Big, Beautiful Bill, a tax law, made the 100% additional first-year depreciation deduction permanent. It covers eligible property acquired after January 19, 2025 ([IRS news release](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill); [Notice 2026-11](https://www.irs.gov/pub/irs-drop/n-26-11.pdf)). For this rule, property is treated as acquired no later than the date a binding written contract for it was signed. So a contract signed on or before January 19, 2025 can rule out 100% bonus, even if you closed later. That's why Inputs B6 asks for the contract date as well as the closing date. Publication 946 describes 100% for qualified property acquired and placed in service after January 19, 2025. It also describes 40% for certain property placed in service after December 31, 2024 and before January 20, 2025 ([IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946)). An election to use 40% instead also exists for some property. The screen in Inputs B21 only checks dates. The CPA confirms eligibility and enters any election in Inputs B22.

**Rule 2. Bonus applies to short-life property, never to land or the whole building.** Qualified property must be tangible property depreciated under MACRS, the IRS's standard depreciation system. Its recovery period must be 20 years or less ([IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946)). The 27.5-year and 39-year building lives are longer, so Calc B13 applies bonus only to Calc B11 (reclassified parts plus furnishings). Land is not depreciable at all ([IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946)).

**Rule 3. The building life is a CPA decision.** Publication 946 defines residential rental property as a building earning 80% or more of gross rental income from dwelling units. It excludes hotel, motel, or inn units where more than half the units are used on a transient basis ([IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946)). How that applies to a single house rented for short stays is a question for your CPA. The Cedar Ridge base case uses 39 years in Inputs B20, the cautious choice if short stays count as transient use. A longer life means a smaller yearly deduction. With 27.5 years, Cedar Ridge's remaining-building line would be $8,872 per full year instead of $6,256.

**Rule 4. The personal-use day test.** Publication 527 treats a dwelling unit as used as a home in one case. That's when personal use exceeds the greater of 14 days or 10% of the days rented at a fair rental price ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). Calc B24 runs that test. Publication 527 also says that with any personal use, expenses are divided between rental and personal use. Set Inputs B36 to 1 if your CPA applies that division to depreciation.

**Rule 5. Average stay and material participation.** Publication 925 says an activity with an average customer stay of 7 days or less is not a rental activity. You figure the average by dividing total days in all rental periods by the number of rentals ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Calc B22 does that math. "Not a rental activity" isn't bad news. It means the usual rental-loss rules don't settle the question on their own. Passing that test does not make the loss usable by itself. The CPA then applies the material participation tests. Two examples are more than 500 hours, or more than 100 hours and at least as much as anyone else ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). That's why Inputs B27 and B28 ask for your hours and everyone else's. A cleaner or co-host who logs more hours than you can make you fail that 100-hour test.

**Rule 6. Loss limits come before the tax effect.** If the activity is passive, the loss may be suspended and carried forward instead of reducing this year's tax. Then Inputs B32 would be 0%. Passive is Publication 925's term. Losses from passive activities generally offset only income from other passive activities, not your paycheck (lesson 0.2 covers this). For Cedar Ridge, Rule 5's tests decide whether it's passive. Publication 925 describes a special allowance of up to $25,000 for active participation in rental real estate. It phases out between $100,000 and $150,000 of MAGI ([IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925)). Ask the CPA whether that allowance can apply to your activity at all, given Rule 5. At Maya and Chris's assumed MAGI of about $185,000, the allowance would be fully phased out, so it doesn't help them either way. A separate excess business loss limit can also apply. For 2025 the IRS threshold was $313,000, or $626,000 for joint filers. Disallowed amounts carry forward as a net operating loss (NOL) ([Form 461 instructions](https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i461)). Cedar Ridge's $107,072 sits far below that 2025 figure. The CPA supplies the current-year figure.

That's why the base case's 100% usable share in Inputs B32 is an assumption, not a finding. It rests on Rule 5's tests going Maya and Chris's way, and their hours aren't logged yet.

**Rule 7. Late studies need a method change.** Suppose the property was placed in service in an earlier year. Reclassifying it can then require Form 3115, a change in accounting method, with a section 481(a) catch-up adjustment ([About Form 3115](https://www.irs.gov/forms-pubs/about-form-3115); [IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946)). Add a note in Inputs B16 if this applies. The CPA handles the filing.

**Rule 8. Depreciation reduces basis whether or not you claim it.** Publication 527 says basis goes down by the depreciation you could have deducted, even if you didn't claim it ([IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527)). The Outputs tab carries the accelerated and straight-line pools forward so the recapture conversation in OP.12 starts with real numbers.

### SR.3.6 Worked example: Cedar Ridge low, base, and high

This is what the Scenarios tab shows. The base column is the Cedar Ridge base case. The low and high columns change only two inputs: the land share and the reclassified share of the building. Everything else stays at the base values. All figures are assumptions.

| Input or output | Low | Base | High |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Land allocation % | 30% | 20% | 15% |
| Reclassified share of building | 15% | 22% | 30% |
| Depreciable basis, land and building | $391,000 | $391,000 | $391,000 |
| Land (not depreciable) | $117,300 | $78,200 | $58,650 |
| Building | $273,700 | $312,800 | $332,350 |
| Reclassified 5/7/15-year parts | $41,055 | $68,816 | $99,705 |
| Remaining building | $232,645 | $243,984 | $232,645 |
| Furnishings | $32,000 | $32,000 | $32,000 |
| **Accelerated depreciation (100% bonus, if eligible)** | **$73,055** | **$100,816** | **$131,705** |
| **Ordinary depreciation, full year at 39 years** | **$5,965** | **$6,256** | **$5,965** |
| **Estimated year-1 deduction** | **$79,020** | **$107,072** | **$137,670** |
| Illustrative tax effect at 32%, if fully usable | $25,286 | $34,263 | $44,054 |
| Tax effect minus $4,500 study fee | $20,786 | $29,763 | $39,554 |
| Adjusted basis after year 1 (with furnishings) | $343,980 | $315,928 | $285,330 |

Low and high happen to land on the same remaining-building figure. That's a coincidence of these two inputs, not a rule.

Here is the base column worked by hand, so you can follow each formula:

1. Basis: $385,000 + $6,000 = $391,000.
2. Land: $391,000 × 20% = $78,200. Building: $391,000 × (1 − 20%) = $312,800.
3. The study moves 22% of the building to short lives: $312,800 × 22% = $68,816. Remaining building: $312,800 − $68,816 = $243,984.
4. Accelerated: ($68,816 + $32,000) × 100% = $100,816.
5. Ordinary, a full first year for simplicity: $243,984 ÷ 39 = $6,256.
6. Year-1 deduction: $100,816 + $6,256 = $107,072.
7. Illustrative tax effect: $107,072 × 32% = $34,263, only if all of it is usable this year.

Now the adjustments your CPA is likely to raise. Each row changes one thing from the base.

| Base with one change | Year-1 deduction | Illustrative tax effect at 32% |
|---|---:|---:|
| Base as above | $107,072 | $34,263 |
| Rental-use fraction applied: 212 ÷ (212 + 10) = 95.5% | $102,249 | $32,720 |
| Mid-month convention, placed in service in June | $104,205 | $33,346 |
| Passive activity, no loss usable this year | $107,072 carried forward | $0 this year |
| No study at all (furnishings bonus + building at 39 years) | $40,021 | $12,807 |

The mid-month row uses $243,984 ÷ 39 × 6.5 ÷ 12 = $3,389 for the building instead of $6,256. The base case uses a full first year for simplicity. Because the house went into service in June, the mid-month row is likely closer to what your CPA's schedule will show.

Read the table this way. The study's real contribution, compared with no study, is $67,051 more deduction in year one ($107,072 − $40,021). At 32% that's $21,456, or $16,956 after a $4,500 fee, if fully usable. The furnishings bonus appears in both the study and no-study rows, because furniture is short-life property whether or not you order a study.

The study mostly changes timing. It doesn't change your basis: $391,000 plus $32,000 of furnishings either way. It moves deductions earlier, and it lowers your adjusted basis faster. That shows up again at sale (OP.12).

The passive row is the one to fear. In that case, the year-one tax effect is zero and the deduction waits. Remember that the base column assumes the whole loss is usable this year (Inputs B32 = 100%). With the $25,000 allowance phased out at their income, that depends on the participation tests in Rule 5.

Two more checks from the day-count inputs:

- Home-use test: the greater of 14 days or 10% × 212 = 21.2 days. Ten personal days is below that, so Cedar Ridge is not "used as a home" under the Publication 527 test, as entered.
- Average stay: 212 ÷ 61 = 3.48 days, which is 7 days or less. The CPA then decides passive status using the participation tests and Maya and Chris's hour logs.

### SR.3.7 Evidence to request from a study provider

The IRS publishes a [Cost Segregation Audit Techniques Guide, Publication 5653](https://www.irs.gov/pub/irs-pdf/p5653.pdf), for its examiners. The current version is dated February 2025. It says there are no standards for preparing these studies, and that studies vary widely in method, documentation, depth, and preparer expertise. It contrasts a detailed engineering approach built on actual cost records with survey, letter, and "rule of thumb" approaches. Its principal elements of a quality study include a preparer with expertise and experience. They also include a reconciliation of total allocated costs to total actual costs. Its information document requests ask who took part and in what role, and where the blueprints came from.

Use that guide to shape what you ask for before you pay. The first five rows come straight from the points above. The rest are my recommendations for making the study usable in this workbook. Get all twelve in writing before you engage a provider.

| Request | Why it matters | Received? |
|---|---|---|
| Preparer's name, credentials, and relevant experience | Pub 5653 lists expertise and experience as a principal element | |
| Every participant and their role (engineer, appraiser, analyst) | Pub 5653 information document requests ask for this | |
| Name and description of the method used | Pub 5653 contrasts detailed engineering, survey, and rule-of-thumb approaches | |
| Reconciliation of allocated costs to total purchase price and basis | Pub 5653 lists reconciliation as a principal element | |
| Source of plans or blueprints, or site-visit notes and photos | Pub 5653 asks where blueprints came from; for an older house the provider may measure on site | |
| Asset-by-asset schedule: description, cost, class, recovery period | Fills Inputs B17 to B19 and the CPA's depreciation schedule | |
| Legal reasoning for each classification | Lets the CPA check why a component is personal property or a land improvement | |
| Land allocation method and support | Ties to Inputs B11; land can't be depreciated | |
| How furnishings already on your books were treated | Avoids counting furniture twice | |
| Placed-in-service date the study assumed | Must match Inputs B8 | |
| Discussion of recapture at sale | Links to OP.12 | |
| Fee, delivery date, and what support they give if the IRS asks questions | Fills Inputs B15; sets expectations | |

### SR.3.8 How to use this with a CPA

1. Before the meeting, fill every input you can document. Leave the CPA cells blank rather than guessing. Mark each status honestly.
2. Send the CPA the workbook, the settlement statement, the furnishing receipts, the study quote, and your calendar of rental and personal days. If you already have the study, send it with the evidence from SR.3.7.
3. Ask the CPA to fill or confirm the dates and basis first. Those are the placed-in-service date (B8), capitalized costs (B10), land method (B11), and building life (B20), all on the Inputs tab. Then the method choices: bonus rate or election (B21, B22), rental-use division (B36), and first-year convention (B35).
4. Ask the CPA to decide participation and limits: which Publication 925 test applies (B29), professional status (B30), MAGI (B31), and the usable share (B32). Bring your hour log.
5. Ask for the marginal federal and state rates the deduction would offset (B33, B34).
6. Rerun the Scenarios tab with the CPA's inputs. Save a dated copy. Record the CPA's name and the date on the Sources tab. An email counts.
7. Decide on the study only after seeing the "added deduction from the study" line, the passive case, and the fee side by side. Keep the dated workbook with your tax records for the OP.12 exit conversation.

A short script for the meeting: "Here is my estimate and every assumption behind it. Which inputs are wrong, which apply to me, and what would you enter instead?"

### SR.3.9 Sources tab

The workbook's Sources tab has one row per rule. Fill it like this, and add a row each time the CPA confirms something. The rows below show how the course team filled it. "Search summary" means the team checked IRS search summaries of each page, not a full read. For your return, your CPA's confirmation is what counts.

| Claim | Source | How checked | Date checked | Checked by |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 100% bonus for eligible property acquired after Jan. 19, 2025; binding contract rule; 40% election | [IRS news release](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill); [Notice 2026-11](https://www.irs.gov/pub/irs-drop/n-26-11.pdf) | Search summary of irs.gov | 2026-09-25 | Content team |
| Bonus needs MACRS recovery period of 20 years or less; land not depreciable; mid-month for real property; transient-use exclusion | [IRS Publication 946](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p946) | Search summary of irs.gov | 2026-09-25 | Content team |
| Personal-use day test; expense division; basis reduced by allowable depreciation | [IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527) | Search summary of irs.gov | 2026-09-25 | Content team |
| 7-day average-stay rule; material participation tests; $25,000 allowance and phase-out | [IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925) | Search summary of irs.gov | 2026-09-25 | Content team |
| Excess business loss threshold (2025) | [Form 461 instructions](https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i461) | Search summary of irs.gov | 2026-09-25 | Content team |

| Section 1245 recapture; unrecaptured 1250 gain rate | [IRS Publication 544](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p544); [Topic 409](https://www.irs.gov/taxtopics/tc409) | Search summary of irs.gov | 2026-09-25 | Content team |
| Your CPA's inputs | CPA engagement letter or email | Direct | | |

### SR.3.10 Open items: unverified assumptions (Cedar Ridge base)

The Outputs tab lists every input whose status isn't "Verified". For the Cedar Ridge teaching example, that list is below. Every cell here is on the Inputs tab.

- Property, owners and entity, and tax year (B3 to B5).
- Contract, closing, and placed-in-service dates (B6 to B8), which drive the bonus rate.
- Capitalized acquisition costs of $6,000 (B10).
- Land share of 20% and its method (B11, B12).
- Study fee of $4,500, the provider, and the reclass split of 15% / 0% / 7% (B15 to B19).
- Building life of 39 years vs 27.5 years (B20).
- Bonus eligibility and any election (B21, B22).
- Rental days, number of stays, and days in rental periods (B23, B25, B26), which are example inputs until real platform reports replace them.
- Whether personal days are counted correctly, and whether expenses are divided (B24, B36).
- Hours logged by the owners and by others; the participation test and professional status (B27 to B30).
- MAGI, usable share, and tax rates (B31 to B34).
- First-year convention (B35).

### SR.3.11 Who confirms what

**Confirm with:** your CPA every input marked CPA to supply in SR.3.2. That includes passive status and the usable share in Inputs B32, the two that move the answer most. Also ask whether Form 3115 is needed, and how the depreciation pools are treated at a sale or exchange (OP.12).

**Confirm with:** the study provider, in writing and before you engage them, everything on the SR.3.7 evidence list.

### SR.3.12 Where this fits

Open this workbook after Sam's PA.5 lesson and bring it to the CPA meeting that PA.10 sets up. No property yet? Build the five tabs, enter the Cedar Ridge base inputs, and check that you get $107,072 and $34,263. That also tests your formulas. The course then moves to PA.6 DSCR Loans 101, where the same property's cash flow meets the lender's math. Keep the tax effect out of that lender math; a lender underwrites cash flow, not deductions. Come back to the workbook each year, and again at OP.12 when you weigh a sale or exchange.

### SR.3 sources

- [IRS: Treasury, IRS issue guidance on the additional first year depreciation deduction amended as part of the One, Big, Beautiful Bill](https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/treasury-irs-issue-guidance-on-the-additional-first-year-depreciation-deduction-amended-as-part-of-the-one-big-beautiful-bill): permanent 100% bonus for eligible property acquired after Jan. 19, 2025; 40% election. Checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS Publication 527](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p527): personal-use day test; expense division; basis reduced by allowable depreciation. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.
- [IRS Publication 925](https://www.irs.gov/publications/p925): 7-day rule; material participation tests; $25,000 allowance and MAGI phase-out. Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary.

Brief-provided; checked 2026-09-25 via WebSearch summary. Full text not opened.
